Foreword

1

Homological algebra first arose as a language for describing topological prop-
erties of geometrical objects. The emergence of a new language is always an
important event in the development of mathematics: Euclidean plane and
spatial geometry, Cartesian analytic geometry, the formalization of Newton's
fluents and fluxions by Leibniz and later by Lagrange start the series to
which homological algebra can be added. As with every successful language,
homological algebra quickly realized its tendencies for self-development. As
with every successful mathematical language, it rapidly began to expand its
semantics, that is, to describe things that it was not originally designed to
describe. The computation of the index of an elliptic operator, exact esti-
mates for the number of solutions of congruences modulo a prime, the theory
of hyperfunctions, anomalies in quantum field theory - these are only some
of the contemporary applications of homological ideas.
    The history of homological algebra can be divided into three periods.
The first one starts in the 1940's with the classical works of Eilenberg and
MacLane, D.K. Faddeev, and R. Baer and ends with the appearance in 1956 of
the fundamental monograph "Homological Algebra" by Cartan and Eilenberg
which has lost none of its significance up to the present day.
    A. Grothendieck's long paper "Sur quelques points d'algebre homologique"
published in 1957 (its appearance had been delayed three years) marks the
starting point of the second period, which was dominated by the influence of
Grothendieck and his school of algebraic geometry.
VI      Foreword

    The third period, which extends up to the present time, is marked by
the ever-increasing use of derived categories and triangulated categories. The
basic technique was developed in the thesis of Grothendieck's student J.-L.
Verdier in 1963, but was slow in spreading beyond the confines of algebraic
geometry. Only in the last fifteen years has the situation changed. First in the
work of M. Sato and his school on microlocal analysis, then in the theory of
D-modules and perverse sheaves with applications to representation theory,
derived categories started to be used as the most suitable instrument.
    We now try to characterize these three periods, although we should apol-
ogize to the reader for our subjective evaluation and judgment and for the
incompleteness of the material: of course, many important developments do
not fit into our rigid scheme.
    The book by Cartan and Eilenberg contains essentially all the construc-
tions of homological algebra that constitute its computational tools, namely
standard resolutions and spectral sequences. No less important, it contains an
axiomatic definition of derived functors of additive functors on the category
of modules over a ring.
    It was this idea that determined the contours of the second period. The
logic of the internal development of analytic and algebraic geometry led to the
formulation of the notion of a sheaf and to the realization of the idea that the
natural argument of a homology theory is a pair consisting of a space with a
sheaf on it, rather than just a space (or a space and a coefficient group). Here
the fundamental contribution of H. Cartan's seminar and J.-P. Serre's pa-
per "Faisceaux algebriques coherents" should be mentioned. Grothendieck's
paper of 1957 quoted above stresses the analogy between pairs (space, sheaf
of abelian groups on it) and pairs (ring, module over it) from the homologi-
cal point of view and emphasizes the idea that sheaf cohomology should be
defined as the derived functor of global sections.
    The break with the axiomatic homology and cohomology theory of Eilen-
berg and Steenrod is in that now an abelian object (a sheaf), rather than
a non-abelian one (a space), serves as a variable argument in a cohomology
theory. More precisely, a homology or a cohomology theory with fixed co-
efficients according to Eilenberg and Steenrod is a graded functor from the
category of topological spaces into abelian groups that satisfies certain axioms
by which it is uniquely determined. The most important of these axioms are
the specification of the homology (or cohomology) of the point, and the exact
sequence associated with the "excision axiom". The cohomology theory of a
fixed topological space according to Grothendieck is a graded functor from
the category of sheaves of abelian groups on this space into abelian groups,
also satisfying a number of axioms by which it is uniquely determined. The
most important of these are the specification of zero-dimensional cohomol-
ogy as global sections and the exact sequence associated with a short exact
sequence of sheaves.
                                                               Foreword     VII

    The development of this idea led to a very far-reaching generalization
of basic notions of algebraic geometry - Grothendieck topologies and topoi.
The essence of this generalization is that since the cohomological properties
of a space are completely determined by the category of sheaves over it, it
is these categories that should be the primary objects of study in topology,
rather than topological spaces themselves. After a suitable axiomatization of
the properties of such categories we arrive at the notion of a topos. The devel-
opment of these abstract ideas was motivated by a very concrete problem -
the famous conjectures of A. Weil on the number of solutions of congruences
modulo a prime. The very statements of these conjectures include the assump-
tion about the existence of a certain cohomology theory of algebraic varieties
in characteristic p > 0, which would allow us to apply to this situation the
Lefschetz fixed point formula; a cohomology theory of this type was provided
by the cohomology of the etale topos constructed by A. Grothendieck and
developed by his students.
    The main product of the homological algebra of this period was the com-
putation and properties of various derived functors RP F, where F is the
functor of global sections, of direct image, of tensor product and so on. These
derived functors arise as the cohomology of complexes of the form F(T),
where T are resolutions consisting of injective, projective, fiat, or some other
objects suitably adapted to F. The choice of a resolution is highly non-unique,
but RP F does not depend on this choice.
    In the course of time it came to be understood that one should study all
complexes, rather that just resolutions T (and complexes obtained by apply-
ing functors to these resolutions), but modulo a quite complicated equivalence
relation, which identifies certain complexes having the same cohomology.
    The final version of this equivalence relation seems still not to be com-
pletely understood. However, a working definition which has proved its worth
was formulated in Verdier's thesis of 1963. The categories of complexes ob-
tained in this way are called derived categories, and axiomatization of their
properties leads to the notion of triangulated categories.
    It seems to us that the main feature of the third period of homological al-
gebra is the development of a special kind of "thinking in terms of complexes"
as opposed to the "thinking in terms of objects and their cohomological in-
variants" that was typical for the first two periods. Perhaps this appears
most vividly in the theory of perverse sheaves; it was shown that the coho-
mological properties of topological manifolds extend to a substantial degree
to spaces with singularities, if we take as coefficients not sheaves but special
complexes of sheaves (as objects of the corresponding derived category). The
conormal complexes of Grothendieck and Illusie and the dualizing complexes
of Grothendieck and Verdier can be considered as earlier constructions of the
same kind.
VIn     Foreword

2
This book is intended as an introductory textbook on the technique of derived
categories. Up to now, as far as we know, a mathematician willing to learn
this subject has had to turn either to the two original sources, the abstract of
Verdier's thesis and the notes of Hartshorne's seminar, or to the oral tradition,
in those mathematical centers where it still has been maintained.
     Thus the central part of the book is Chaps. III-IV, and the reader with
even a slight acquaintance with abelian categories and functors can start
directly from Chap. III.
     Chapter II is directed to the reader who has hardly had anything to
do with categories, and we have tried to make clear the intuitive meaning
of standard categorical constructions, and to give examples of "thinking in
categories". The main practical aim of this chapter is an introduction to
abelian categories.
     Finally, Chaps. I and V resulted from our attempt (which had cost us a
lot of trouble) to separate off homological algebra from algebraic topology,
without burning the bridge between them. Triangulated spaces and simplicial
sets are perhaps the most direct methods of describing topology in terms of
algebra, and we decided to start the book with an introduction to simplicial
methods. On the other hand, algebraic topology is unthinkable without ho-
motopy theory, and the book ends with a treatment of the foundations of
homotopic algebra in Chap. V.
     We worked on this book with the disquieting feeling that the development
of homological algebra is currently in a state of flux, and that the basic
definitions and constructions of the theory of triangulated categories, despite
their widespread use, are of only preliminary nature (this applies even more
to homotopic algebra). There is no doubt that similar thoughts have occurred
to the founders of the theory, and to everyone who has seriously worked with
it; the absence of a monographic exposition is one of the symptoms.
     Nevertheless, this period has already lasted twenty years; papers whose
main results cannot even be stated in the old language are multiplying; the
need for a textbook is growing. We therefore present this book to the benev-
olent judgment of the reader.


3
The plan of the book evolved gradually over several years when the authors
were running seminars in the Mathematics Department of Moscow Univer-
sity, and were in contact with members of the "Homological Algebra Fan
Club". A.A. Beilinson, M.M. Kapranov, V.V. Schechtman, whose papers and
explanations provided us with live examples of thinking in complexes.
    J.-P. Serre, J.N. Bernstein and M.M. Kapranov have read the manuscript
and made a series of very useful comments.

    V.E. Govorov very kindly to provided us with an extensive card index of
works on homological algebra.
    We are gra;teful to all them, and also to V.A. Ginzburg, R. MacPherson,
S.M. Khoroshkin and B.L. Tsygan.
   Our debt to the founding fathers of the subject, whose books, papers and
ideas we have used and have been inspired by, should be obvious from the
contents.

Reference Guide




1. General References. Five main sources for the classical homological al-
gebra are books by Cartan - Eilenberg [1], MacLane [1], Hilton - Stammbach
[1], Bourbaki [1] and the large paper by Grothendieck [1]. Simplicial methods
are presented in Gabriel - Zisman [1] and in May [1], sheaves in Godement
[1], Bredon [1], Golovin [1], Iversen [1]. Topoi are discussed, in particular, in
Goldblatt [1] and Johnstone [1]. Among the books on cohomology of various
algebraic structures we mention Brown [1], Serre [8], Guichardet [1], Fukchs
[2]. A large list of books on algebraic topology contains, among others, Eilen-
berg - Steenrod [1], Hilton - Wiley [1], Spanier [1], Dold [1], Massey [2],
Boardman - Vogt [1], Fukchs [1], Dubrovin - Novikov - Fomenko [1], Bott -
Tu [1].
     Modern algebraic geometry is an ample source of homological algebra of
various kind. Here we must mention the pioneering paper by Serre [3] and the
publications of Grothendieck and his school: Grothendieck - Dieudonne [1]
(especially Chaps. 0 and III) and [2], Grothendieck et al. [SGA] (especially
4, 4 1/2, 6), Artin [1], Hartshorne [1], Berthelot [1], Deligne [1], [2]. Among
several textbooks on this subject we mention Hartshorne [2] and Milne [1].
     The history of the homological algebra has yet to be written; we can
recommend to the interested reader the paper by Grey [1], the corresponding
parts from Dieudonne [1] and reminiscences of Grothendieck [5].
2. Topics We Have Not Considered in the Book.
a) Noncommutative cohomology. Some problems in group theory and topol-
ogy lead to cohomology with non-commutative coefficients. A systematic the-
ory exists only in low dimensions (::; 2 or ::; 3). An excellent exposition for
the case of group cohomology based on the paper by Dedecker [1] is Serre [7].
Most commonly used is 1-cohomology, or torsors. About intermediate "state
of the art" see Giraud [1].
b) Derivatives of non-additive functors. First constructions of derivatives of
non-additive functors, such as the symmetric or the exterior power of a mod-
ule, were suggested by Dold - Puppe [1]. Their technique was developed
further by Illusie [1] who applied such functors in certain algebraic geom-
etry situations. Crucial in the construction of these functors are simplicial
methods. In Feigin - Tsygan [1], [2] the additive K-theory is interpreted as
XII     Reference Guide


the derivation of the functor that associates to each ring its quotient by the
commutant.
c) Continuous cohomology. Functional analysis and infinite-dimensional ge-
ometry produce some cohomology-like construction in various categories of
algebraic structures with topology, such as linear topological spaces, Banach
algebras, Lie groups, etc. However, most of these categories (and the most
important ones) are non-abelian, and the standard technique does not work.
Usually in definition and computations the authors exploit some specific
classes of complexes. See Helemski [1], Guichardet [1], Borel - Wallach [1],
Johnson [1].
d) Products and duality. Some odds and ends the reader can find in various
parts of the book, but a satisfactory general theory in the framework of ho-
mological algebra presumably does not exist. See [SGA 2] and Hartshorne
[1] about the duality in algebraic geometry, Verdier [1], [2] and Iversen [1]
about the duality in topology. The theory of DG-algebras (see Chap. V)
can be considered as an attempt to introduce the multiplicative structure
"from scratch". About deeper results see Boardman - Vogt [1], Shechtman [1],
Hinich - Shechtman [1]. Classical theory of cohomological operations (Steen-
rod powers, Massey operations) also can be considered from such viewpoint.
e) Homological algebra and K -theory. The literature on K -theory is very
ample; see the basic papers by Quillen [1], [2], [4], the review by Suslin [2],
as well as [KTl], [KT2], where one can find further references.
f) Miscelleneous. Applications of Galois cohomology in number theory are
based, first of all, on class field theory; see the classical exposition in Artin -
Tate [1] and subsequent papers by Tate [1 J, Mazur [1 J, among others. There
exists a large literature on homological methods in commutative algebra; see
[ATT], Serre [6], Andre [2], [3], Avramov - Halperin [1], Quillen [3J. About
some other applications of homological algebra see [AN], [ES], [SDJ.
3. To Chapter I. Sect. 1.1-1.3: About further results of simplicial algebra,
and, in particular, about its applications to homological algebra, see Gabriel-
Zisman [IJ and May [1]; see also the remarks to Chap. V below. Its application
to the derivation of non-additive functors see in Dold - Puppe [1] and Illusie
[1]. Deligne extensively used symplicial methods in the theory of mixed Hodge
structures, see Deligne [1], Beilinson [2]. About Exercises 2, 3 to Sect. 1.2 see
Duskin [1], [2].
     Sect. 1.4: Algebraic topology is only slightly mentioned here, see Sect. 1
of this guide.
     Sect. 1.5: About the classical sheaf theory see Serre [3]' [4], Godement [1],
Bredon [1], Golovin [1], Iversen [1]. For sheaftheory in general topoi, as well
as in etale, cristalline, and other topoi of algebraic geometry see [SGA 4],
Artin [1], Berthelot [1], Milne [1].
     The most important development of the sheaf theory in the last ten years
is related to the notion of a perverse sheaf and the corresponding cohomolog-
ical formalism which is well suited to the study of singular varities. Perverse
                                                           Reference Guide     XIII

sheaves are objects of the derived category of usual sheaves, so that a perverse
sheaf is a complex of usual sheaves. See Goresky - MacPherson [1], Beilinson
- Bernstein - Deligne [1], and [IR], [ES].
    Sect. 1.6: An exact sequence is the main tool of homological algebra. See
further development in the framework of derived and triangulated categories
in Sect. II1.3 and IV.l.
    Sect. 1.7: There exists a large list of papers whose authors introduce and
study important specific resolution and complexes such as de Rham, Cech,
Koszul, Hochshild, and bar-resolutions, cyclic complexes, complexes of con-
tinuous co chains , etc. See, in particular, Priddy [1], [2], Karoubi [1], [2], [3],
Connes [1], Fukchs [2], Hochshild [1].
4. To Chapter II. Sect. ILl: See MacLane [1], Goldblatt [1], Faith [1];
about 2-categories see Gabriel - Zisman [1].
     Sect. 11.2: About the theory of fundamental group in algebraic geometry
see [SGA 2], about Gelfand duality see Gelfand - Shilov [1], about Morita
equivalence see Morita [1], Faith [1]. A classical example of the non-trivial
equivalence is the description of coherent sheaves on projective algebraic man-
ifolds by corresponding modules over homogeneous coordinate ring, see Serre
[3] and a generalization in Grothendieck - Dieudonne [1, EGA 3].
     Further generalizations of these ideas lead to remarkable equivalences be-
tween some derived categories, see Sect. IV.3.
     Sect. II.3: Several important theorems give an abstract characterization of
representable functors. About Freyd's theorem in the general category theory
framework see MacLane [2]. A lot of important spaces (like moduli spaces,
i.e. bases of universal deformations) in algebraic and analytic geometry are
introduced using the notion of a representable functor. In this context the
characterization of representable functors by a short list of easily verified
properties leads to some fundamental existence theorems, see Grothendieck
[1], [2], [4], Artin [1], Knutson [1].
     The fundamental notion of the adjoint functor was introduced by Kan
[1]. Several important constructions in algebra, geometry, and topology can
be described using this notion, see examples in Andre [1], Faith [1], MacLane
[2].
    Sect. II.4: For details about ringed spaces see Grothendieck - Dieudonne
[1, Chap. 0]' [2]. For the nerve of a category see Quillen [4], Suslin [1]. For
quadratic algebras (Ex. 5) see Manin [1].
    Sect. II.5-II.6: This is a classical part of the theory of abelian categories,
see Cartan - Eilenberg [1], MacLane [1], [2], Grothendieck [1]. For the de-
velopment of these ideas in the context of derived categories see Sect. II1.6,
IV.l. About ex. 1-7 in Sect. II.5 see MacLane [2]. About ex. 9 in Sect. II.5
see Serre [2].
5. To Chapter III. Sect. IILl-III.4: See Hartshorne [1], Verdier [3]. The
fundamental diagram in Lemma III.3.3 is taken from Bourbaki [1].
XIV     Reference Guide


     It seems that the main deficiency of the definition of a derived category is
in the bad definition of distinguished triangles. The problem of what should
be a good definition is discussed in unpublished notes of Deligne. See also the
discussion about the functor det in Knudsen - Mumford [1], and in [SGA 6]
and the definition of Tot in Exercises to IV.2.
     Sect. IlI.S: The classical theory of the functors Ext in terms of complexes
is due to Yoneda [1] (it generalizes the Baer's theory of Ext!). Homological
dimension was studied in algebraic geometry (Serre [4]), in depth theory
([SHA 2]), in group theory (Brown [1] and several papers in [HG]).
     About theorem IlI.S.21 see Hartshorne [1]. This theorem can be consid-
ered as one of the theorems establishing the equivalence between a derived
category and a category of complexes modulo homotopic equivalence, see
Beilinson [1], Bernstein - Gelfand - Gelfand [1], Kapranov [1], [2], [3]. For
results related to ex. 4 see Happel [1].
     Sect. IlI.6: The main references here are the same as in Sects. IlI.I-IlI.4.
For ex. I-S see Deligne's paper in Grothendieck et al. [SGA 4, XVII], for ex.
6 see Roos [1], [2], about ex. 7-10 see Spaltenstein [1].
     Sect. III.7: While an exact sequence can be considered as the main tool in
the study of cohomology dependence on the abelian variable, a spectral se-
quence plays a similar role in the study of the dependence on the non-abelian
variable. The first spectral sequence was introduced, presumably, by Leray
[1]; the classical exposition of Serre [1] remains an excellent introduction into
the subject. The standard construction of the spectral sequence associated to
a filtered complex is given in Cartan - Eilenberg [1], and the one associated
to an exact couple is given in Massey [1] (see also Eckmann - Hilton [1], [2]).
Grothendieck [1] showed that some standard spectral sequences relate derived
functors of the composition to the derived functors of factors. However, spec-
tral sequences in homotopic topology are of different nature, see McCleary
[1]. Fukchs [1] gives a fascinating description of the Adams spectral sequence.
See also exercises to IV.2.
     Sect. IlI.S: This section, together with exercises to it and to IV.4, presents
sheaf cohomology theory, as it is seen nowadays. The main difference from
the status fixed in Godement [1] is the appearence of the functor 1', which
can be defined only using derived categories. This functor leads to the Verdier
duality, which also can be formulated only in derived categories, and to the
"six functors" formalism (see exercises to IV.4). References are Verdier [1],
[2], [4], the volume [IR], Iversen [1]. The parallel theory in algebraic geometry
is presented in Hartshorne [1] for coherent sheaves and in [SGA 4] (especially
XVII) for etale topology.
6. To Chapter IV. Sect. IV.I-IV.2: The main sources for us were Verdier
[3], Hartshorne [2] and Kapranov [3]. See also Happel [1], Iversen [1]. Exercises
to Sect. IV.2 were composed by Kapranov.
     Sect. IV.3: The description of derived categories of coherent sheaves on
on projective spaces was initiated in Beilinson [1] and Bernstein - Gelfand-
                                                         Reference Guide     XV

Gelfand [1]. A series of consecutive generalizations of this theory was found
by Kapranov [1], [2], [3]; see also Meltzer [1]. The "S-A duality"described in
this section possesses far reaching generalizations, see Priddy 1], [2], Lofwall
[1], Happel [1], Gorodentsev - Rudakov [1].
     Sect. IV.4: We present here some ideas from Beilinson - Bernstein -
Deligne [1]. The main application of this theory, that is, the construction of
perverse sheaves, is not discussed in this book. About ex. 1-5 see Beilinson
- Bernstein - Deligne [1], about ex. 6 see Bernstein - Gelfand - Ponomarev
[1], Brenner - Butler [1], about ex. 7 see Happel [1].
7. To Chapter V. In this chapter we study homotopic algebra (algebraic
foundations of homotopy theory), which much less developed than homolog-
ical algebra.
    Sect. V.I-V.2: Here we introduce the main axiomatic notion that of a
closed model category (Quillen [1]), which axiomatizes the main homotopic
properties of topological spaces. Since we view simplicial sets as a bridge
between topology and algebra, we give a (rather lengthy) proof that simplicial
sets form a closed model category. We hope that these two sections will help
an interested reader to study deeper parts of the book by Quillen [1], as
well as further literature: Quillen [2], [4], May [1], Bousfield - Gugenheim [1],
Tanre [1].
    Sect. V.3-V.4: The second part of this chapter introduces to the reader
some ideas of the famous paper by Sullivan [1] where he shows that the
rational homotopic type of a manifold can be determined by its algebra of
differential forms.
    We prove that differential graded algebras form a closed model category
and study minimal models in this category.
    Exercises to these sections are based, mainly, on results from Tanre [1].
    Sect. V.5: Here we present (without proofs) main results of the theory
of rational homotopic type. The proofs, together with further details and
references, can be found in Lemann [1], Bousfield - Gugenheim [1], Deligne
- Griffiths - Morgan - Sullivan [1], Morgan [1], Halperin [1], Avramov -
Halperin [1], Tanre [1].

Chapter I. Simplicial Sets




§ 1. Triangulated Spaces

1. Main Definitions

Figure 1 depicts three triangulated spaces. Their main property is that they
are glued from simplices: points, segments, triangles, tetrahedra and their
higher dimensional generalizations. Such spaces can be described combina-
torially: one has to specify how many simplices of any dimension should be
taken and how they should be glued together. Let us give precise definitions.
    a) The n-dimensional simplex is a topological space




The point ei for which Xi = 1 is called the i-th vertex of ..1n ; the set of
vertices is ordered: eo < el < ... < en. More generally, to each subset
I C [n] ~ {O, 1, ... , n} one associates the 1-th face of ..1 n defined as the set of
all points (xo, ... ,xn ) E ..1 n with Xi = 0 for i r/: I. Sometimes it is convenient
to replace I by the monotone (increasing) mapping f : [m] -> [n] with the
image I, where card I = m + 1. It is clear that there exists a unique linear
mapping ..1 f : ..1 m -> ..1 n that preserves the order of vertices and has the
I-th face as its image.
    b) By gluing data we mean the following set X of structures:
     What to glue: X(O) points, X(1) segments, X(2) triangles, ... , X(n) n-
dimensional simplices. (Elements of X(n) serve as indices to number sim-
plices).
    How to glue: for any pair (n, Ie [n]), card 1= m+l, a map X(n) -> X(m)
is given that specifies which m-dimensional simplex should be identified with
the I-th face of the corresponding n-dimensional simplex.
    More precisely, let a face correspond to an increasing map f : [m] -> [n]
and let XU) : X(n) -> X(m) be the corresponding gluing map. The family
{X un    should satisfy the two following conditions:

                    X(id) = id,       X(g 0 J) = XU) 0 X(g)
2      I. Simplicial Sets


(where id is the identity map). This means that to different elements of X(n)
correspond different simplices and that "a face of a face is a face" .
    What one gets after gluing is a topological space IXI with the underlying
set U~=O (Lln x X(n)) jR, where R is the weakest equivalence relation that
identifies (s, x) E Lln x X(n) and (t, y) E Ll m x X(m) with

                            y = X(f)x,                                         (1.1)
for some increasing mapping f : [mJ ---> [nJ. We shall denote the situation as in
(1.1) by (t, y) J.. (s, x). The canonical topology on IX I is the weakest topology
for which the canonical mapping Il~=o (L1n x X(n)) ---> IXI is continuous.
    The space IXI together with the corresponding gluing data is called a
triangulated space, and the gluing data themselves are called its triangulation.

4. Skeleton

The k-skeleton of the triangulation (X(i)' X (J)) is the gluing data
(X(i),i::; k;X(J)). The corresponding triangulated space SkklXI is called
the k-skeleton of the triangulated space IXI. Using the previous proposition
we get the following properties of skeleta:
a) IXI = skoolXI = Uk:::-OskkIXI·
b) Natural mappings SkklXI----+ skdXI, k ::; l, are closed imbedding.
c) Skk+1IXI is obtained from SkklXI by gluing several (k + I)-dimensional
   open simplices along their common boundaries.

1. Draw triangulations of the Mobius sheet, ofthe real projective plane, and
of the two-dimensional torus.


§2. Simplicial Sets

1. Definition. A simplicial set is a family of sets X. = (X n ), n = 0,1, ...
and of maps XU) : Xn ~ X m , one for each nondecreasing map f : [m] ~ [n]
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

                     X(id) = id,X(g 0 f) = XU) 0 X(g).

    The only difference with the gluing data introduced in L1.1b) is that f
need not be strictly increasing. Elements of Xn are called n-simplices. Instead
of X. we will often write simply X.
    For any nondecreasing map f : [m] ~ [n] we define the "f-th face" Ll f
as the linear map Ll m ~ Ll n that maps any vertex ei E Ll m into the vertex
ef(i) E Lln, i = 0, ... , m.
    Contrary to the situation in L1.1a), Ll f is not necessarily an embedding: if
f is not strictly increasing, then Ll f decreases the dimension gluing together
some vertices of Ll m .

2. Definition. The geometric realization IXI of a simplicial set (Xn) is the
topological space with the underlying set Il:=o (Ll n x Xn) / R, where R is the
                                                         2. Simplicial Sets      7


weakest equivalence relation identifying points (s, x) E Lln x Xn and (t, y) E
Ll m x Xm if
                         y = X(f)x,        s = Llf(t)
for some non decreasing map f : [mJ ~ [nJ. As in I.1 we shall write these
conditions as
                               (t,y) f-> (s,x).
    The topology on IXI is the weakest one for which the factorization by R
is continuous.

3. Nerve of Covering
Let Y be a topological space, U = (U,:x) be its covering (either by open or by
closed sets), a running over some set A. Let
                   Xn = {(ao, ... ,an) IUno n ... n Un" =I- 0}
         X(f)(ao, ... ,an) = (af(O),.·.,af(m)) for f: [mJ ~ [nJ.
    This simplicial set reflects the combinatorial structure of a covering. One
can show that if the covering U is locally finite and all nonempty finite inter-
sections Uno n ... n Un" are contractible, then the geometric realization IXI
of X is homotopically equivalent to Y, so that the topology can be efficiently
encoded into combinatorical data.
    The similar role is played by the following construction.

4. Singular Simplices
Let Y be a topological space. A singular n-simplex of X is a continuous map
rp : Ll n ~ Y. Let
             Xn = set of all singular n-simplices ofY, n = 0,1, ...
         X(f)(rp) = rp 0 Llf where f: [mJ ~ [nJ, Llf: Ll m ~ Ll n ·
    Sets Xn are, in general, very large; if Y possesses an additional struc-
ture it might be reasonable to consider only those singular simplices that
are compatible with this structure (smooth if Y is a smooth manifold, or
linear if Y is a polyhedron). Finally, if Y is a triangulated space with the
gluing data (X(n),X'(f)) then, by Proposition 1.1.3, Y is a disjoint union
of open simplices (including vertices) of its triangulation. A singular simplex
rp : Ll n ~ Y is said to be compatible with a triangulation if rp(Ll n ) coincides
with one of its simplices and rp : Lln ~ rp(Ll n ) is linear and preserves the
order of vertices. Let
             X~ = set of all singular simplices compatible with
                   the triangulation (X(n)' C(f));
                              X'(f)(rp) = rp 0 Ll f .
    This construction enables us to associate to each triangulation a simplicial
set with the same geometric realization.

6. The Simplicial Set Associated to a Triangulated Space

Let IXI be a triangulated space corresponding to the gluing data X(n) ,
n = 0,1, ... and X(J) : X(n) ----t X(m) for increasing maps f : [m] ----t [n].
U sing these data, we construct a simplicial set X = (Xn' X (J)) as follows.
As Xm we take the set of all pairs (x, g) where x E X(k) and 9 : [m] ----t [k] is a
nondecreasing map. Let now (x,g) E Xm and f: [m]----t [n] be a nondecreas-
ing map. Let us represent go f : [n] ----t [k] as a composition go f = It 012,
where It : [l] ----t [k] is an injection and 12 : [n] ----t [l] is a surjection, and
set X(J)(x, g) = (X(It)x, h) E X n . We leave it to the reader to verify that
X(id) = id and X(J' 0 J) = XU) 0 X(J').
    In corollary I.2.13 of this section we shall show that the geometric realiza-
tion IXI of this simplicial set X is homeomorphic to the triangulated space
IXI·
    Let us remark also that not every simplicial set can be obtained from some
gluing data. There are "fewer" gluing data than simplicial sets. We leave to
the reader the proof of the following statement:

7. Proposition. A simplicial set X can be obtained from some gluing data
X iff for any nondegenerate (see I.2.9 below) simplex x E Xn and for any in-
creasing map f : [m] ----t [n] the simplex X(J)(x) E Xm is also nondegenerate.
In this case X determines X uniquely.

    The structure of the geometric realization of a simplicial set X can be
clarified using an analogue of Proposition 1.1.3. To formulate such an analogue
we need the notion of a nondegenerate simplex.

9. Nondegenerate Simplices

Let X be a simplicial set. An n-simplex x E Xn is said to be degenerate iff
there exists a surjective nondecreasing map f : [n] -> [m], m < n, and an
element y E Xm such that x = X(f)y. One can easily check that if x is
nondegenerate and x = X(f)y for some f and y, then f is an injection.
   Let now

14. Skeleton and Dimension

Let X be a simplicial set. The n-skeleton of X is a simplicial set sknX with

(sknX)p = {x E Xp 13q ::; n, 3f : [P] ---- [q], 3y' E Xq such that x = X(f)y}

(skn' X) (g) is a restriction of X(g) on sknX.
     Hence, p-simplices of the n-skeleton of X are exactly degenerations of
q-simplices of X with q ::; n. We leave to the reader to verify that X(g), 9 :
[p] ---- [q], maps (sknX)q into (sknX)p'
     From the definitions and from the proof of Proposition 1.2.10 we obtain
that the mapping
                            r II ~m xX(m) ---- IsknXI
                              m<S;n

is one-to-one. Therefore, IsknXI is a closed subspace in IXI.
    A simplicial set X is said to have the dimension n if X = sknX -I skn_1X.
This means that any simplex of dimension > n is degenerate, while nonde-
generated n-simplices do exist.

§3. Simplicial Topological Spaces
and the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem

1. Three Descriptions of Lip X Liq

In 1.1.5 we have already remarked that the product of two geometric simplices
is not a simplex, and we have constructed the canonical triangulation of this
product by "diagonal" simplices. Thus we have the following view at Llp x Llq.
    a) Llp x Llq is the geometric realization of the simplicial set X whose
nondegenerated N-simplices are "shufHes" {(io,jo), ... , (in,jn)), 0 ::; io ::;
... ::; i ::; p, 0::; jo ::; ... ::; jn ::; q, all pair (ik,jk) are distinct.
    On the other hand we have set-theoretically
    b) Llp x Llq = UxE..:l q (Ll p x x).
    c) Llp x Llq = UyE..:l p (y x Ll q ).
    Two last descriptions are rather similar to the definition of Llp x Llq
by some gluing data. There are two differences: a nonessential one, and an
essential one. The nonessential one is that Llp (resp. Llq) at the right-hand
side of b) (resp. of c)) should be replaces by its standard triangulation. The
essential difference is that we must take into account the topology on the set
of indices x E Llq (resp. y E Llp); otherwise, the corresponding "geometric
realization" of Llp x Llq split into a continuum of connected components.
Therefore, we must change the definition of geometric realization in such a
way that it would take into account the topology on the set of indices.

2. Definition. a) A simplicial topological space is a family of topological
spaces X = (X n ), n = 0,1,2, ... and of continuous maps XU) : Xn -> X m ,
one for each nondecreasing map f : [m] -> [n], such that

                    X(id) = id, X(g 0 j) = XU) 0 X(g).
18       I. Simplicial Sets


    b) The geometric realization IXI of a simplicial topological space is a topo-
logical space with the set of points l1~=o (L1n x Xn) / R where R is the equiv-
alence relation from I.2.2. The topology of IXI is the weakest topology for
which the factorization map


                              II (L1n x Xn)
                              00

                                              ---t   IXI
                              n=O

is continuous; at the left-handside we have the disjoint union of the product
of topological spaces.

    This definition is a reasonable axiomatization of the viewpoints I.3.1b)
and I.3.1c).
    On the other hand, a reasonable generalization of the combinatorial data
for the decomposition of a topological space into bicsimplices L1p x L1q is the
notion of a bisimplicial set.

3. Definition. A bisimplicial set is a family of sets (Xmn), m, n = 0, 1,2, ... ,
and of maps X(f, g) : Xpq ---t X mn , one for each pair of non decreasing maps
f : [m] ---t [p], 9 : [n] ---t [q], such that

              X(id, id) = id, X(f 0 f', gog') = X(f', g') 0 X(f, g).

4. Example. Let X, Y be two simplicial sets, and let

                    Zmn = Xm X Y m , Z(f, g) = (X(f), Y(g)).

     This bisimplicial set is called the direct product of X and Y.
5. Definition. The diagonal of a bisimplicial set X = (Xmn, X(f, g)) is the
simplicial set D X with

                        (DX)n = X nn , DX(f) = X(f, 1).

6. Geometric Realization of a Bisimplicial Set

It can be defined in three different ways according to the three descriptions
of L1p x L1q.
    a) For a simplicial set X we define IXI D = IDXI to be the geometric
realization of the diagonal of X.
    b) First construct a simplicial topological space X I that can be considered
as a "geometric realization of X with respect to the first index":

                         X~ = IXenl,XI(g) = IX(id,g)l.
More explicitely, for a fixed second index n the family of sets and of mappings

of this simplicial set.
    Next, for any nondecreasing map 9 : [n] --+ [n'] the family

                   X (id[m] , g) : X mn' --+ X mn' m = 0,1, ... ,
is a simplicial map of simplicial sets X. n' --+ X.n; we denote it by X(id,g).
Its geometric realization (see 1.2.12) IX(id,g)1 is a continuous map of the

               X(id, id) = id, X(id, gog') = X(id, g') 0 X(id, g).

7. Theorem (Eilenberg-Zilber). Three geometric realizations of a bisimplicial
set X are canonically isomorphic.

8. The Plan of the Proof

Denote by D[m, n] the following simplicial set:

                              D[m, n] = D(Ll[m] x Ll[n]).
Let us consider the following disjoint union of simplices:

                      z = II (Xmn x D[m, n]k x Llk) .
                               m,n,k

Introduce on Z three equivalence relations R 1 , RI I, RD generated by the
following identifications:

              RI       (x E X mn ' (f, g) E D[m, n]k, s E Llk)
                       rv   (x' E Xm'n, (f', g) E D[m', nJk' s E Ll k) ,
if there exists h : [mJ --+ [m'J such that

                            X (h, id[n]) (x') = x, h 0 f = 1'.
(Recall that D[m, n]k consists of pairs of nondecreasing maps f : [kJ --+ [m],
g: [k] --+ [n].)
20       I. Simplicial Sets


     Similarly

                 RII      (x E X mn , (f,g) E D[m, nlk' 8 E Ll k)
                          rv (x' E X mn " (f, g') E D[m, n'lk, 8 E Ll k ) ,


if there exists h : [nl . . . . [n'l such that

                              X (id[m], h) (x') = x, hog' = g.
     Finally, RD is generated by identifications

               RD         (x E X mn , (f, g) E D[m, nlk' 8 E Ll k)
                         rv (x E X mn , (f', g') E D[m, nlk" 8' E Ll k,) ,
if there exists a nondecreasing map h: [kl . . . . [k'l such that

                          l' 0 h = j,g' 0 h = g,Llh(8) = 8'.
    We show that factorizing Z with respect to R I , RI I, RD in different
orders we get respectively IXII,II, IXIII,I, IXID. On the other hand, we can
factorize Z by the equivalence relation generated by RI, R II , RD obtaining
the same space.

9. Consecutive Factorizations

We describe the last assertion in somewhat different form and in a more
general situation. Let Z be an arbitrary set, Rl and R2 be two equivalence
relations on Z, R be the equivalence relation generated by RI and R 2 . We
want to show that the following three factorizations coincide:
a) Let R2/ RI be the equivalence relation on Z/ RI generated by the following
   identifications: (x mod R I ) rv (y mod R I ) mod R 2 /R I if in Rl-classes of
   x, y there exist R 2 -equivalent representatives x', y'.
   Now we can take (Z/R I )/(R2 /R I ).
b) Similarly we can define (Z/R 2 )/(RI/R2 ).
c) We can take also Z / R.
    We leave it to the reader to prove that these three quotient spaces are
naturally isomorphic, and any of them can be obtained as follows: x, y E Z
lie in the same class iff there exists a chain x = xo, Xl, ... ,Xn = Y such that
for any i either Xi rv Xi+! mod RI or Xi rv Xi+! mod R 2 .
     One can prove a similar statement for any finite family of equivalences.
     Finally, if Z is a topological space and any quotient inherits the weakest
topology for which the factorization is continuous, then all three quotient
spaces as above are natural homeomorphic. In fact, a subset in any of these
quotients is open iff its preimage in Z is open.
     Now we can describe the results of all intermediate factorizations of the
space Z from 1.3.8.

§4. Homology and Cohomology

1. Chains and Co chains

The boundary of the geometric simplex Ll1 is the difference of its vertices (1)-
(0). In such a form the boundary appears in the Leibniz formula Jo1 f'(x)dx =
f(I) - f(O). Similarly, the boundary of Ll n is the alternating sum of its faces.
    To make these definitions precise we need the following notions:
    An n-dimensional chain (or simply n-chain) of a simplicial set X is an
element of the free abelian group Cn (X) generated by all n-simplices of X. So
an n-dimensional chain is a formal linear combination LXEXn a(x)x, where
a(x) E Z and a(x) =I 0 for a finite number of simplices x.
    Let a~ : [n - 1] ---> [n] be the unique strictly increasing mapping whose
image does not contain i E [n].
    The boundary of an n-chain c E Cn(x) is the (n -I)-chain dnc defined by
the following formula:
                                                  n

              dn   l~. a(x)x) L a(x) L (_I)i X (a~) (x).
                                     xEXn        i=O
                                                                                 (I.3)
24     I. Simplicial Sets


   The so defined boundary operator dn : Cn(X) -> Cn - 1 (X) is clearly a
group homomorphism. For n = 0 we set do = O.
   There exists an obvious generalization of this construction, namely, chains
with coefficients in an abelian group A. Such a chain is a formal linear com-
bination EXEx n a(x)x, a(x) E A. In other words,
                            Cn(X,A) = Cn (X)0A,
                                                    z
so that Cn(X) = Cn(X, IE).
    The boundary operator dn : Cn(X, A) -> Cn+l(X, A) is again defined by
the formula (1.3).
    Dually, one can define cochains with coefficients in A: cn(x, A) is the
group of functions on Xn with values in A.
    The co boundary dn : cn(x, A) -> cn+l(x, A) is given by the formula
                                  n+l
                  (d n J) (x) =   L (_l)i f (X (a~+l) (x)).                  (2)
                                  i=O
    Formally, chains can be considered as special cases of cochains: there exists
an inclusion Cn(X,A) c cn(X,A) that maps a chain EXEXn a(x)x into the
function a : Xn -> A. However, this inclusion is incompatible with the action
of dn and dn (they act in opposite directions) and, even more important, it is
incompatible with the behavior of Cn and cn under simplicial maps X -> Y
(we will consider this later).
    Let us note also that in (1.3) we can not allow the infinite number of
non-zero coefficients a(x) because the right-hand side can become undefined.
On the contrary, in (2) we can consider a subgroup C'l(X, A) of co chains
taking non-zero values only for a finite number of simplices x; the coboundary
operator maps this group into Cj+l(X, A).
2. Lemma. a) dn- 1 0 dn = 0 for n ~ l.
   b) dn+l 0 dn = 0 for n ~ O.

3. Complexes
Let us define several algebraic notions. A chain complex is a sequence of
abelian groups and homomorphisms
                       C• : ... dn+l
                                ~
                                        dn C
                                     Cn ~       dn-l
                                            n-l ~ ...
with the property dn 0 dn+l = 0 for all n. Homomorphisms dn are called
boundary maps or boundary operators.
   A cochain complex is a similar sequence
                        • .   dn - 1   n   dn     n+l d n + 1
                      C .... ~C~C                       ~ ... ,

d n odn - 1 = O. Any chain complex can be transformed into a cochain complex
by setting Dn = C- n , d n = d_ n - 1. So we will usually consider only cochain
complexes.
    The following definition is the central one in homological algebra.
4. Definition. a) Homology groups of a chain complex C. are
                          Hn(C.) = Kerdn/Imdn+l.
b) Cohomology groups of a cochain complex C· are
                          Hn(c·) = Kerd n /Imdn - 1 .
    A substantial part of homological algebra can be considered as a collection
of methods for computing (co ) homology of various complexes. In this section
we will give the simplest examples. For a simplicial set X we will use the
following notations:
          Hn(X, A) = Hn (C. (X, A)), Hn(x, A) = H n (C· (X, A)).
    Elements of the group Hn(X, A) are called homology classes, those of
Hn(x, A) are called cohomology classes (of the simplicial set X with coeffi-
cients in A). Each homology (resp. cohomology) class is represented by an
n-chain c (resp. cochain f) such that dnc = 0 (resp. dn f = 0). Such chains
(resp. co chains) are called cycles (resp. cocycles). A cycle c in a given homol-
ogy class is defined up to a summand of the form b = dn+l c'; such chains are
called boundaries. Similarly, cochains of the form dn-1c' are called cobound-
aries. Two chains whose difference is a boundary are said to be homological.
    The same terminology applies to general complexes.
    Let us clarify the meaning of the above definitions in simple examples. We
will consider for a moment only triangulated spaces and chains constructed
from nondegenerated simplices (this will be justified by Ex. 7.l.d).

7. Coefficient Systems

We can construct chains and cochains of a simplicial set using as coefficients
something more involved than just abelian groups. There are two types of
coefficient systems: for homology and for cohomology.
8. Definition. a) A homological coefficient system A on a simplicial set X
is a family of abelian groups {Ax}, one for each simplex x E X n , and a
family of homomorphisms A(f, x) : Ax - t AX(f)x, one for each pair x E X n ,
f : [m] - t [n], such that the following conditions are satisfied:
                             A(id, x)      idj
                           A(fog,x)        A(g, X (f)x)A(f, x).
     The second equality means that the following diagram is commutative:

                                        AX(f)x



                               /~
                              Ax                 • AX(fog)x                  (1.6)


    b) A cohomological coefficient system B on a simplicial set X is a family
of abelian groups {Bx}, one for each simplex x E X n , and a family of ho-
momorphisms B(f, x) : BX(f)x - t B x , one for each pair (x, f) as in a), such
that the following conditins are satisfied:
                  B(id, x) = idj B(f 0 g, x) = B(f, x)B(g, X(f)x).           (1. 7)
    The second equality is equivalent to the commutativity of a diagram sim-
ilar to (1.6).

9. Remarks and Examples

a) Let Ax = A for each x, A(f, x) = id for each f, x. Such coefficient system
is called constant. It is both homological and cohomological.
    b) Let Y be a topological space, U = (Ua) an open covering, X the nerve
of U defined in 1.2.3. The following data form a cohomological coefficient

11. Examples

a) Homology and cohomology of a simplicial set X with coefficients in the con-
stant system A = (A, idA) (see 1.4.9a) coincide with Hn(X, A) and Hn(x, A)
respectively.

functions on Y with respect to the covering (UoJ. Taking other coefficient
systems we get similar groups for sheaves of smooth, analytic, etc. functions
(see also n.7.4).

the cohomological coefficient system constructed in 1.4.9c). Groups Hn(X, A)
and Hn(x, B) are called homology and cohomology groups respectively ofthe
group G with coefficients in A.

§5. Sheaves

1. Examples of Sheaves

a) Holomorphic Functions of the Riemann Sphere. The Riemann
sphere is the topological space C U {(X)} with the base of neighbourhoods
of infinity formed by exteriors of circles in C. The structure of the Riemann
sphere includes also a family of complex-valued function f : U ~ C on open
subsets U. Such functions (called holomorphic) can be characterized by the
following property: any point Zo E U has a neighbourhood V, Zo EVe U,
in which f is represented by a convergent power series E:'o ai(z - ZO)i for
Zo E C or E:'o aiz-i for Zo = 00. The family of all such functions (more
precisely, of pairs (1, V) consisting of a function f and its domain V - not
necessary the maximal one!) is called the sheaf 0 of holomorphic function on
C U { 00 }. Due to the locality of the definition of holomorphicity, two following
problems can be very nontrivial.
    Problem 1. Whether or not a given holomorphic function f : U ~ C can
be extended to a larger open set V :J U?
    (Example: the holomorphic continuation of the Riemann zeta-function
((z) = E~=l n- Z from the region {z E C IRez > 1} to C\{1}. For some
other interesting Dirichlet series E~=l ann- z with integral coefficients, this
problem is still unsolved).
    Problem 2. Describe the set r(U, 0) of all function holomorphic in a given
domain U.
    For example, the Liouville theorem asserts that r(C U {oo}, 0) consists
of constant functions only.
    The theorem about the convergence radius of a Taylor series says that




   The main point of the sheaf theory is that we should not pay attention to
such particular problems, considering instead the sheaf 0 as a single object
and compare it with other similar objects.
b) The Sheaf of Solutions of a Linear Differential Equation. Let
U C C U {(x)} be an open set, ai(z) E r(U, 0), i = 0,1, ... , n - 1. Denote by
S the set of pairs (V, 1) consisting of an open subset V C U and a holomorphic
function f in V satisfying in V the equation

This gives the sheaf of holomorphic solutions of the differential equation
Lf =0.
32        I. Simplicial Sets


      In the case when V is connected and simply connected, the theorem on
the existence and uniqueness of a solution implies that the space r(V, S) of
solutions holomorphic in V is an n-dimensional linear space over C. For more
complicated regions V the answer ceases to be that simple, and to formulate
it one has to introduce of the notion of monodromy. Let, for example, Lf =
d 2 f /dz 2 +z- 1 df /dz, U = C\O. Solutions of the equation Lf = 0 are c1log z+
C2, where log z is "any branch" of the logarithm function. For example, in
an annulus V : 0 ~ rl < Izl < r2 there is no single valued branch of the
logarithm, hence r(V, S) = {constants}. The computation of r(V, S) for
an operator L of the second order with three singular points of simplest
type on the Riemann sphere constitutes a substantial part of the theory of
hypergeometric functions.
      The sheaf S is, in an obvious sense, a subsheaf of 0 and L, in the same
obvious sense, acts from 0 to 0 in such a way that S is the kernel of this
action. The formalis we present later in this section axiomatizes the structures
in the examples above.
2. Definition. a) A presheaf of sets F on a topological space Y consists of
the following data:
- a set F(U) (of sections of the presheaf F) for any open subset U c Y;
- a (restriction) map ruv : F(U) ----+ F(V) for any pair V c U.
These data should satisfy the following conditions:
                  rUU = id, rvw 0 ruv = ruw         for   W eVe U.
    b) A presheaf:F is said to be a sheaf if the following additional condition
is satisfied:
- For any open covering U = UiE1Ui and for any family of sections Si E

                               rUi,UinUj (Si) = rUj,UinUj (Sj),

     for all i E I.

J(U) : F(U) ----+ g(U), one for any open set U C Y, commuting with the
restrictions:
                        rUv 0 f(U) = f(V) 0 ruv·
    A morphism of sheaves is a morphism of corresponding presheaves.
    Instead of F(U) one uses also the notation r(U, F), as we have done in
1.5.1.
    For any presheaf F one can define the presheaf F /U - the restriction of
F on an open set U by setting (F/U)(V) = F(U n V). If F is a sheaf, F /U
is also a sheaf.

3. Presheaves and Sheaves of Structured Sets

A presheaf F can be a presheaf of groups, rings, topological spaces, etc.; by
definition, this means that each set F(U) is endowed with the corresponding
structure and each restriction map is a morphism of corresponding structures.
    Similarly one can define external composition laws: a presheaf of modulus
M over a presheaf of rings 0 consists of a family of O(U)-modules M(U)
such that the composition laws commute with restrictions in an obvious sense.
    Here is an example that will play an important role later: Let F, Q be
two presheaves of abelian groups on a topological space Y. A morphism
f : F ---t Q consists of a family of group homomorphisms f(U) : F(U) ---t Q(U)
commuting with restrictions. Let

                   K(U) = Ker(U),C(U) = Q(U)/f(F(U)).
    Defining K(U) ---t K(V), C(U) ---t C(V) for V c U in an obvious way we
get the presheaves K and C that are called respectively the kernel and the
cokernel of the morphism f.
   A sequence of presheaves F -.!...... Q ~ 1{ is said to be exact at Q if for
any open U C Y the sequence of abelian groups F(U) !J!!J Q(U) f!S!!J1i(U)
is exact at Q(U) (i.e. Ker g(U) = 1m f(u), see Sect. 1.6).
    Let us look through all the constructions of this subsection under the
assumption that all involved presheaves are in fact sheaves.
    Definitions of sheaves of rings, groups, etc. and of their morphisms do not
change.
    We have to be careful only in those cases when we construct new
presheaves from old ones: even if old presheaves were sheaves, the new one
might fail to be a sheaf. Let us consider a typical situation.
4. Proposition. a) The kernel K a morphism of sheaves of abelian groups
f : F ---t Q is a sheaf of abelian groups.
    b) The cokernel of a morphism of sheaves of abelian groups is always a
presheaf but might fail to be a sheaf.
Proof a) Let U = UUi and Si E K(Ui ) be a family of sections of K that
agree with each other on pairwise intersections. As K(Ui ) C F(Ui ) and F is
a sheaf, there exists a unique section 8 such that 8i = rU,Ui (8). Let us verify
that 8 E K(U). We have rU,ui (f(8)) = f (ru,ui (8)) = O. As Q is a sheaf, there
exists a unique section of Q over U whose restriction on each Ui is zero, and
this is clearly the zero section. Therefore, f(8) = 0 and 8 E K(U).
     b) Let us give an example. Let Y = C\{O}, and let Oy be the sheaf of
holomorphic functions on Y (see 1.5.1a). Define f : Oy ---t Oy by f(cp) =
dcp / dz for a holomorphic function cp on an open set U eX. One can easily
see that any y E Y has a neighbourhood Vy such that Coker f (Vy) = 0 (more
precisely, this property holds for any V C Y that does not encircle the point
0). On the other hand, dim(Cokerf(Y)) = 1: the equation dcp/dz = lJt for a

holomorphic function tJi on Y with the Laurent series tJi =         I::-

solution iff a_I = O. Therefore, the presheaf Coker f on Y is not a sheaf. 0
    In Sect. II.5 we will show that the definition of the cokernel of a morphism
of sheaves can and should be changed in such a way that the cokernel will
always be a sheaf.
    In Sect. 1.7 of this chapter we introduce the notion of Cech cohomology
with coefficients in presheaves and in sheaves; in particular, I-cohomology
measures the degree of violation of the axiom 2b of a sheaf.
    Returning to general definitions, we introduce the following useful notions.

7. Main Classes of Sheaves

Roughly speaking, we can divide sheaves arising in various mathematical
problems, into two large classes: a) sheaves similar to sheaves of functions
(holomorphic, as in example I.5.1a, or smooth, continuous, algebraic, etc.),
b) sheaves similar to constant sheaves (as the solution sheaf in the example
I.5.1b).
    We give some examples and definitions starting with class b).

8. Definition. Let A be a set, Y a topological space.
    a) The constant presheaf A on Y with fiber A is defined by A(U) = A for
all U C Y, ru, v = id for all V cUe Y.
    b) The constant sheaf A on Y with fiber A is A = A + .

   This example clearly illustrates the influence of the topology of the space
Y on the structure of a sheaf. The space of A is, obviously, Y x A (with
the discrete topology on A). Therefore sections of A = A + over an open set
36      I. Simplicial Sets


U are locally constant functions on U with values in A. In particular, for a
connected U we have A(U) = A.
   The following, more interesting, class of sheaves contains, in particular,
sheaves of holomorphic solutions of linear differential equations from 1.5.1b.
9. Definition. A sheaf:F on Y is said to be locally constant if any point Y
has an open neighbourhood U such that :F I U is a constant sheaf

    Let us remark here that Definitions 1.5.8 and 1.5.9 can be extended to
sheaves of structured sets provided the structure is stable under the limits
required to pass from A to A + .
    However, it might happen that a sheaf that is constant as a sheaf of sets
ceases to be constant when considered as a sheaf of structures. A simple
example is a sheaf of Lie algebras depending on parameters.
    The classification of locally constant sheaves over a topological space is
closely related to the structure of the fundamental group of this space (see
11.2.9).
    Further generalization of the notion of locally constant sheaves gives con-
structive sheaves. We shall not enter into details here; let us remark only that
these sheaves enable us to describe some types of "jumps" and of "singular-
ities" like, for example, those arising as singularities of solutions of linear
differential equation.

10. Sheaves of Functions and Extension of Sections

Sheaves of functions can be considered, first of all, as a glue that enables one
to construct global objects (smooth manifolds, analytic spaces, scheme, etc.)
from local models. This viewpoint is studied in detail in 11.1.4, and we advise
the reader to look through the beginning of 11.1.4 before going further.
    Here we consider only one property of sheaves of functions, namely the
rigidity of a sheaf. The simplest question about the rigidity is the following
one. Let U c V be two nonempty open sets; whether or not a section of a
sheaf on U can be extended to V and what can be said about the uniqueness
of such an extension. Let us consider the full spectrum of possibilities.
    a) The function on U might be nonextendable even to the closure (or on
the part of the boundary) of U, to say nothing about V. Example: 1/ (x 2 - 1)
for U = (-1,1) C R This remark can be applied equally to continuous,
smooth, or analytic functions. Therefore the natural extension question can
be asked about closed, and not open, sets. Let us remind, that, according to
1.5.5, a section of 0 over Z is a class of sections of 0 over open neighbourhoods
U'~Z.
    b) So, let Z c X be a closed subset. If Y is a Hausdorff space and 0
is the sheaf of continuous real-valued functions on Y, then any section of 0
over Z can be extended to a section of 0 over X. In the other words, the
canonical map (restriction) F(X, 0) --+ F(Z, 0) is surjective.
                                                                              5. Sheaves   37


    The same is true if X is a smooth (COO) manifold and 0 is the sheaf of
Coo-functions on X.
    The degree of nonuniqueness is illustrated already by the fact that for
any closed Z' eX - Z and for any continuous (resp. smooth) function on Z'
the extension can be chosen in such a way that its restriction on Z' would
coincide with this function. (Indeed, the restriction r(X, 0) --> r(Z u Z', 0)
is again surjective.)
    If 7r : E --> X is a vector bundle, then the same extend ability properties
hold for the sheaf of germs of sections of 7r.
    c) Now let X be a connected complex analytic manifold, Z C X a closed
subset, 0 the sheaf of holomorphic functions. Then r(X,O) --> r(Z,O) is
injective because if a function on Z that can be extended to X at all, it can
be extended uniquely: the difference of two extensions is zero in some neigh-
bourhood of Z, hence vanishes on X (recall that a section of 0 over Z is not
an analytic function on Z, but an analytic function on some neighbourhood
of Z).
    A lot of functions are nonextendablej for example, if Z is a point and X
is compact, then only the germs of constant functions can be extended to X.
    Rigidity properties of sheaves of holomorphic functions (and of sections
of holomorphic bundles) are close to those of locally constant sheaves.

§6. The Exact Sequence
1. Homology as a Function in Two Variables

In Sect. 1.4 we have defined groups Hn(H, A) and Hn(x, B), where X is a
simplicial set, A and B are coefficient systems. In some simple cases these
groups can be computed directly. But the main technique consists in the
study of the behavior of these groups under the change of X or the change
of A.
   We shall call A (or B) an abelian variable and X a nonabelian variable. In
the next chapter we shall try to show that any (co ) homology theory contains
(explicitly or implicitly) these two arguments.
   In this section we fix X and study the dependence of homology and co-
homology on coefficients. The main tool here is the theorem about the exact
sequence.

2. Exact Sequences

An exact sequence of abelian groups is a complex C· with all cohomology
groups vanishing (for chain complexes the definition is the same). This means
that Kerd n = Imdn - l for all n.
   An exact triple (or a short exact sequence) of abelian groups is an exact
sequence of the form

            ... -+0 -+0 -+ A ~ B ~ C -+0 -+0 -+ ...
     Usually such a sequence is written an

                              o -+A ~ B ~ C -+0.                       (1.12)
To give such a triple is the same as to give an abelian group B and its
subgroup A. The homomorphism theorem says that
                     Imi = Kerp      implies   C = BjA.
3. Theorem. Let X be a simplicial set. Any exact triple of abelian groups
(1.12) canonically determines a cohomology exact sequence
         0-+ HO(X, A) -+ HO(X, B) -+ HO(X, C) -+ Hl(X, A) -+
         -+ Hl(X,B) -+ ... -+ Hn(X,A) -+ Hn(X,B) -+        (1.13)
         -+ Hn(x, C) -+ Hn+l(x, A) -+ ...
                                                   6. The Exact Sequence        41


and a -similar homology sequence

       ... - t   Hn(X,A) - t Hn(X,B) - t Hn(X,C) - t H n - 1 (X, A) - t
                                                                     (Ll4)
       . . . - t H 1 (X, C) - t Ho(X, A) - t Ho(X,B) - t Ho(X, C) - t 0



4. Remarks

a) This theorem has an almost obvious special case. If the exact triple (1.12)
splits, i.e., has the form

                     o - t A ~ B = A EB C ~ C - t 0,
                           i(a) = (a, O),p(a, c) = c,
then the sequence (1.13) can be decomposed into split exact triples of the
form
             o - t Hn(x, A) - t Hn(x, B) =
                   = Hn(x, A) EB Hn(x, C) - t Hn(x, C) - t o.
The main difference of the general case from this special one the is non-trivial
phenomenon of the "connecting" (or boundary) homomorphisms



    b) Theorem 1.6.3 has an analogue for coefficient systems; it will be stated
later.
    c) The proof of the theorem consists in two steps: the construction of all
homomorphisms in (1.13) and the proof of the exactness. It is more convenient
to perform both steps in a somewhat more general setting. Namely, we show
that the exact triple (1.12) gives rise to the similar exact triple of chains (and
of cochains) and then we prove an analogue of Theorem 1.6.3 for exact triples
of complexes, independent of their origin.

§7. Complexes
1. Where Complexes Come from?

In this section we describe several classes of complexes of either algebraic or
geometric origin. All these complexes can be roughly divided into two groups:
combinatorial complexes and differential geometry complexes. An example
of a complex from the first group is the chain complex of a simplicial set,
that from the second group is the de Rham complex. We describe also some
algebraic operations with complexes.
46     I. Simplicial Sets


2. Definition. A simplicial abelian group is a simplicial set A = (An), n =
0,1, ... (see Definition 1.2.1) such that each An is an abelian group and all
A(f) : An -+ Am are group homomorphisms.

    In Sect. 1.2 we constructed groups of chains Gn(X, A) for any simplicial
set X and any homology coefficient system A on it. For any nondecreasing
f: [m]-+ [n] we define a homomorphism

            Gn(f)   C~n a(x)x) x~n A(f, x) (a(x)) X(f)(x).

comes a simplicial abelian group.
   Similarly to formula (1.9) in Sect. 1.4, one can construct a complex for
any simplicial abelian group as follows.

B(f) : Bm -+ Bn, one for each nondecreasing map f : [m] -+ [n] satisfying
the conditions B(id) = id, B(f 0 g) = B(f) 0 B(g).

- Lemma 1.7.3

<p = {<p"o ... "'" E F(U"o n ... n U",,J}, one for any (m + I)-tuple of indices

5. Complex of Singular Chains

Let Y be a topological space, Xn the set of its singular n-simplices (see 1.2.4),
and Cn(X, A) a simplicial abelian group of its chains with coefficients in an
abelian group A. Construct the complex as in 1.7.3. Its homology is called
singular homology of Y with coefficient in A and is denoted H~ing(y, A).
Similarly one defines singular cohomology.

6. Homology and Cohomology of Groups

Let G be a group, A a left G-module. In 1.4.9 c) we used A to construct a
homological and a co homological coefficient system on the simplicial set BG.
    Homology and cohomology of BG with these coefficient systems are called
respectively homology and cohomology of G with coefficients in A. They are
denoted Hn(G, A) and Hn(G, A) respectively.

7. The de Rham Complex

Let X be a Coo-manifold, C(X) be the ring of Coo-functions on X, and
{]i(X) the C(X)-module of i-forms on X, so that {]O(X) = C(X). The
exterior differential d : {]k(X) --+ {]k+l(X) is defined in local coordinates
(xl, ... , xn) by the formula




                                        - ,
                                . ) III -
               I -- (.Zl, ... , Zk,       k dx I -- dx il /\ ... /\ dx ik .
It is completely determined by the following properties:
a) d(w k /\wl) = dw k /\wl + (_l)kw k /\dwl,w k E {]k(X),wl E {]1(X);
b) d 2 =O;
c) d: C(X) --+ {]l(X) associates to each function its differential.
    Cohomology groups of the complex {]e(x) is called the de Rham coho-
mology groups of X and are denoted sometimes HDR(X).
    This construction allows several important modifications. Associating to
an open set U C X the linear space {]k(U) and defining the restrictions
{]k(U) --+ {]k(V) for V c U in a natural way we get a sheaf that is denoted
by {]k. Exterior differentials commute with restrictions, so that we get a
complex of sheaves {]e.
    To get another modification we can consider a complex analytic manifold
and holomorphic differential forms on it.

9. Homotopic Mappings of Complexes

important technical tool to compute homology: changes of complexes that
preserve homology. More generally, let f., g. : B. --+ C. be two morphisms
of chain complexes, H. (f), H. (g) be corresponding morphisms of homologies
as in 1.6.5. Morphisms f. and G. are said to be homotopic if there exists a
sequence of group homomorphisms k = (kn ), kn : Bn --+ Cn+1, such that

§ 1. The Language of Categories and Functors
1. Definition. A category C consists of the following data:
   a) A set of Ob C whose elements are called objects of C.
   b) A collection of sets Hom(X, Y), one for each ordered pair of objects
X, Y E ObC, whose elements are called morphisms (from X to Y); they are
denoted cp : Y --4 Y.
   c) A collection of mappings
                    Hom(X, Y) x Hom(Y, Z) ~ Hom(X, Z),
one for each ordered triple of objects X, Y, Z E ObC. Any mapping in this
collection associates to a pair cp : X --4 Y, 'ljJ : Y --4 Z a morphism from X
to Z, denoted 'ljJ 0 cp or 'ljJcp : X --4 Z, and called the composition or product
of cp and'ljJ.
     This data should satisfy the following conditions:
    A) Any morphism cp uniquely determines X, Y E ObC such that cp E
Hom(X, Y). In other words, the sets Hom(X, Y) are pairwise disjoint.
    B) For any X E ObC there exists the identity morphism idx : X --4 X
of X; it is determined uniquely by the conditions id x 0 cp = cp, 'ljJ 0 id x = 'ljJ
wherever these compositions are defined.
    C) The composition of morphisms is associative:

                                   (~'ljJ)cp = ~('ljJcp)

for any cp : X --4 Y, 'ljJ : Y --4 Z, ~ : Z --4 U.


2. About Notations

We shall sometimes write X E C instead of X E ObC, and Homc(X, Y) or
C(X, Y) instead of Hom(X, Y). A morphism cp E Hom(X, Y) may sometimes
be called an arrow starting at X and ending at Y. The set UX,YEC Hom(X, Y)
is denoted Mor(C).
    The definition of a category include manipulations only with morphisms,
not with objects. The simplest concrete statement in a category is that some
composition of morphisms is equal to some other composition, say 'ljJcp = cpl'ljJ'.
58     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


Instead of speaking about this identity, it is often more convenient to say that
the diagram

                                            rp
                           x                              y




                           u
                                            rp'
                                                           I~
                                                           z

is commutative.

3. The Category of Sets and Categories of Sets with a Structure

A mathematician spends most of his working time in the category Set of "all"
sets and "all" maps of sets. The composition of morphisms is the composition
of map; the identity morphisms are identity maps. In standard axiomatic set
theory (say, Zermelo-Frankel) the collection of all sets forms a class, but
not a set, so some operations with sets are forbidden. So the above naive
definition of Set forbids some categorical constructions we will consider later.
The standard way of dealing with the situation is to introduce the Universe,
i.e., a large set of sets which is closed under all necessary operations, and to
consider only the sets belonging to the Universe. Later in this book we will
always assume, whenever necessary, that all the required hygiene regulations
are obeyed.
     An important class of categories is formed by categories whose objects
are sets with some additional structure and whose morphisms are maps re-
specting this structure. Here are some examples:
- Top, the category of topological spaces with continuous maps between
  them;
- Diff, the category of COO-manifolds and Coo-maps;
- Ab, the category of abelian groups and homomorphisms;
- A-mod, the category of left modules over some fixed ring A;
- Gr, the category of groups and homomorphisms.

Now we introduce the second most important notion of category theory.
6. Definition. A functor F from a category C to a category V (notation:
F : C ~ V) consists of the following data:
a) A mapping ObC ~ ObV: X ~ F(X)
b) A mapping MorC ~ MorV : <p ~ F(<p) such that for <p E Homc(X, Y)
   we have F(<p) E HOffiov(F(x), F(Y)).
This data should should satisfy the following conditions: F( <p'ljJ) = F( 'ljJ) for
any <p, 'ljJ E Mor C for which <p'ljJ is defined. In particular, F( idx ) = idF(x).

8. Remarks

The notion of a functor appears as a generalization of what was once called
a "natural construction", and the examples from II.1.7 show several such
constructions. In II.l.lO we give other examples of functors. A very special
role is played by the functor (or in fact the collection of functors) hx : C ->
Set, X E ObC
          hx(Y)          Homc(X,Y)
       hx(J)(cp)         f 0 cp, where f : Y -> Y', cp E Homc(X, Y).
We will return to this functor later in Sect. II.3.
    b) The functors F : C -> V defined in ILL 7 are sometimes called co-
variant functors. There exist also contravariant functors; such a functor G is
defined as a pair of mappings G : ObC -> ObV, MorC -> MorV such that
G(cp) E Homv(G(Y), G(X)) for cp E Homc(X, Y), and G(CPV;) = G(cp)G(V;).
However, today's fashion is to do this "inversion of arrows" in the initial
category C.
    Formally, define the dual category Co as follows: ObCo = ObC (but X E
ObC considered as an object of Co will be denoted XO); Homco(XO, YO) =
Homc(Y, X) (to cp : Y -> X we associate cpo : XO -> YO); finally V;O 0 cpo =
(CPV;)O, idxo = (idx)o.
62      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


    Now a "contravariant" functor G : C --> D is defined as a (covariant) func-
tor G : Co --> D. An example: view Home as a functor of its first argument,
h'x : Co --> Set:
            h'x(Y)        Homc(Y,X)
         h'x (J) (t.p)    t.p ci f where   f: Y' --> Y, t.p E Homc(Y, X).

The notation Llo Set, introduced in II.1.4b, reminds us that any simplicial set
X. can be considered as a functor X. : Llo --> Set:
                         X.([nJ) = X n ,   X.(J) = X(J).
    c) It would be natural to consider the functor Homc as a functor of two
variables. Instead of formally introducing the corresponding definition, we
nowadays use the notion of the direct product of categories.
    Let, for example, C and C' be two categories. Define

            Ob(C xC') = ObC x ObC',
            Homcxc ' ((X, X'), (Y, Y')) = Homc(X, Y) x Homc/(X', Y'),
            (t.p, t.p') 0 ('l/J, 'l/J') = (t.p 0 t.p', 'l/J 0 'l/J')
            id(x,x /) = (idx , idx / ) .

It is easy to check that C x C' is a category. In a similar way one can define the
product of any indexed family of categories. A functor in several variables is,
by definition, a functor on the corresponding product of categories. We have
already seen two examples:

                             Homc : CO x C ~ Set
and bisimplicial sets X •• (see 1.3.3)
                             X •• : Llo x Llo ~ Set.

     d) By composing (set-theoretically) two functors C ~ D -S E we get
the functor C ~ E. The identity map idc : ObC --> ObC, MorC --> MorC
is a functor. So we can consider any set of categories as a new category with
functors as new morphisms.

9. More Examples of Functors

a) Any presheaf of abelian groups on a topological space X is a functor

                                F: Topx ~Ab

(the category Topx was defined in ILLS).
    b) Let 1 be a partially ordered set, C(1) be the corresponding category (see
ILLS). A functor G : C(1) --> Ab is a family of abelian groups {G(i)li E 1}
                              1. The Language of Categories and Functors       63


and of maps gij : G i - . G j, one for each ordered pair i :S j; these maps should
satisfy the conditions gjkgij = gik for i :S j :S k, gii = id ci . Such families
usually appear as raw material for inductive and/or projective limits.
    c) Forgetful Functors. One can obtain a lot of functors in the following
way: just forget one (or several) structures on an object of the initial category.
Among the examples of these functors are the "set of elements" functors
                            Top, Diff, Ab, Gr ~ Set,
as well as the functors
                          Diff ~ Top, A-mod ~ Ab.
    The last definition of this section is that of a morphism of functors (they
are called "natural transformation of natural constructions").
10. Definition. Let F, G be two functors from C to V. A morphism of func-
tors from F to G (notation f : F -. G) is a family of morphisms in V:
                             f(X) : F(G) ~ G(X),
 one for each X E Ob C, satisfying the following condition: for any morphism
'P : X -. Y in C the diagram

                                      f(X)
                          F(X)                     G(X)


                    F(~) j                            jG(~)
                                      f(Y)
                       G(X)                        G(Y)

is commutative.
    The composition of morphism of functors, as well as the identity morphism
of functors, are defined in the obvious way. As a result, functors from C to V
form a category, which is usually denoted Funct(C, V).

12. Several Definitions

Finally, we give some more definitions that will be constantly used throughout
this book.
    A category C is called a subcategory of a category V if
a) ObC c ObV;
b) Home (X, Y) c Homv(X, Y) for any X, Y E ObC;
c) The composition ofmorphisms in C coincides with their composition in V,
   for X E Ob C the identity morphism idx in C coincides with the identity
   morphism idx in V.
  A subcategory C is said to be full if Homc(X, Y) = Homv(X, Y) for any
X,Y E ObC.
  A functor f : C -+ V is said to be faithful if for any X, Y E ObC the map

                      F: Homc(X, Y) ~ Homv(FX,FY)

is injective, and full if this map is surjective.
                                  1. The Language of Categories and Functors   65


    In particular, an embedding of a complete subcategory is a fully faithful
functor. One can prove that, conversely, any fully faithful functor can be
obtained in such a way (see Ex. 2.1).
    An object a of a category C is said to be an initial object if Homc(a, X) is
an one-element set for any X E Ob C. Similarly, an object W is said to be a final
object in C if Homc(X,w) is an one-element set for any X E abC. Clearly,
both an initial and a final object (if they exist) are determined uniquely up
to an isomorphism.
13. Example. In the category Set the empty set is the initial object and
anyone-element set is a final object.
