§2. Categories and Structures,
Equivalence of Categories

1. Isomorphisms

A significant part of all mathematical problems is what is called classification
problems: classification of simple groups, of singularities, etc. In the next
two sections we will discuss categorial aspects of these problems. Usually
classification means classification up to an (or the) isomorphism.

2. Definition. a) A morphism cp : X ~ Y in a category C is said to be an
isomorphism if there exists a morphism 'Ij; : Y ~ X such that cp 0 'Ij; = idy,
'Ij; 0 cp = idx.
       b) Objects X, Y in a category C are said to be isomorphic if there exists
at least one isomorphism between them.
       The reader can easily check that the relation "to be isomorphic" is an
equivalence relation on ObC. Two morphisms 'Ij; : X ~ Y and 'Ij; : Y ~
X with the properties indicated in the definition above are called mutually
inverse. The inverse of a given isomorphism is determined uniquely.
       Applying the above notion to the category Funet (C, V) of functors (see
II.l.IO) we obtain the important notion of an isomorphism of two functors
F : C ~ V and G : C ~ V is a morphism of functors: it is a morphism of
functors cp : F ~ G for which there exists the inverse morphism 'Ij; : G ~ F,
'lj;cp = id F , cp'lj; = ide.
       The reader can easily check that the existence of the inverse morphism
'Ij; : G ~ F in this definition can be replaced by a more natural condition:
for any X E ObC the morphism cp(X) : F(X) ~ G(X) is an isomorphism.
       A meaningful example of an isomorphism of functors is "double dualiza-
tion" as described below.

3. Example. Let k be a field and Vectk the category of linear spaces over
k with linear mappings as morphisms. Let Vect~ be the full subcategory of
70      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


Vectk formed by the finite-dimensional vector spaces. Let * be the dualization
functor
                           * : Vectk ------> (Vectkt;
it is defined on objects by

         *(L) = Homvectk(L,k) = the space of linear functionals onL
and transforms a morphism cp : L ~ M to

                      *(cp) : Hom(M, k)             ------>     Hom(L, k)
                          (f : M ------> k)         f---+       (f 0 cp : L ------> k).
Instead of writing *(L) and *(cp) we will write, as usual, L * and cp*. The fact
that * is a contravariant functor is expressed by the important formula
                                         (cp 0 'ljJ)* = 'ljJ* 0 cp*.

We also have *(Vectt) C (Vectt).
   Now we note, that * can be considered also as a functor from (Vect)O to
Vectk,
                         * : (Vectkt ------> Vectk'
(A very cautious reader may feel that the distinction between these two
functors should be reflected in the notation). The composition of these two
dualizations gives the functor

                                        ** : Vectk ------> Vectk'
This functor is isomorphic to the identity functor when restricted to the
subcategory Vectt. More explicitly, we have a morphism of functors

                                         Id Vectk = Id ------> **
given by
      L ------> L ** , l   1---+   (l    considered as a functional on the space of
                                   functional on              L).
It is well known that L ~ L ** is an isomorphism for a finite-dimensional
L, so that the morphism Id ~ ** is an isomorphism on Vectt. However, it
definitely ceases to be an isomorphism on the whole of Vectk' Indeed, if L
is a space with a count ably infinite basis, then L *, and so also L **, have
uncountable bases.

4. A Useless Notion: Isomorphism of Categories

In an attempt to apply the definition from II.2.2 to the category of categories
(see ILL 7 d), one would obtain the following. An isomorphism between cate-
gories C and V is given by a pair of functors F : C ~ V, G : V ~ C, such that
                  2. Categories and Structures, Equivalence of Categories     71


FG = Id1), GF = Ide. Contrary to expectations, this notion appears to be
more or less useless, the main reason being that neither of the requirements
FG = Id1), GF = Ide is realistic. Whenever we apply two natural construc-
tions to an object, the most we can ask for is to get a new object which is
canonically isomorphic to the old one; it would be too much to hope for the
new object to be identical to the old one. An illuminating example is the
double dualization.
    A much more useful notion is given by the following definition.

5. Definition. a) A functor F : C ----; V is said to be an equivalence of
categories if there exists a functor G : V ----; C such that the functor GF is
isomorphic to Ide, and the functor FG is isomorphic to Id1).
    b) Categories C and V are said to be equivalent if there exists an equiva-
lence F : C ----; V.
    In the situation of a), one sometimes says that the functor G is quasi-
inverse to F.

6. Example. Let Vect k be the full subcategory of Vectk consisting of all n-
dimensional vector spaces over k, and let V;: be the category with one object
k n and all linear mappings of kn to itself as morphisms. There exist an obvious
inclusion functor VI: ----; Vect k, and it is an equivalence of categories.

   This example displays the following typical features:

a) equivalent categories have "the same" isomorphism classes of objects and
   "the same" morphisms between these classes.
b) a quasi-inverse functor is usually not unique, and to define such a functor
   one often must use the axiom of choice (in our example one must choose
   a basis in every n-dimensional vector space).
   In proving that a given functor is an equivalence of categories the following
result is sometimes useful.

7. Theorem. F: C ----; V is an equivalence of categories if and only if
a) F is a fully faithful functor.
b) Any object Y E Ob V is isomorphic to an object of the form F(X) for
   some X E ObC.

Proof. (=». Let F be an equivalence of categories and G           V ----; C be a
quasi-inverse functor. Let

                      f(X)       GFX --+ X,X E ObC,
                      g(Y)       FGY --+ Y, Y E Ob V.

be isomorphisms of functors f : GF ----; Ide, g : FG ----; Id1). First note that
an object Y E ObV is isomorphic to the object FX, where X = GY E ObC;
this proves b).
72      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory

     Next, for any cp E Hom(X, X') the diagram

                                      g(X)


                           I
                        GFX                           X

                  GF('P)
                                      g(X')
                        GFX                          X'

is commutative. Therefore, cp can be reconstructed from F( cp) by the formula

                        cp = g(X') 0 GF(cp) 0 (g(X))-l                      (II.2)
so that F is a faithful functor. Similarly, G is a faithful functor. To prove that
F is fully faithful, consider an arbitrary morphism 'l/J E Homc(F X, F X'), and
set
                  cp = g(X') 0 G('l/J) 0 (g(X))-l E Hom(X, X').
Then (see (II.2)) cp = g(X') oGF(cp) o(g(X))-t, and G('l/J) = GF(cp) because
g(X), g(X') are isomorphisms. Since G is a faithful functor, 'l/J = G(cp), so
that F is fully faithful.
   (<=). Assume a) and b) are satisfied. For any Y E ObC we fix X y E ObC
and an isomorphism g(X) : FXy ...... Y. To construct a functor G : V ...... C
which is quasi-inverse to F, we set GY = X y for Y E ObV. Next define G
on morphisms 'l/J E Homv(Y, Y') by
       G..p = g(y)-l o'l/J 0 g(Y) E Hom(FGY, FGY') = Hom(GY, GY')
(the last equality follows from a)). It is easy to check that G is a functor and
9 = {g(Y)} : FG ...... Idv is an isomorphism of functors. Finally, g(FX) :
FGFX ...... FX is an isomorphism for any X E ObC. Therefore, a) implies
that g(FX) = F(J(X)) for a unique isomorphism f(X) : GFX ...... X. An
easy calculation shows that f : {f (X)} : GF ...... Id C is an isomorphism of
functors. Therefore G is quasi-inverse to F.                                   0

    A meaningful theorem about an equivalence of two categories can often be
interpreted as giving two complementary descriptions of mathematical object.
We will try to illustrate this using as examples several small mathematical
theories that can be thought of as equivalence theorems.

8. Galois Theory

Let k be a field; for simplicity assume that char k = O. Denote by G the Galois
group of the algebraic closure k/ k with the Krull topology. A substantial part
of Galois theory can be summarized as follows:
                   2. Categories and Structures, Equivalence of Categories         73


   The dual category of commutative semi-simple k-algebras (k-Alg)O is
equivalent to the category of finite topological G-sets G-Set.
    Let us give some explanations.
    a) A finite-dimensional commutative k-algebra L is semi-simple if it con-
tains no non-zero nilpotent elements. Any such algebra is isomorphic to a
direct sum of finite algebraic extensions of the field k, L c:::' ffik
    b) The Krull topology on G is defined as follows: a neighbourhood basis of
the identity element in G consists of subgroups G K = {g E G I \:Ix E K, gx =
x}, where K runs over all finite extensions of k inside k.
    c) A functor F : G-Set ----> (k-Alg)O that establishes the equivalence is
constructed as follows:
         F(S) = Mapa(S, k) = the algebra of G-invariant k-valued
                             functions on S such that f(g8) =
                                    g(1(s)) for all s E G, g E G.
The action of the functor F on morphisms is the natural one: for cp : T ----> S
the morphism F(cp) : F(S) ----> F(T) transforms a function f on S to the
function f 0 cp on T. The algebra F(S) consists of k-valued functions, and,
therefore, does not contain nilpotent elements. Its dimension over k is finite
because the intersection H of stabilizers of all the points of H = {g E G I
gs = s, \:Is E S}, is open in G, so that the field kH has finite degree over k,
and the values of invariant function on S lie in this field.
    It is clear that F transforms disjoint unions to direct sums. If S is an
irreducible G-Set (Le., with one G-orbit), then the k-algebra F(S) is isomor-
phic to the field kH , where H is the stabilizer of some point s E S. This
implies that any object of k-Alg is isomorphic to an object of the form F(S).
We won't explain the full faithfulness of the functor F; this would mean
reproducing a substantial part of Galois theory. We will only say that the
quasi-inverse functor F' : (k-Alg)O ----> G-Set is given on objects by
                             F'(L) = Homk-Alg(L, k)
with the action of an element 9 : k ----> k being the composition L ----> k !!." k- .
The definition of F' on morphisms also uses this composition.

10. Commutative Banach Algebras

A commutative Banach algebra is a commutative C-algebra A with the unit
1 and with the norm Ilxll making A a complete Banach space; the product
in A is required to be continuous with respect to this norm. An involution
in A is an anti-linear homomorphism * : A ~ A with (X*)* = x, Ilxll
Ilx*ll, Ilxx*11 = IIxl1 2 for any x E A. Define the category Ban as follows:
                 2. Categories and Structures, Equivalence of Categories         75


               Ob Ban     = {commutative Banach algebras with
                              involution} ,

         HomBan(A, A')        {algebras homomorphisms r.p : A --->
                              A' respecting the norm and the
                              involution} .
   On the other hand, denote by Haus the full subcategory of the category
Top consisting of all compact Hausdorff spaces. One of main results in the
theory of commutative Banach algebras can be stated as follows:
    Categories Ban and Haus are equivalent.
                                                 F
   Corresponding quasi-inverse functors Ban ~ Haus can be constructed
                                                 G
as follows:
         G X = {the ring of continuous functions on X with the norm
                Ilfll = max

                f(x)}.
    To define F, we introduce the maximal ideal space for a commutative
Banach algebra A. The ideal meA is said to be maximal if it is not contained
in any other proper ideal in A. Such an m is necessarily closed, and Aim = <C.
Let M(A) be the set of maximal ideals invariant under the involution in A.
Any element a E A determines a complex-valued function fa on M(A) =
fa(m) = {the class of a in Aim = <C}. Endow on M(A) with the weakest
topology such that all functions fa, a E A are continuous. With this topology
M(A) becomes a locally compact Haussdorf space. Define F : Ban ---> Haus
by F(A) = M(A).
    The theory of commutative Banach algebras says that the functors F and
G are quasi-inverse. In particular, the equivalence of functors Id Ban ~ GF
means that each commutative Banach algebra with involution is isomorphic
to the algebra of continuous functions on the space of its maximal ideals.

11. Pontryagin Duality

Let C be the following category:
                         {commutative locally compact groups}
                         {continuous group homomorphisms        Al ---> A 2 }.
   The Pontryagin duality theorem can be formulated as follows:
   The category C is equivalent to the dual category Co.
   The equivalence is established via the theory of characters. Let S be the
group of complex numbers with moduls 1 (under multiplication). A char-
acter of A is an element X E Homc(A, S), i.e., continuous homomorphism
76     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


of A to S. Pointwise multiplication makes the set of all characters A into a
group denoted by A. Supply A with the topology of uniform convergence on
compact subsets. Any continuous homomorphism cp : Al -+ A2 determined a
continuous homomorphism cp : A2 -+ Al by the formula
                   CP(X)(Xl) = X (cp(Xl)) , Xl E Al, X E A2 .
SO we obtain the functor F : C -+ Co:
                           FA = (Ar,F(cp) = (cpr·
Let FO : Co -+ C be the dual functor, i.e.
                     FO(AO) = A,Ao E Obco,A E ObC.
    A somewhat more precise form of the duality theorem says that the func-
tors F : C -+ Co, FO : Co -+ C are quasi-inverse.

12. Final Remarks
The examples in II.2.8-II.2.11 show several typical patterns of category the-
ory. The only way to learn patterns is to consider a lot of examples; however,
we will try to formulate several rules.
    a) A good classification theorem should describe not only objects, but
also morphisms of these objects. From this point of view the complete theory
of semisimple Lie algebras should contain not only Cartan classification (in
terms of root systems), but also the theory of finite-dimensional represen-
tations of these algebras (H. Weyl's highest weight theory), as well as some
other problems of Lie algebras theory.
    b) The interrelations between algebra and geometry are given by a system
of functors of the type
               spaces   +-----+   rings of functions
               spaces   +-----+   cohomology rings
              sheaves   +-----+   modules over rings of functions.
    Good categorical properties of such functors (e.g. equivalence) are so im-
portant that to save them one is often forced to change old structures or to
introduce new ones. This is how affine schemes, nuclear vector spaces, rigid
analytic spaces, and objects of derived categories appeared in mathematics.
    c) The formal inversion of arrows furnished in the definition of dual cat-
egory in concrete examples often produce duality theorems (when categories
coincide or are close to each other), or some relations of the type geometry
vs algebra (when categories are not alike, e.g., rings and their spectra).

determines an equivalence of the category C with a full subcategory in V.

2. Quotient Categories. Let C be a category and let for any pair of objects
X, Y of C an equivalence relation rv=rvx,Y in Homc(X, Y) be given. Then
there exists a category V = C/ rv and a functor Q : C -+ V such that
(i) if f rv f' in C then Qf = Qf' in V;
(ii) if F : C -+ V'is a functor such that F f = F f' for any f rv f' then there
     exists a unique functor G : V -+ V' such that F = G 0 Q.
     Sketch of the Proof Let us assume first that the family of equivalence
relations rv X,Y satisfies the following condition: f rv x, y f' implies afb rvu, V
af'b for any a : Y -+ V, b : U -+ X. Then define V = C/ rv as follows:
Ob V = ObC, Hom1) (X, Y) = Home (X, Y)/ rv with the natural composition,

3. Morita Equivalence. a) An object X of a category C is said to be a
generator if h'x : Y f--+ Homc(X, Y) is a faithful functor from C to Set (Le.,
for any Y1 , Y 2 E 0 b C the map h'x : Home (Yl, Y 2 ) -+ Homset (h'x Y1 , H!x Y 2 ) is
an embedding).
    b) Morita's theorem says that for two rings A and B the following condi-
tions are equivalent:
(i) Categories A-mod and B-mod are equivalent.
(ii) Categories mod-A and mod-B are equivalent.
(iii) There exist a finitely generated projective generator P of mod-A and a
      ring isomorphism B S:! EndAP.
    Proof of (iii) => (ii): an equivalence of mod-A and mod-B is given by the
functor h p : X f--+ HomA(P, X), and the inverse equivalence is given by the
functor hp. : Y f--+ HomB(P*, Y), where P* = HomA(P, A).
    Rings A and B are said to be Morita equivalent if they satisfy the equiv-
alent conditions (i)-(iii). Example: matrix rings Mn(k) (k is a fixed field) for
different n are Morita equivalent.
    c) A property T of a ring A is said to be Morita invariant if for any two
Morita equivalent rings it is always either satisfied or not satisfied for both of
them. The following properties of a ring A are Morita invariant: A is simple;
A is semisimple; A is finite; each right ideal in A is projective; each right
ideal in A is injective. The following properties of a ring A are not Morita
invariant: A does not have zero divisors; A is a field; A is commutative; each
projective A-module is free.
    d) The center Ze of a category C is the ring of all morphisms of the
identity functor Ide: C -+ C. Prove that the center of the category mod-A is
isomorphic to the center of the ring A. Therefore two commutative rings are
Morita equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic.

§3. Structures and Categories. Representable Functors

1. What Should We Do?

We have to learn how to treat an object of a category as if this object were
a set endowed with some structure. We have to be able to define the direct
product or the limit of a projective system of objects, to define what one
would call a group object, and so on. In classical constructions we use that
objects are composed of elements (points), and that these points can be pro-
cessed in various manners: one can form pairs or sequences, choose elements
with a given property, etc.
    To work with an abstract category C we must either describe set-theoretic
constructions in the diagram language and transfer the result to C, or else
we must find substitutes in the category language for the notions of points
or elements.
    We describe the second approach first. It is based on the simple but
useful remark that any set X in the category Set can be identified with the
set Homset(e, X), where e is a one-point set. In an arbitrary category C an
analogue of e does not necessarily exist. However, by considering instead
Homc(X, Y) for all Y simultaneously, we can recover complete information
about the object X (up to an isomorphism). In this language morphisms
'P: Y ----> X are sometimes called Y-points of an object X, and Home (X, Y)
is denoted X (Y).
    To be more precise we introduce the category of functors (see 11.1.10)
                             C= Funct(CO, Set)
and consider the functor hx : Co ----> Set defined by hx(YO) = Home (X, Y)
(see 11.1.7 b) as an object of C.
2. Definition. A functor F E ObC is said to be representable if it is iso-
morphic to a functor of the form hx for some X E ObC one says also that
the object X represents the functor F.

    Let 'P : Xl ----> X 2 be a morphism in C. We associate to 'P the morphism
of functors h", : hXl ----> hX2 defined by the property that



for Y E ObC maps a morphism () E Home(Y,X I ) = hXl(Y) to the composi-
tion 'P 0 () E Homc(Y, X 2 ) = hX2 (Y). It is clear that h",..p = h",h..p.

3. Theorem. With the notation above the map 'P I----> 9", yields an isomor-
phism of sets
                  Homc(X, Y) ~ Homc(hx, hy).
                    3. Structures and Categories. Representable FUnctors       79


Moreover, this map is an isomorphism of the two functors CO xC ---+ Set (both
of which are functors in variables X and Y). Therefore, the functor h : C ---+ C
defined by h(X) = hx, h(<p) = hcp, yields an equivalence of C with the full
subcategory of C formed by representable functors.

object in C is defined uniquely up to a unique isomorphism.

6. Arrow Inversion

Given any categorial construction, we can create the dual construction by
making the initial construction in the dual category Co and interpreting the
result in C; in other words, we invert all arrows in the original construction.
In such a way one can obtain the amalgamated sum (or coproduct, cocarlesian
square) X 11 Y corresponding to a diagram of the form X ~ S ~ Y:
               S

13. Cartier Duality

Let us remark that a structure of commutative algebra on an abelian group
A can be defined by giving the following homomorphisms of abelian groups:

                       fl : A 0 A - + A, fl(a 0 b) = ab,
                         E: Z - + A, c:(m) = m· IA.

with the commutativity and associativity axioms for fl and the identity axiom
for E. Therefore, the corresponding structure of commutative bialgebra is
described by homomorphisms of abelian groups

                       A0A~             A     ~A0A,
                                                                           (II.4)


together with various axioms. One part of the axioms is contained in II.l.I;
another part can be obtained from it by inverting the arrows and substituting
fl and E for p, and c:; an additional axiom requires p, : A - A 0 A to be a
homomorphism of algebras with the multiplications fl and fl0 fl respectively.
    Now let A be a free abelian group of finite type and A* = Homz(A, Z).
Identifying (A 0 A)* with A* 0 A* and Z* with Z, we obtain a bialgebra
structure on A* by dualizing the diagrams in (II.4):

                     A* 0A* ~           A*    £     A* 0A*,
                                              g*
                            Z--=-=--'   A*    -+Z,
                                         ,*
                                A*      -+    A*.

  The bialgebra axioms for A * trivially follow from those for A.
  The group scheme X* = Spec A * is called the Cartier dual to the com-
mutative group scheme X = Spec A.

14. Relative Group Schemes

One can relativize all the discussion in II.3.11-II.3.13, considering algebras
A over a fixed commutative ring K. The only thing we must do is to replace
Z by K, and to assume that all mappings are homomorphisms of K-modules
and all tensor products are taken over K.


15. Limits

The notion of a functor gives us an important example of a universal con-
struction in an arbitrary category.

17. The Universal Property of the Limit
Any functor F : .:1 -+ C is determined by the set of objects F(j) = Xj E ObC
(one for every j E ObC) and the set of morphisms f(<p) : Xj -+ Xi' in C (one
for every morphism <p : j -+ j' in .:1).
    Assume that the limit X = limF exists. Take Y = X in (11.5). To the
                                   +-
identity morphism id x : X -+ X there corresponds the morphism of functors
f : LlX -+ F, that is, the set of morphisms f(j) : X -+ Xj in C (one for
every j E Ob.:1) satisfying the conditions
                F(<p)f(j) = f(j')       for each <p: j --> j'        in.:1.            (II.6)
Next, an arbitrary morphism of functors g : LlY -+ F is a set of morphisms
g(j) : Y -+ Xj in C, j E Ob.:1, satisfying similar conditions:
                      F(<p)g(j) = g(j')       for any <p: j --> j'.                     (II.7)
                     3. Structures and Categories. Representable FUnctors         87


     Equation (II.5) shows that the definition of lim F can be given in the form
                                                     <-
of the following universality condition:
       An object X E Ob C is the projective limit of the functor F : .1 ---> C
in the category C if there is a family of morphisms f(j) : X ---> Xj = F(j)
in C (one for every j E Ob.J) satisfying (II.6) and such that for any family
g(j) : Y ---> Xj, j E Ob.J, satisfying (11.7) there exists a unique morphism
'IjJ : Y ---> X in C with g(j) = f(j) 0 'IjJ.

21. Corollary. In the category Set all finite limits and co limits exist.
Proof. The final object in Set is a set consisting of one element, the direct
product in Set is the usual direct product of sets, and the equalizer of two
mappings 'IjJ,'IjJ' : Xo --> Xl is the set X = {x E Xo,'IjJ(x) = 1/J'(x)} with the
natural inclusion X '----t Xo (check it!). A similar proof holds for colimits. D

24. Adjoint Morphisms

Let F and G be a pair of adjoint functors, so that we have functorial in X
and Y set isomorphisms

                 a: Homc(X, G(Y)) ---+ Homv(F(X), Y).                 (11.14)
                       3. Structures and Categories. Representable Functors        91


Substituting, as in lI.3.22, X = G(Y) we get a morphism

                                O"y : FG(Y) --+ Y.

On the other hand, substituting Y = F(X) we get a morphism

                         TX = a (idF(x») : X --+ GF(X).

The commutative diagram (1I.13), together with a similar commutative dia-
gram for T, show that {o"y} and {TX} determine functor morphisms

                          a: FG --+ Id v , T : Ide --+ GF.                    (II.15)

These morphisms are called the adjunction morphisms corresponding to the
pair of adjoint functors F, G. One can easily verify that they satisfy the
following condition:
    Compositions

                       F ~ FGF ~ F,G ~ GFG ~ G                                (11.16)

are identity morphisms of functors F and G respectively.
    One can prove that the existence of adjunction morphisms is equivalent
to the adjointness of F and G. More precisely, if F : C -> V and G : V -> C
are two functors such that there exist functor morphisms (II.15) satisfying
(II.16), then F and G are adjoint to each other: the isomorphism (II.12) is
a( u) = ay 0 F(u) and the inverse isomorphism is a- 1 ( v) = G(v) 0 TX.

§4. Category Approach to the Construction
of Geometrical Objects

1. Three Geometrical Categories

We recall the definition of three classes of manifolds: topological (CO), smooth
(COO) and complex analytic (An).
    A manifold of each class is a pair (M, OM) consisting of a topological
space M and a sheaf OM of (partially defined) functions on M. A manifold
of each class above is described using the following scheme.
    a) We describe explicit ely some manifolds from this class (the so called
local models). Next, b) we say that a pair (M, OM) belongs to the given class
94      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


if it is locally isomorphic to a local model, that is, if any point x E M has
a neighbourhood U such that (U, OM /U) is isomorphic to some local model
from the given class.
     Models of CO are pairs (an open set U C ~n, continuous partialy defined
real-valued functions on U).
     Models of Coo are pairs (an open set U C ~n, partially defined Coo real-
valued functions on U).
     Models of An are pairs (an open set U c en, complex-valued partially,
represented functions on U defined in a neighbourhood of any point of the
definition domain by a convergent power series).
     Here a partially defined function is a pair (D(J), f) where D(J) C U is
an open subset, f : D(J) ~ ~ (or q is a function on D(J). The sheaf OM
is called the structure sheaf of a manifold M. Usually M is assumed to be a
Hausdorff space.

2. Atlases

Several decades ago manifolds were usually defined in a slightly different way,
using atlases. An atlas on M is an open covering M = UiE1Ui together with
a coordinate system (z~i), ... , z~i)) on each Ui (n may depend in a connected
component of M) such that the following conditions are satisfied:
    a) The map <Pi : Ui ~ ~n for CO and Coo (resp. <Pi : Ui ~ en for An)
given by <pi(X) = (z~i) (x), ... , z~i))X = ) is an open embedding.
    b) For any pair i, j with nonempty intersections Ui n Uj coordinates z(i)
are continuous (resp. smooth, resp. complex analytic) functions in coordinates
z(j).
    This definition is equivalent to the previous one. To define a sheaf by an
atlas we set f E OM if for any non-empty intersection U n Uj the restriction
of f to Un Uj is a continuous (resp. smooth, resp. complex analytic) function
in local coordinates z(i). To define an atlas by a sheaf one must choose local
models covering M.

3. Morphisms

To make Co, Coo, An categories one must define morphisms. A morphism <I> :
(M,OM) ~ (N,ON) is defined uniquely by a continuous map <P: M ~ N.
However, the existence of structure sheaves poses some restrictions to <P can
appear. Namely, let f E OM(U). Define a function <pe(J) on <p-l(U) C M by
                             <pe(J)(X) = f(<p(x)).
Then <I> is a morphism if and only if <pe(J) E OM (<p-l(U)) for all U, f. The
reader can easily check that if M and N are given by atlases M = UVi,
N = UUj , and <p(Vi) C Uj(i), then the above condition means that the
functions expressing local coordinates on Uj(i) in terms local coordinates on
       4. Category Approach to the Construction of Geometrical Objects            95


Vi belong to the given class. We recommend to verify that this definition
makes Co, COO, An categories.

4. Structure Sheaves Consisting Not of Functions

The language of category theory enables one to introduce geometrical objects
of nonclassical nature: the so-called locally ringed spaces. We describe two
classes of local models that give a useful illustration of features of these
objects.
    a) Super-regions in ]Rmln. Let m, n be two nonnegative integers. A super-
region in ]Rmln is a pair (U, 0u), where U c ]Rm is an open set and for
VcU
         °u(V)         is the ring of formal sums of the form
                            I:     lil .. ' ik ~il ... ~ik , where lil" .ik are
                       l~il < ... <ik~m
                       smooth functions on V, ~j are anticommuting
                       formal variables.
    In other words, 0u =   Aou  (OU~l EB ... EB OU~n) where Ae is the exterior,
or Grassmann, algebra, and Ou is the sheaf of smooth function on U. The
definition of the structure sheaf also includes a Z2-grading: deg Ou = 0,
deg~j = 1.
    b) Affine Schemes. Let A be a commutative ring. Define Spec A to be the
set of all prime ideals in A (see Ex. 1.5.3). Recall that the Zarisky topology
on Spec A is defined as follows: any closed subset is of the form V (I) where
! c A and V(I) = {pI! C p}. In particular, for any element I E A an open
set D(f) is defined by D(f) = Spec A - V(f) = {pil ~ pl. These open sets
form a base; obviously, D(fg) = D(f) n D(g).
    The ring of fractions Af can be defined by Af = A[TJ/(fT - 1). By Ex.
1.5.3, on the topological space X = SpecA there exists a unique sheaf Ox
such that
                              r (D(f), Ox) = Af
and the restriction map r (D(f), Ox) ~ r (D(f g), Ox) is induced by the
map

               A[TfJ/(fTf - 1)       ---+    A[TfgJ/(fgTfg - 1),
               the class of T f      f---t   g. the class of Tfg

(the class of Tf in Af should be thought of as 1/1, therefore the class of Tfg
is 1/lg).
    In both examples sections of the structure sheaf are not functots anymore.
So we can not directly repeat the definition of a morphism from II.4.3, because
a continuous map of spaces does not automatically define a transfer of sections
of structure sheaves. In particular, we can not glue together global spaces from
local ones, because we do not know what an isomorphismis.
96     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


    A natural remedy is to define separately morphisms of spaces and of
sheaves and to impose minimal compatibility conditions. This approach leads
to the category of ringed spaces.

space and OM is a sheaf of rings on M (the structure sheaf).
   b) A morphism of ringed spaces if> : (M, OM) ~ (N, ON) is a pair (cp, e),

        resU2,ul   = rescp-l(U2),cp-l(Ud : CP.(OM)(UZ) ----+ CP.(OM)(U1).
                                                                        e
    It is easy to check that cp. (0 M) is a sheaf of rings on N, and defines a
morphism of sheaves (denoted by the same letter) e: ON - CP.(OM).
    The class of ringed spaces with the above notion of a morphism becomes
a category. We recommend to the reader to define the composition and to
verify the axioms.
    There exist natural constructions of ringed spaces leading to some sheaves
of functions. One of the most important constructions yields the spaces
(M, 'D M ), where M is a manifold of the class Coo or An and 'DM is the
sheaf of a linear differential operator, Le., of maps P : OM ~ OM such that
in the local coordinates (Z1, ... , Zn) they have the form

              P -_ ~ f ( )8     1
                                 1 -     il     in I -           . ).
                                                         (.21,"" 2n
                   L I Z 8z 1 ' Z -     Z1 ... Zn'   -


On the other hand, ringed spaces 11.4.4 are so close to spaces with functions
that there exists an axiomatic definition of a class of ringed spaces, including
both classical manifolds and super-regions and affine spaces.

6. Definition. a) A ringed space (M, OM) is called a locally ringed space
if OM is a sheaf of local rings, i.e., for any x E M the fiber OM, x is a local
ring.
    b) A morphism if> = (cp, e) : (M, OM) - (N, ON) is called a morphism
of locally ringed spaces if for any pair x E M, yEN with y = cp(x) the
corresponding homomorphism ey,x : 0 N,y ~ 0 M,x is a local homomorphism
of rings.
        4. Category Approach to the Construction of Geometrical Objects          97


7. Commentary

a) Recall first of all that a ring A is said to be a local ring if it contains the
 unique maximal ideal mAo In this case mA is a prime ideal and A/mA is a field.
A homomorphism f : A - t B of local rings is called a local homomorphism
if f(mA) C mB·
    Next, the fiber OM, x at a point x E M is the direct limit ofrings OM(U),
where U runs over the set of neighbourhoods of the point x, ordered by
inclusion. Therefore, an element of OM,x is a germ of a section of OM over
x: it is represented by a section over some neighbourhood of x and two such
representatives determine the same stalk if they coincide on some smaller
neighbourhood of x contained in the regions of definitions of both sections.
    b) If M is a manifold of the class Co, Coo, or An, then the unique maximal
ideal mx in the local ring 0 M,x consists of all germ of functions vanishing at
x (the proof follows from the fact that any stalk, nonvanishing at x, is in-
vertible in OM,x). Therefore, such manifolds are locally ringed spaces. Next,
morphisms of manifold, defined in II.4.3, conserve values of functions in the
sense that if cp = cp(x), then e(f)(x) = f(y) for any germs f E ON,y. There-
fore, these morphisms are morphisms of locally ringed spaces.
    Conversely, consider a morphism (cp,e) : (M : OM) - t (N,ON) of, say,
CO-manifolds in the category of locally ringed spaces. The sheaves OM and
ON are sheaves of IR-algebras. We show that if e respects the structure of an
IR-algebra (i.e., e is identical on constant functions), then (cp, e) is a classical
                 e
morphism, i.e., can be recovered from cp as in II.4.5. Indeed, the function
e(f) is determined by its values at all points of Mj next, e(f)(x) = f(y) if
f(y) = 0 (and, as before, y = y(x)); finally, if f(y) = c, then e(f - c)(x) =
(f - c)(y) by locality, and, as e(c) = c, e(f)(x) = f(y) in the general case.
    Let us note that one can construct nontrivial morphisms (nonidentical on
constants) even between "points", i.e., between manifolds (x, C), where x is
a single point. Such morphisms are in one-to-one correspondence with the
algebraic automorphisms of the field C.
    c) Affine schemes (X = Spec A, Ox) also are locally ringed spaces. The
fiber of Ox at a point x corresponding to a prime ideal peA is the ring of
fractions As, where S = A\pj the image ofp in As is the maximal ideal in As.
Every morphism f : A - t B induces a morphism (in the opposite direction)
of locally ringed spaces cp(p) = f- 1 (p),p c B. Any morphism between two
spectra is obtained in such a way.
    d) Now let (M, OM) be a general locally ringed space. Any section of the
structure sheaf f E OM(U) determines a function with variable range:

                 f(x) = f mod mx E OM,x/mx = k(x), x E U.
These values are also conserved by morphisms of locally ringed spaces. More
precisely, a local morphism ey,x : ON,y - t OM,x yields the morphism of fields
iJy,x : k(y) - t k(x) and this morphism transforms the value of f at y to the
98      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


value of 8(f) at x. However, a function is not determined by its values at all
points: for example, nilpotent elements of the structure sheaf take only zero
values.
    e) Super-regions in lR mln ' for n =I=- 0 do not satisfy conditions of definition
II.4.6, because Grassmann algebras are noncommutative. However, one can
easily give a necessary extension of the notion of commutativity.

8. Supercommutativity

Let A = Ao + Al be a Z2-graded ring. This means that the additive group of
A is the direct sum of two subgroups consisting of even (Ao) and odd (Al)
elements, and if f E Ai,g E A j , then fg E Ak, where k = i + j(mod2).
The convenient notation for f E Ai is j = i. Define the supercommutator
[f,g] = fg - (-1)19gf for any pair of homogeneous elements f, 9 E A and
extend it to A by additivity.
    A ring A is said to be supercommutative if [f, g] = 0 for all f, g. In other
words, odd elements anticommute among themselves, and even elements com-
mute with both even and odd elements. Assume that 2 is invertible in A. Then
for any odd f we have 0 = [f, f] = 2f2, so that P = o.
    Bya morphism of Z2-graded (in particular, supercommutative) rings we
mean any ring homomorphism preserving Z2-grading. Definitions of a local
ring and of a local morphism remain the same as in the commutative case.
Now we can give the definition of a superspace that is completely parallel to
the definition II.4.6, and in fact contains it as a special case.
9. Definition. a) A locally ringed superspace is a ringed space (M, OM)
such that OM is a sheaf of supercommutative rings (all restriction maps pre-
serve Z2-degree) and all fibers OM,x are local rings.
    b) A morphism of locally ringed superspaces is a morphism of correspond-
ing ringed spaces such that all By,x 's are local morphisms of Z2-graded rings.

    Using definitions 11.4.6 and II.4.9 we can add to the list of geometrical
categories those defined by the following local models: (affine schemes, super-
regions in lRmln or analytic super-regions in c m1n ) (the reader is suggested
to give the corresponding definitions).

10. Definition. A scheme is a locally ringed space (M,OM) such that any
point x E M has a neighbourhood U with the property that (U, OM I U) is
isomorphic to an affine scheme in the category of locally ringed spaces.

11. Definition. A differentiable supermanifold is a locally ringed superspace
(M, 0 M) such that any point x E M has a neighbourhood U with the property
that (U, OM I U) is isomorphic to a superregion in lRmln in the category of
superspaces locally ringed by lR-algebras.

     (About the significance of the lR-algebra condition see II.4.7b).
       4. Category Approach to the Construction of Geometrical Objects          99


12. How to Describe a Topology in the
Category Theory Language?

Until now we have studied some extensions of classical geometrical cate-
gories. Now we change our viewpoint and describe additional data one must
introduce on a category so that it might play the role of an analogue of a
topological space.
    Recall first of all, that open sets in an arbitrary topological space X are
objects of the category Topx whose morphisms are inclusions (see II.1.5e).
This category has a final object X: for any open set U there exists a unique
morphism U - t X. Combinatorial structure of Topx is described by intersec-
tions; in the category theory language intersections are fiber products over
X (see II.3.5)
                                UI n U2 = UI X U2 .
                                             x
Finally, an important notion of the covering is described in terms of sums
in Topx (more precisely, in terms of the amalgamated sum over the empty
open set, see II.3.6):
   a) for any family (Ui ) the sum 11 Ui exists and coincides with the set-
theoretic union UUi ·
                  i
   b) a family of inclusions Ui - t U, i E Ii is a covering if and only if the
canonical morphism 11 Ui - t U is an isomorphism.
                       i
    Historically, the first categorical generalization of this categorial descrip-
tion of a topological space was the notion of the Grothendieck topology on
a scheme. Consider an affine scheme Spec A, where A is an integral domain,
say A = IF q [Xl, ... ,xn]. In the Zariski topology the intersection of any two
nonempty open subsets of this scheme is again non-empty. Therefore, the
nerve of any finite open covering has the combinatorial type of a simplex. The
same is true for any irreducible algebraic variety. Therefore, purely topolog-
ical invariants cannot distinguish these varieties. The consideration of coho-
mology with coefficients in coherent sheaves improves the situation, but this
improvement appears to be unsufficient. For example, one still lacks a good
Lefschetz type formula for the number of fixed points of a mapping.
    The Grothendieck idea to overcome this insufficiency was to extend the
notion of topology: he suggested to consider as "open sets" not just open
imbeddings but certain more general mappings f : U - t X such as, for ex-
ample, nonramified coverings, flat morphisms (in the category of schemes),
etc. In such generalization open sets become objects of some category. Inter-
sections and preimages are defines using fiber products. The essential point
is that the notion of the covering is not deduced from some structures in the
category, but instead forms a part of the definition. A convenient intermediate
notion is the notion of a sieve.
13. Definition. Let S be a category, U E Ob S, .p = {'Pi, Ui - t U liE I} be
some family of morphisms in S.
100     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


     a) <P is said to be a sieve over U if any composition V ~ Ui ~ U with
'P E <P belongs to <P.
     b) The minimal sieve containing all morphisms 'l/Jj : Uj ---t U,j E J, is
 called the sieve generated by this family of morphisms.
     c) Let ¢ be a sieve over U and'P : V ---t U be a morphism. The restriction
<Pv of the sieve <P on V is the family of all 'l/Ji : Vi ---t V such that 'P 0 'l/Ji E <P
for all i.

14. Definition. A Grothendieck topology on the category S consists of fam-
ilies C(U) of sieves, one fanily for any U E ObS. The sieves from C(U) are
called covering sieves over U. They should satisfy the following axioms.
     a) The set of all morphism 'P : U' ---t U in S is a covering sieve over U.
     b) The restriction of a covering sieve is a covering sieve.
     c) The notion of the covering sieve is local in the following sense. Let <P
be a covering sieve over U and let 1[/ be another sieve over U such that the
restriction of 1[/ to any element of <P is a covering sieve. Then 1[/ itself is a
covering sieve.

   A covering sieve over U in the category Top X is, roughly speaking, a
covering containing all its subdivisions.

15. Sheaves

A category together with a Grothendieck topology on it is called a site.
    Axioms of Grothendieck topologies (contrary to, say, topologies in func-
tion spaces) are oriented not to limit processes but to processes of gluing
global objects from local ones and to the related cohomology invariants.
Sheaves are main tools in such constructions. Now we show how to define
a sheaf in a site.
    A presheaf of sets (of abelian groups, of rings, etc.) on a site S is a (con-
travariant) functor F : So ---t Set (resp. So ---t Ab, So ---t Ring). The standard
language of the sheaf theory can be easily extended to the case of presheaves
on a site: elements s E F(U) are called sections of F over Uj the image of s
in F(V) under the mapping F(f) is called the restriction of s to V. (It can
be denoted by sjV, keeping in mind that V can be mapped to U by different
morphisms J, and sjV depends on f).
    Let F be a presheaf, <P a sieve over U. A family of sections s<p E F(V),
one for any element 'P : V ---t U of the sieve <P, is said to be compatible if for
any V' ~ V ~ U we have s<po'IjJ = s<p jV'.
    A presheaf F is said to be a sheaJif for any object U, any covering sieve
<P over U, and any compatible family of sections (s<p) over <P there exists a
unique section s E F(U) whose restrictions to elements of <P are s<p.

19. Remarks

a) Similarly one can prove that the category of sheaves of abelian groups
on Sc is equivalent to the category of G-modules. In applications one needs
some variants of these constructions where G is a topological group with
the continuous action on elements of a site. Let, for example, k be a field,
G = Gal(k/k) with the Krull topology and Sc be the category of G-sets with
continuous action of G. This is the etale Grothendieck topology on the point
affine scheme Speck (cf. II.3.11). A sheaf of abelian groups on Speck is the
same as a continuous G-module.
    b) The proof of proposition II.4.17 shows that any sheaf of sets on Sc is
a representable functor.
    In the general site we do not have a direct relation between sheaves of sets
and representable functors. However, the existence of such a relation is quite
important. In particular, there exists a theorem that gives a characteriza-
tion of those sites where sheaves of sets coincide with representable functors.
Roughly speaking, these sites are themselves categories of sheaves with some
canonical topology.
104     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


    Now we can describe the last geometrical construction of this section. It
associates to any category a big (in general, infinite-dimensional) topological
space whose homotopy properties form, for example, the basis for the alge-
braic K-theory. This space is the geometrical realization of some simplicial
set (see I.2), the nerve of the category, and we will use the language of sim-
plicial sets. The nerve can be constructed only for small categories, i.e. those
where objects form a set.
20. Definition. a) The nerve of a small category C is the simplicial set NC
such that
               NC n = is the set of diagrams of the form
          Xo ~ Xl ~ ... ~1 X n , Xi E ObC, 'Pi E Mor C.                    (II.17)

otherwise.
    b) Let F : C - C be a functor from one small category to another. The
nerve of this functor is the morphism of simplicial sets (see 1. 2.15) that maps
a simplex (Xi, 'Pi) into the simplex (F(Xi),F('Pi))·
21. Example. Let En be the category with n + 1 objects, Ob En =
{O, 1, ... ,n} and with HomEn(k,l) consisting of one object for k :<::::: land

categories to the category Ll 0 Set of simplicial sets. Here are some of its prop-

22. Properties of the Nerve

a) The category C can be recovered from its nerve uniquely up to an iso-
morphism (not equivalence!)). In fact, let X = NC. Then ObC = X o,
MorC = Xl.
    More explicitely, in the notations of I.4.1, an I-simplex x E Xl represents
a morphism from X (aD (x) to X (an (x). Next, let Xl, X2 E Xl be two 1-

a unique 2-simplex X2 0 Xl E X 2 with X (a~) (X2 0 Xl) = Xl, X (ag) (X2 0

    b) N(C xC') = D(NC x NC') (see definitions II.1.7c) and 1.3.4, 1.3.5).
The proof is obvious.
    c) The functor N is fully faithful: the mapping F f-+ N Find uces a bijec-
tion
                   HomSCat(C, C') ~ Hom..1 0set(NC, NC').
To recover from f : NC ----> NC' a functor F : C ----> C' such that f = N F is
easy: the action of F on objects (resp. on morphisms) is the action of f on
a-simplices (resp. on I-simplices). The fact that F is a functor follows, by a),
from the commutativity of f with simplicial maps.
    Note now that Homscat(C, C') (functors from C to C' is again a category
with natural transformations as morphisms (see II.l.IO where we wrote Funet
instead of Homscat). Hence we may try to compute the nerve of Homscat(C, C')
in terms of NC and NC'. To state the answer it is useful to discuss one general
notion.

23. The Object "Inner Hom"

Morphisms of one set into another form a set; morphisms of one abelian
group into another form an abelian group. To axiomatize the situation when
for two objects Y, Z E Ob C one can construct the third object "inner Hom"
Hom(Y, Z) E ObC, it is natural to construct the corresponding representing
functor. In most cases it is given by a formula of the form

                  Homc(X, Hom(Y, Z)) = Homc(X * Y, Z)

where * is some product operation (so that Z f-+ Hom(Y, Z) is right adjoint
to X f-+ X *Y). The reader can check that this formula is valid in the following
categories: Set (* is the direct product of sets), Ab (* is the tensor product
over Z), Cat (* is the product of categories, cf. II.l.7c)). The next theorem
shows the existence of "inner Hom" in the category L\ ° Set and its relation
to the corresponding object in Cat.
    For two simplicial sets X, Y define X * Y = D(X x Y) (see 1.3.4, 1.3.5) so
that (X * Y)i = Xi X Yi and (X * Y)(f) = (X(f), Y(f)) for f : [mj ----> [nj.

§5. Additive and Abelian Categories

1. Abelian Variable of the Homology Theory

We have already mentioned in 1.6.1 that any homology theory depends on two
variables: abelian and non-abelian. An abelian variable is usually an object
of an abelian category (while a non-abelian one is the category itself). The
notion of the abelian category is used to axiomisize main properties of the
following categories.

a. Abelian groups.
b. Modules over a ring.
c. Systems of coefficients (1.4.8) and presheaves of abelian groups (1.5).
d. Sheaves of abelian groups (1.5).
    We will formulate one by one axioms A1-A4 of an abelian category C
(defining corresponding notions), check these axioms for the categories a.-d.,
and finally, show how some of these axioms break up for certain similarly,
looking non-abelian categories such as, for example, the categories of topo-
logical abelian groups or of filtered groups.


2. Axiom Al

Any set Home(X, Y) is an abelian group (we will use additive notation); the
composition of morphisms is bi-additive.
   In other words, Home is a functor Co xC -7 Ab. Note also that this implies
the non-emptyness of each set Homc(X, Y) because any group contains the
zero element.
   In all examples a.-d. the group structure on any Home(X, Y) is clear.
110     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


3. Axiom A2
There exists a zero object 0 E ObC, that is an object such that Homc(O, 0) is
the zero group. This implies that Home (0, X) and Home (X, 0) are also the
zero groups for any X E Ob C and that any two zero objects are isomorphic.
   In the examples a.-d. zero objects are obvious ones.

4. Axiom A3
For any two objects Xl, X 2 there exist an object Y and morphisms
                                        Pl     P2
                                    Xl~Y~X2                                 (II.19)
                                        i1     i2


such that
                  Pl it = id x l'   P2 i 2 = id x2 ,   ilPl + i2P2 = id y
                                                                            (II.20)
                  P2il = Pl i 2 = O.
The following simple lemma clarifies the significance of this axiom.
5. Lemma. The following two squares are respectively cartesian and co-
cartesian:

                      Pi
            Y

        ~j                          j
                                    o                                   o
In other words, Y is both the direct sum and the direct product of Xl and
X 2. In particular, for given Xl and X 2 any two diagrams of the form (II.19)
are canonically isomorphic.

Proof. We shall show, that given a diagram Xl ~ Y
construct a unique morphism cp : Y' --+ Y such that
                           p~ = Pl 0 cp,     p~ = P2 0 cpo                  (II.21 )
If such a cp exists, then, multiplying the first equality by i l , the second one
by i2, and summing up the products we get (taking into account (II.20)) that
ilP~ + i2p~ = cpo On the other hand, cp = ilP~ + i2p~ satisfies (II.21). This
proves that the first square is cartesian; we leave the second verification to
the reader.                                                                  0

   In categories a.-d. the existence of direct sums and products is verified
automatically.
   Now we begin to analyze the least trivial property of categories a.-d.,
namely the existence of exact sequences.
                                         5. Additive and Abelian Categories        111


6. Kernel

Let a category C satisfy axioms Al and A2, and let cp : X -> Y be a morphism.
Consider the following functor ker cp : Co -> Ab:
                        (kercp)(Z) = ker(X(Z) ----> Y(Z))
          (ker cp) (f) = is the restriction of h x (f) to (ker cp) (Z) (see
                         II.3.1, II.3.2).
The inclusion (ker cp)( Z) '---4 X (Z) defines a morphism offunctors k : ker cp ->
h x . Assume that kercp is represented by an object K. This object is defined
together with a morphism k : K -> X (theorem II.3.3) such that cp 0 k = O.
The diagram K ~ X ~ Y satisfies the following universal property: for
any morphism k' : K' -> X with cp 0 k' = 0 there exists a unique morphism
h : K' -> K with k' = k 0 h.
    The morphism k, or the pair (K, k), is called the kernel of cp; if confusion
is impossible we may call the kernel the object K itself.
    Let us prove that if the kernel (K, k) ofthe morphism cp exists, it is unique.
First of all, applying the universal property of the kernel to k : K -> X we
see that the only morphism e : K -> K with k 0 e = k is e = id K . Let now
k1 : K1 -> X and k2 : K2 -> X be two kernels of the morphism cpo From
the universality of the diagrams K1 ~ X ~ Y and K2 ~ X ~ Y we
get that there exist unique morphisms '1/;1 : K1 -> K 2, '1/;2 : K2 -> K1 with
k2 0 '1/;1 = k1' k1 0 '1/;2 = k 2. Therefore k1 0 '1/;2 0 '1/;1 = k1 and cancelling, as
mentioned above, by k1' we get '1/;2 0 '1/;1 = idK1 · Similarly, '1/;1 0 '1/;2 = idK2'
and pairs (Kb k 1) and (K2' k 2) are isomorphic.
    In categories a.-d. there is a set-theoretical construction of the kernel:
cp-1(O) in groups and modules, the family cp;1(O) in the systems of coefficients
(see 1.5.8), the family CPu 1 (O) in sheaves (where a morphism of sheaves cp :
F -> Q is given by morphisms CPu : F(U) -> Q(U) for all open sets U).
7. Lemma. a} In the categories of examples a.-d. the set-theoetical kernel
of a morphism cp : X -> Y is an object K of the same category.
    b} The canonical embedding of K into X is the kernel in the corresponding
category (in the sense of II.5.6).

   We advise to the reader to check at least that K is a sheaf (and not just
a presheaf) (see 1.5.4a).

8. Cokernel

The first coming to mind naive definition of Coker cp as the object repre-
senting the functor Z 1-+ Coker(X(Z) -> Y(Z)) is wrong. This functor is not
isomorphic to the functor represented by the set-theoretical cokernel even
in the category of abelian groups. Indeed, let X = Y = Z, cp be the mul-
tiplication by an integer n > 1, Z = ZjnZ. Then X(Z) = Y(Z) = 0 so
112      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


that Coker (X(Z) -+ Y(Z» = 0 but Hom(ZjnZ,Coker<p) =I 0 (because the
set-theoretic cokernel is Coker<p = ZjnZ). We strongly recommend to check
that the functor Z t-t Coker (X(Z) -+ Y(Z» is not even representable.
    The correct definition of Coker <p - in the case this functor is representable
- requires the double dualization:
                              Coker <p = (ker <po t ,
where ° denotes the duality functor (see 11.1.7b). The reader can check that
this definition is equivalent to each of the following ones.
    a) The cokernel of a morphism <p : X -+ Y is a morphism c : Y -+ K'
such that for any Z E ObC the sequence of groups
             0--+ Home (K', Z) --+ Homc(Y, Z) --+ Home (X, Z)
is exact (this means that (K')O represents ker <pO).
    b) The co kernel of a morphism <p : X -+ Y is a morphism c : Y -+ K'
such that c 0 <p = 0 and for any morphism Cl : Y -+ K~ with Cl 0 <p = 0 there
exists a unique morphism h : K' -+ Ki with Cl = hoc.
    The cokernel C : Y -+ K' (similarly to the kernel), if it exists, is determined
uniquely up to a unique isomorphism.
    In categories a.-c. one has the usual set-theoretic definition of the cokernel
and the analog of lemma II.5.7 is valid. For example, in the category Ab of
abelian groups the cokernel of a homomorphism <p : G -+ H is the pair
(K', c), where K' = Hj<p(G), c : H -+ K' is the factorization. Similarly one
defines the cokernel of a morphism in the category of modules over a fixed
ring. The cokernel of a morphism <p : X -+ Y in the category of presheaves
is the pair (K', c), where K' is the presheaf K'(U) = Y(U)j<pu(X(U», Cu :
Y(U) -+ K'(U) is the factorization. Similarly one defines the co kernel in the
category of local systems on a fixed simplicial set.
    The situation is more complicated in the category S Ab of sheaves of
abelian groups. The reason is that even if <p : X -+ Y is a morphism of sheaves
{K'(U) = Coker <pu} might be a presheaf, but not a sheaf (see 1.5.4b). One
can verify that {K'(U)} is the cokernel in the category of presheaves.
    In II.5.12-II.5.17 we construct cokernels of morphisms in the category of
sheaves. Now we will state the last axiom.

9. Axiom A4
For any morphism <p : X -+ Y there exists a sequence
                         K~X~I~Y~K'                                         (11.22)
with the following properties:
a) j 0 i = <Pi
b) K is the kernel of <p, K' is the cokernel of <Pi
c) I is both the kernel of k and the cokernel of c.
      Any such sequence is called a canonical decomposition.
                                      5. Additive and Abelian Categories     113


10. Definition. An additive category is a category with axioms Ai-A3; an
abelian category is a category with axioms Ai-A4.
    All the categories a.-d. from 11.5.1 are additive. Moreover, they are
abelian. The existence of the canonical decomposition of a morphism <p : G ---7
H in the category Ab (Le., the isomorphism 1m<p 3:! G/ker<p) is guaranteed
by the homomorphism theorem for abelian groups; similarly one checks that
the category of modules over a fixed ring is abelian. For categories from c.
the canonical decomposition is constructed componentwise. For example, in
the category PAb of presheaves of abelian groups the canonical decomposi-
tion of a morphism <p : X ---7 Y is obtained from canonical decomposition of
morphisms <pu : X(U) ---> Y(U) in Ab

               K(U) ~ X(U) ~ (U) ~ Y(U) ~ K'(U);

one can easily check that they are compatible with restrictions.
   Below we prove that the category SAb of sheaves of abelian groups is
abelian (proposition II.S.IS).

11. Commentary to the Axiom A4

a. If a canonical decomposition of a morphism exists, then any other canonical
decomposition is isomorphic to it, and this isomorphism is unique.
    b. If one requires only the existence of kernels and cokernels, then for any
morphism <p there exist two halves of the diagram (II.22):
                      k i
                    K~X~I,                I'~Y~K'
where k = ker <p, i = Coker k, c = Coker <p, j = ker c. The additional
requirement of axiom A4 is that I should coincide with I'; more precisely
that there should exist an isomorphism l : I ---7 I' such that <p = j 0 l 0 i.
One can check that a morphism l with such property can be constructed
canonically, and ker l = Coker l = 0 (i.e., l is both a monomorphism and an
epimorphism), so axiom 4 requires, in fact, that it were invertible. Sometimes
(I',j) is called the image of <p, and (I,i) is the coimage of <p.
    c. The axiom A4 is self-dual in the following sense. Consider the diagram
(II.22) in the dual category Co:



If (II.22) is the canonical decomposition of <p, then (II.22°) is the canonical
decomposition of <po.
    Similar self-duality properties can be stated for axioms AI-A3. Hence, if
we set Home (X, Y) = Homeo (yo, XO) as an abelian group, then the category
dual to an additive one is additive, and that dual to an abelian one is abelian.
114    II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


    d. In an abelian category any morphism cp with ker cp = 0 and Coker cp = 0
is necessarily an isomorphism. Indeed, the cokernel of 0 ~ X is isomorphic to
id : X ~ X and the kernel of Y ~ 0 is isomorphic to id : Y ~ Y. Therefore,
by axiom A4, i and j are isomorphisms, and X ~ Y.
    e. Morphisms cp with ker cp = 0 are called monomorphisms, those with
Coker cp = 0 are called epimorphisms.

12. Sheaves and Presheaves

Let SAb (resp. PAb) be the additive category of sheaves (resp. presheaves)
of abelian groups on a fixed topological space M. Any sheaf is a presheaf
satisfying some additional conditions; so that one can define an inclusion
functor t : SAb ~ PAb. The main role in the proof that the category SAb
is abelian, plays a functor in the opposite direction s : PAb ~ SAb and a
canonical isomorphism of bifunctors

                    Horns Ab(SX, Y) ~ HompAb(X, tY).                    (11.23)

After the proof of (11.23) one checks that the co kernel of a morphism of
sheaves cp : X ~ Y can be defined as s(K) where K is the cokernel of cp in
PAb, and then proves the existence of the canonical decomposition.

17. Filtered Abelian Groups

An object of the category AbF of filtered abelian groups is an abelian group

     HomAbF(X, Y) = {cp E HomAb(X, Y) I cp(FiX) C Fiy for all i}.

    The kernel of a morphism cP in AbF is the group ker cp (Le. the kernel of

18. Topological Abelian Groups

Objects of the category AbT are abelian groups with a Hausdorff topology;
morphisms in AbT are continuous group homomorphisms. Any morphism
'P : X -+ Y in AbT has the kernel and the cokernel: ker'P is the kernel of
'P in Ab with the induced topology; Coker 'P is Y/'P(X), where 'P(X) is the
closure of 'P(X) in y.
    Let, for example, '¢ : Y -+ Z be a morphism such that '¢ 0 'P = O. Then
ker'¢ = '¢-l(O) C Y is a closed subgroup containing 'P(X), so also 'P(X).
Therefore, '¢ can be factored through a morphism Y/'P(X) -+ Z.
    In the notations ofII.5.11b we have I = X/ker'P, I' = 'P(X) with the in-
duced topology. If 'P(X) is not closed, then the canonical morphism I -+ I' is
not an isomorphism. It may happen also that 'P(X) is closed but the topology
induced from Y is weaker than the one induced from X. For example, the
identity mapping of IR with discrete topology into IR with the usual topology
has zero kernel and cokernel, but is not an isomorphism.

8. Jordan-Holder Theorem. A non-zero object of an abelian category A
is said to be simple it if has no proper subobjects. A Jordan-Holder series
of an object X is a filtration {O} = Xo C Xl C ... C X n - l C Xn = X
such that all Xii X i - l are simple objects. The Jordan-Holder theorem says
that any filtration {O} = Xb c Xi c ... C X:r,-l c x:r, = X of a finite
length object X such that all XU XI-I are non-zero can be compressed to a
Jordan-Holder series, and any two Jordan-Holder series of an object X are
equivalent (i.e., determine the same, up to a permutation, collection of simple
objects Xd Xi-i). In particular, all Jordan-Holder series have equal lengths.
9. Quotient Categories a la Serre. Let A be an abelian category, B C A

and any quotient object.

their extension (i.e., the middle term of any exact triple of the form 0 --t
B' --t B --t BIf --t 0).

of all monomorphisms 'P : B --t A such that Coker 'P C Ob B with the
following ordering: ('P : B --t A) < ('P' : B' --t A) if and only if 'P can be
factored through 'P', i.e., 'P = 'P' e for some (obviously unique) e : B --t B'.
    Dual definition gives a partial ordering on the set QUBA of all epimor-

    Standard examples of subcategories B satisfying the above conditions are

in Vectk, 2-Gr (2-groups) in F Gr (finite groups), etc. The existence and good
properties of the quotient categories AlBin the sense of Serre enable us to
use arguments "modulo B" (for example, to work in the category of abelian
varieties up to isogeny).


§6. Functors in Abelian Categories

1. Definition. Let C, C' be two additive categories. A functor F : C ~ C' is
called additive if all maps

            F : Homc(X, Y) ---> Homc/(FX, FY), x, Y E ObC,

are homomorphisms of abelian groups.

   In this book we will consider mostly additive functors. However, the reader
should not think that all interesting functors are additive. Important non-
additive functors are tensor powers and their canonical direct summands on
the category A-mod over a commutative ring A



If A is a Q-algebra then any idempotent a in the group algebra of the sym-
metric group Sn yields the functor

In particular, in such a way we get the symmetric power sn (E) (correspond-
ing to a = ;h L:sEsn s) and the exterior power An(E) (corresponding to
a = ;h L:sEsn (sgn s)s).
   The main properties of additive functors are related to whether or not they
conserve kernels and cokernels. Let us introduce all necessary definitions.

   d) A complex X· in an abelian category is said to be exact (or acyclic, or

4. Definition. Let C, C' be two abelian categories. An additive functor F :
C ---+ C' is said to be exact if for any exact sequence

                           O----XLY~Z----O
in C the sequence

                    o ---- F(X) ---- F(Y) ---- F(Z) ---- 0
in C' is also exact. F is said to be left exact if the sequence (*) is exact
everywhere except, possibly, the term F(Z). Similarly, F is said to be right
exact if (*) is exact everywhere except, possibly, the term F(X).
    The interest in the exactness properties of an additive functor F can be
explained as follows. In many situations we are interested (and historically
this was the reason to study exactness properties) in the values of F on some
specific objects. For example, the computation of the space of sections F(F)
for some sheaf F (or of its dimension) is the classical "Riemann-Roch type
problem" . If the functor "sections of a sheaf" were exact, this problem would
become much easier: a) for any short exact sequence 0 ---- Fl ---- F ----
F2 ---- 0 we would have dimF(F) = dimF(F1 ) + dimF(F2). b) sheaves
we are interested in, say on Riemann surfaces, can be easily represented
as sequential extension of some simple sheaves with known dimensions of
dimF(F).
    However, the functor F is not exact but only left exact, and the main task
of the homological algebra is to overcome this nonexactness.
    In this section we collect basic facts about exactness properties of some
important functors.
126     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory

8. Definition. a) An object Y of an abelian category A is said to be projec-
tive if the functor X f-+ HomA (Y, X) is exact.
    b) An object Y of an abelian category A is said to be injective if the
functor X f-+ HomA(X, Y) is exact.
    c) A left A-module X (resp. a right A-module Y) is said to be flat if the
functor Y f-+ Y 0 X (resp. X f-+ Y 0 X) is exact.
                  A                     A

   Part c) of this definition can be applied to sheaves of modules on an
arbitrary site.
   Let us discuss various aspects of this definition.

9. Injectivity, Projectivity and Extension of Morphisms

Let us consider two diagrams in an abelian category A:




             1\
              X -X"- 0
               (projectivity diagram)
                                               1\
                                               X-X'- 0
                                                (injectivity diagram)


      We will interpret these diagrams as the following properties of the object
Y:
    a) For any surjective morphism X --+ X" and for any morphism Y --+
X" there exists a morphism Y --+ X that makes the projectivity diagram
commutative.
    b) For any injective morphism X' --+ X and for any morphism X' --+ Y
there exists a morphism X --+ Y that makes the injectivity diagram commu-
tative.
                                         6. Functors in Abelian Categories       129


    (Main agreement: a solid arrow (resp. a dahsed arrow) means "for any"
(resp. "there exists"); cf. the diagram in the definition 2.6.2b).
    We claim that the property a) (resp. the property b» is equivalent to the
projectivity (resp. to the injectivity) of an object Y. Indeed, let, for example,
an object Y satisfy a) and we are given an exact triple

                         o ~ X' L X ~ X" ~ o.
Let us consider the corresponding sequence of Hom's
                            f*                g*
      o ~ Hom(Y, X') ~ Hom(Y, X) ~ Hom(Y, X") ~ o.                           (11.37)

    By proposition II.6.5, it is exact in all terms except, possibly, Hom(Y, X")
and property a) means that g* is surjective. Therefore the sequence (II.37)
is exact and Y is projective.
    On the other hand, let Y be projective. Since the surjection X ~ X" in
the projectivity diagram can be completed to an exact triple, the exactness
of (II.37) at Hom(Y, X") yields property a).

10. Projective Modules and Free Modules

The previous statement easily gives a description of projective objects in
categories of modules (right or left ones): a module over a ring is projective
if and only if it is a direct summand of a free module.
    First, a free module Y is projective. Indeed, let {Yi} be a family of free
generators of Y. Suppose that we are given a projectivity diagram for Y. To
construct 'I/J it suffices to define 'I/J((Yi). We set 'I/J(Yi) = Xi where Xi E X is
an arbitrary element such that 7r(Xi) = <P(Yi) (such an Xi exixts because 7r is
surjective) .
    Next, a direct summand of a projective module is itself projective. In fact,
let Y = Y1 EB Y 2 and we are given a projectivity diagram for Y1

completes the diagram (11.38).
     On the other hand, let Y be projective. There exists an exact sequence

rather few projective objects. In some cases instead of projective objects
one can consider locally free sheaves (Le., sheaves that become free after
restrictions to elements of some covering or crible).

11. Injective Modules and Divisibility

Any finite dimensional linear space of over a field k is both a projective and
an injective object of the category Vectf: is is projective because it is free,
and injective because the category is equivalent to the dual one.
    The category of modules over an arbitrary ring is, in general, very far
from being equivalent to the dual category, and the classes projective and
of injective modules are very different from each other (see, however, IV.3).
The projectivity of a module is related, roughly speaking, to the absence of
relations among appropriate generators, while the injectivity is related to the
possibility to divide by elements of the ring A. A typical obstruction to the
injectivity is of the following kind: Let an element x' E X' can be divided by
a E A in a bigger module X :J X' (a x = x' for some x E X) but not in X'.
Then the identity morphism cp = id : X' ---+ X' can not be completed in the
appropriate injectivity diagram.
    Injective modules over a commutative ring are also closely related to prime
ideals of this ring. As an example, we give a list of minimal injective abelian
groups (Le., of Z-modules):
    a) Q (the localization of Z by the complement of the prime ideal {O}),
    b) Q(p)/Z (p is a prime, Q(p) is an additive group of rational numbers
with denominators pn).
    Let us check that these groups are injective. Consider an injectivity dia-
gram
                              Y




                                 ~
                            X-X' -               0

(so that X' is a subgroup of X), where Y is either Q or one of groups Q(p)/7L..

     This example leads to an important observation: injective abelian groups
are infinitely generated. This is a general phenomenon: in the next chapter
(III.8.1 in the next chapter) that to prove the existence of sufficiently many
injective objects in, say, the category of sheaves of abelian groups one has to
use infinite limits.

13. Flat Modules and Projective Modules

The following properties of flat modules are almost obvious:
a) Free modules are flat.
b) Direct summands of flat modules are flat.
c) Inductive limits of families of flat modules are flat.
                                          6. Functors in Abelian Categories      133


(To check this property one must use the fact that inductive limits com-
mute with direct limits and preserve exactness. See some details in the next
section.)
    Properties a) and b) imply that projective modules are flat, and property
c) implies that inductive limits of projective modules are flat. The theorem
proved independently by Govorov and Lazard says that, conversely, any flat
A-module is isomorphic to an inductive limit of free modules of the finite
type.

14. Acyclic Objects of a Site

Continuing the analysis of objects generating exact functors we return to the
situation of proposition H.6.6. Let A be an abelian category of sheaves on a
site S. An object U E ObS is said to be A-acyclic if the functor A ---+ Ab,
F f--+ F(U), is exact.
     Here are three important examples of acyclic objects. In all these examples
S is the topology on a topological space X.
     a) A is the category of sheaves of abelian groups on a topological space
X, U c X is an open subset homeomorphic to a ball.
     b) A is the category of sheaves of modules on a scheme X, that are
locally isomorphic to cokernels of morphisms of Ox-modules            oi- ---+   oi
(such sheaves are called quasicoherent; about schemes see H.4.lO and Ex.
1.5.3). An open (in Zariski topology) subset U c X is A-acyclic iff (U, OxlU)
is isomorphic to an affine scheme as a ringed space (the Serre theorem).
    c) A is the category of modules on an analytic space that are locally
(in the usual Hausdorff topology) isomorphic to cokernels of Ox-modules
0i-   ---+0i  (quasicoherent analytic sheaves). The class of A-acyclic subset
U c X coincides with the class of the so-called Stein manifolds (the Cartan
theorem).
    Finally, we consider the exactness properties of direct and inverse images
of sheaves.

15. Definition. Let f : M ---+ N be a continuous map of topological spaces,
F be a sheaf of sets on M. Its direct image f.F is defined as a sheaf on N
whose sections on any open U C X are given by

                              f.(F)(U) = F (J-l(U))

and the restriction map from U to V c U is determined by the restriction
map from f-l(U) to f-l(V).

      Clearly, f.(F) is a presheaf; one can check directly that it is a sheaf.
134     II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


16. Properties of the Direct Image

a) The construction of f. preserve additional structures that may exist on a
sheaf F: the direct image of a sheaf of groups is a sheaf of groups, and so on. If
tJ> = (f, B) is a morphism of ringed spaces (see II.4.5) and F is a sheaf of OM-
modules, then f. (F) has a natural structure of a sheaf of modules. Indeed,
we must define an action of ON(U) on f.(F)(U) = F (J-l(U)). But the
morphism tJ> of ring spaces contains a (compatible with restrictions) family
of ringed homomorphisms CPu: ON(U) ~ OM (J-l(U)) and F (J-l(U)) is a
OM (J-l(U))-module. Therefore f.(F)(U) becomes an ON (U)-module. This
also shows that {CPu} can be considered as a morphism of sheaves of rings
ON ~ f.(OM)'
    b) Let f : M ~ {e} (one point space). Then f.(F) = r(M,F) (global
sections).
    More generally, if f : M ~ N is a locally trivial fibration, then f.(F) is
the sheaf of "sections of F along the fibers of f" ..
    Let i : M ~ N be a closed imbedding. The sheaf i.(F) is sometimes
called "the extension of F by zero": if U c N\M, then i.(F)(U) is one-point
set (if F is a sheaf of sets) or i.(F)(U) = {O} (if F is a sheaf of abelian
groups).
    The use of the same name for the sheaf i. (F) in the case when i : M ~ N
is a general (not necessarily closed) embedding is safe if one remembers that
the stalk of i.(F) at a point of the boundary of i(M) might be non-trivial.
    c) Any morphism of sheaves cP : F ~ Q on M induces (in an obvious
way) the morphism of sheaves f.(cp) : f.(F) ~ f.(Q) on N. Therefore, f. is
a functor from the category of sheaves on M to the category of sheaves on
N. The same is true for categories of abelian groups, of modules over ringed
spaces, and so on.
    We mention also two functorial properties of f. with respect to f:

                             (fg). = f.g.,id. = id.

of topological spaces, and F a sheaf of sets on N.

                        Homv(FX, Y) = Home (X, FY)                             (HAl)

so that F is left adjoint to G, and G is right adjoint to F. Then F is right
exact and G is left exact.
   Indeed, let

be an exact sequence in D. Then, using (IIAl) and the left exactness of the
