Exercises

1. Limits In Abelian Categories. a) Prove that the kernel of morphisms
f, g : X --t Y in an abelian category A coincides with the equalizer of f - g
and the zero morphism 0 : X --t Y. Similar results hold for cokernels and
coequalizers. Conclude that finite limits and colimits exist in any abelian
category.
138      II. Main Notions of the Category Theory


    The existence of infinite limits (and colimits) imposes additional restric-
tions to an abelian category A. Following Grothendieck, we formulate some
standard axioms.
      AB3). The direct sum EBXi of any family (Xi)iEI of objects in A exists.
    b) Let I be some set of indices. Define the category AI whose objects
are families (XI )iEI ,Xi E Ob A (with obvious morphisms), prove that it is
abelian, and, assuming AB3, prove that (Xi) ~ EBXi determines a functor
A I ~ A. Prove that this functor is exact for finite I and is right exact for an
arbitrary I. Give an example showing that, in general, it is not left exact (i.e.,
that the direct sum of monomorphisms might fail to be a monomorphism).
Thus,
  AB4). AB3 is satisfied and the direct sum of monomorphisms is a mono-
morphism.
    c) Formulate dual axioms AB3*, AB4* (about direct products).
    d) Prove that the category Ab (and, more generally, the category R-mod
for any ring R) satisfies AB4, AB4*, and the category of sheaves of abelian
groups on a topological space satisfies AB4 and AB3*, but does not satisfy,
in general, AB4*.
2. Categories That Are Equivalent to Categories of Modules. Let A
be an abelian category satisfying AB3. An object P of A is said to be strictly
projective (or a projective generator) if the functor h' : X t---+ HomA (P, X)
from A to Ab is exact (projectivity of P), strict (i.e., h'(X) = 0 =}
X = 0), and commutes with direct sums. For such an object P we set
R = HomA(P, P). Any group h'(X) = HomA(P, X) carries a natural struc-
ture of a right R-module. Prove that h' determines an equivalence of A and
mod-R.
    b) Let A be Noetherian (any increasing chain of subjects stabilizes), and
P be a projective object in A such that h' is a strict functor. Then the
ring R = HomA(P, P) is right Noetherian and h' determines an equivalence
between A and the category of finitely generated R-modules.
    c) Prove that conditions from b) are satisfied in the case when A has only
a finite number of pairwise non-isomorphic simple objects Xl"'" Xn such
that for any i there exists an epimorphism Pi ~ Xi with a projective Pi and
P = EBPi . Categories with these (or similar) properties often appear in group
representation theory.

and Derived Functors




§1. Complexes as Generalized Objects

1. Generators and Relations

Homological algebra was founded by D. Hilbert. He considered, in particular,
the following problem. Let L:7=1 aijXj = OJ i = 1, ... , nj aij E k[tt, ... , trl,
be a system of linear homogeneous equations with coefficients lying in the
polynomial ring over a field. All polynomial solutions are linear combina-
tions (with polynomial coefficients) of a finite subset of solutions. However,
in general there exists no basis of solutions that are linearly independent over
k[tl, ... , trl. Linear relations among elements of a generating system of solu-
tions are, in turn, linear combinations of some finite set of relations, and again
it might happen that there exists no free system of generators for relations.
    Hilbert had called such linear relations among solutions, among relations
and so on, syzygies. This name came from celestian mechanics where integral
relations among periods of solar system planets period are closely related
to stability and resonance phenomena. Hilbert's fundamental theorem states
that for the ring k[tl, ... ,trl the chain of syzygies ends after r + 1 stepsj this
is a generalization of the fact that the space of solution of a linear system
over a field (r = 0) has a basis.
    Using a more modern language, let us consider a ring A (not necessarily
commutative), a left A-module E, and an exact sequence in the category of
left A-modules
                  d- n         d- n +1
    ... ---+ F- n ---+ F- n + 1 ---+ F- n +2 ---+ ... ---+ FO ~ E ---+ 0    (IILI)
with free modules Fi.
   The complex F- together with the augmentation morphism £ : F- ---t
E is called a free (left) resolution of the module E. Choosing generators
Ut, ... ,it) of the free module Fi we can describe the resolution (IlL 1) in
Hilbert's terms:
-   £   UP) ,... ,£ UZo) are generators of the A-module E (because £ is a sur-
    jection);
140     III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


- d- l UIl) , ... , d- l (f;;_\) are generators ofthe A-module of first syzygies,
  that is, of relations among the above generators (because d- l maps p - l
  onto the kernel of c; which consists just of these relations);
- d- 2 (12) , ... , d- 2 (f;;_22 ) are generators of the A-module of second syzy-
  gies, and so on.
    We shall return to the Hilbert theorem in Sect. Il1.5, and now we shall
look at resolutions.
    One of the main techniques in homological algebra is to replace E by its
resolution pe. Let is discuss first the uniqueness of such a resolution.
    We start with some general definitions. Let A be an abelian category.
The category Kom(A) of complexes over A is defined in an obvious way (see
II.1.4e for A = Ab).

let k = (k i ), k i : Ki -+ L i - l be a collection of morphisms in A. Then the
maps

4. Remark. a) Take X = Y and f = id x in Theorem IlL 1.3. Then Theorem
claims that for any two resolutions of the same object there exists a morphism
between them extending the identity morphism, and this morphism is unique
modulo homotopy. This means that a projective resolution "is unique modulo
homotopic equivalence" .
    b) The proof of the theorem shows that it remains valid even if we do not
assume that Q-i are projective; we need only that Qe ~ Y ---+ 0 is exact.
    c) Reversing arrows and replacing projective resolutions by injective ones
we obtain a dual version of Theorem IlL1.3, as well as a dual version of its
generalization from the previous remark.
5. Definition. A morphism f : K e ~ U of complexes in an abelian cat-
egory A is said to be a quasi-isomorphism if the corresponding homology
morphism Hn(J) : Hn(Ke) ~ Hn(u) is an isomorphism for any n.
    Earlier we have met with the following examples of quasi-isomorphisms:
    a) There exists a quasi-isomorphism among any two projective (injective)
resolutions of one object (Theorem IlI.1.3a) and Lemma IlL1.2c)).
    b) Any object X of an abelian category A can be considered as a complex
... ~ 0 ~ 0 ~ X ~ 0 ~ 0 ~ ... (with X at the O-th place). This complex
is acyclic outside zero, and its O-th cohomology is isomorphic to X; such a
complex will be called a O-complex. The augmentation eX of a left resolution
pe 5:£. X determines a quasi-isomorphism of complexes
                  ---+   p-l   ---+    pO    ---+   0   ---+   0


                         1o
In this way the notion of a resolution is a special case of the notion of a
quasi-isomorphism.
   c) Let oe be the complex with all terms equal to the zero object of A.
Then a unique morphism Ke ~ oe (and oe ~ K e ) is a quasi-isomorphism if
and only if K e is acyclic.

6. What is the Derived Category

The idea of the derived category, as we understand it today, can be formulated
as follows.
144    III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


    a) An object X of an abelian category should be identified with all its
resolutions.
    b) The main reason for such an identification is that some most important
functors, such as hom, tensor products, r, should be redefined. Their "naive"
definitions (see 11.6) should be applied only to some special objects, which
are acyclic with respect to this functor. If, say, X is a flat module and Y is an
arbitrary one, then X ® Y is the correct definition of the tensor product. But
in general case in order to get a correct definition one has to replace X ® Y
by p. ® Y, where p. is a flat resolution of the module X. Similarly, to get
the correct definition of the group r(F) of section of a sheaf F one has to
take the complex r(r), where r is an injective resolution of F.
    c) To adopt this point of view we must consider from the very beginning
not only objects of an abelian category and their resolutions, but arbitrary
complexes. One of the reasons why we have to do this is that p. ® Y and
r(I·) in the above examples would usually have nontrivial cohomology in
several degrees and not only in degree O. (Some readers can recall that in the
classical homological algebra the corresponding cohomology groups are called
derived functors Tori (X, Y) and Hi(F) respectively.) Hence the relation that
enables us to identify an object and its resolution should be generalized to
arbitrary complexes. The appropriate generalization is given by the notion of
a quasi-isomorphism (definition IIL1.5).
    d) The equivalence relation between complexes generated by quasi-iso-
morphisms is rather complicated, and what happens after the factorization
by this equivalence relation is difficult to trace. The technique means that
enables us to do it form the core of the theory of derived categories; it will
be discussed in the present chapter.
    e) The above (see b)) redefinition of such functors as ®, r and others
makes semiexact functors in some sense "exact". The very notion of exactness
in a derived category is by no means obvious, see the discussion in Sect.
III.3. The basis of this notion in the classical homological algebra is the exact
sequence of higher derived functors, which is invariant under the change of a
resolution.


§2. Derived Categories and Localization
1. Definition (- Theorem). Let A be an abelian category, Kom(A) the
category of complexes over A. There exists a category D(A) and a functor
Q : Kom(A) -+ D(A) with the following properties:
   a) QU) is an isomorphism for any quasi-isomorphism f·
   b) Any functor F : Kom(A) -+ V tronsforming quasi-isomorphisms into
isomorphisms can be uniquely factorized through D(A) i.e., there exists a
unique functor G : D(A) -+ V with F = Go Q.
   The category D(A) is called the derived category of the abelian category
A.
                                 2. Derived Categories and Localization    145


2. A Simple Proof of the Existence: Localization of a Category

Let 8 be an arbitrary category and S an arbitrary class of morphisms in
8. We show that there exists a universal functor transforming elements of
S into isomorphisms. More precisely, we construct a category 8[S-1] and a
"localization by S" functor Q : 8 ~ 8[S-1] with the universality property
similar to that of III.2.1b) above.
   To do this we set first Ob8[S-1] = Ob8 and define Q to be identity on
objects.
   To construct morphisms in 8[S-1] we proceed in several steps.
   a) Introduce variables Xs, one for every morphism 8 E S.
   b) Construct an oriented graph r as follows
         vertices of r = objects of 8;
         edges of r = {morphisms in 8} U {Xs, 8 E S};
         the edge X ~ Y is oriented from X to Y;
         the edge Xs has the same vertices as the edge 8 but the
         opposite orientation.
    c) A path in r is a finite sequence of edges such that the end of any edge
coincides with the beginning of the next one.
    d) A morphism in 8[S-1] is an equivalence class of paths in r with the
common beginning and the common end. Two paths are equivalent if they
can be joined by a chain of elementary equivalences of the following type:
- two consecutive arrows in a path can be replaced by their composition;
- arrows X ~ Y ~ X (resp. Y ~ X ~ Y) can be replaced by
      id             id
  X ~ X (resp. Y ~ Y).
    Finally, the composition of two morphisms is induced by the conjuction
of paths and the functor Q : 8 ~ 8[S-1] maps a morphism X ~ Y into the
class of corresponding path (of length 1). For any 8 E S the morphism Q(8)
is clearly an isomorphism in 8[S-1], the inverse being the class of the path
Xs·
   For another functor 8 ~ 8' transforming morphisms from S into quasi-
isomorphisms the functor G : 8[S-1] ~ 8' with the condition F = Go Q is
constructed as follows:

                  G(X) = F(X),X E Ob8 = Ob8[S-1];

                          G(J) = F(J), f E Mor 8,
                       G(class ofx s ) = F(8- 1),8 E S.
The reader can easily verify that all definitions are unambiguous and that
the functor G is unique.
146    III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors

5. Variants

In applications it is often useful to consider complexes with various finiteness
conditions. In particular, let

                  Kom+(A) : Ki          0   for   i:-:; io(K e ),
                  Kom-(A):K i           0   for   i~io(Ke),

                     Komb(A) = Kom+(A) nKom-(A).
These are full subcategories in Kom(A) and localizing by quasi-isomorphisms
we can form the corresponding derived categories D+(A), D-(A), Db(A).
For example, left projective resolutions lie in Kom - (A), while right injective
resolutions lie in Kom+(A).
    Now we see that we could define, say D+(A), either as the localization of
Kom+(A) by quasi-isomorphisms or as the full subcategory of D(A) consist-
ing of complexes K' with Hi(K') = 0 for i < i o. We would like to be certain
that these two constructions coincide. However, to prove this we do not have
enough technique.
    The problem is, that morphisms in 5[S-1] constructed by the method of
Subsection III.2.2 are just formal expressions of the form

        ftosl1ofzos"21o ... osk 1 ofk       where      fiEMor8,siES          (*)
and to manipulate with such expression we need some algebraic identities like
"finding the common denominator". For example, in some cases it is rather
hard even to figure out whether or not 8[S-1] is equivalent to the trivial
category with one object and one morphism.
    All the required algebraic identities are provided by the following defini-
tion.

§3. Triangles as Generalized Exact Triples
1. The ProbleIn

In this section we introduce some diagrams in derived categories - called
distingushed triangles - that may be considered as analogs of exact triples
in abelian categories. The definition of such a generalization is not at all
obvious. First of all, we do not even know that the category D(A) is additive:
to add morphisms we have, in a sense, to find "a common denominator".
Next, although D(A) will happen to be additive, it will never be abelian (see
Proposition III.2.4) so that we cannot apply to D(A) the usual definition of
exactness.
    However, although D(A) is not abelian, distinguished triangles in D(A)
give some remarkable structure, which reflects main homological properties
of the initial abelian category A.

2. Translation, Cylinder, Cone

Let A be an abelian category. All complexes below are assumed to be objects
of Kom(A).
154     III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


    In what follows we will often give definitions of various morphisms and
then verify identities among them by applying them to "elements" as if we
were working with abelian groups or modules; see also comments to II.6.
    a) Fix an integer n and for any complex K e = (Ki, dk) define a new
complex K[n]e by (K[n])i = Kn+i, dK[nj = (-I)ndK. For a morphism of
complexes f : K e -+ U let J[n] : K[n]e -+ L[n]e coincide with f componen-
twise.
    It is clear that Tn : Kom(A) -+ Kom(A), Tn(Ke) = K[n]e, m(f) = f[n],
is a functor, called the tmnslation by n functor, which is an auto equivalence
of Kom(A). It induces an auto equivalence of any of categories Kom+(A),
Kom-(A), Komb(A) and of the corresponding derived categories.
    b) Let f : K e -+ L e be a morphism of complexes. A cone of f is the
following complex C(f):
      C(f)i = K[I]i EB Li, dC(f) (ki+l, li) = (-dKki+l, f (ki+l) + dLl i ) .

triangulated space by a transparent geometrical construction and have com-

be considered (up to the change of chains to cochains) as the cone of the
identity map.
    If f is a morphism of O-complexes (see III.1.4b.), then C(f) is a complex
... -+ 0 -+ K~ 1 -+ Lg -+ 0 ... In particular,

the same boundness condition. Moreover, if Ke and U lie in Kom(B), where
Be A is an additive subcategory, then C(f) and Cyl(f) also lie in Kom(B).

§4. Derived Category as the Localization
of Homotopic Category

1. Definition. Let A be an abelian category. The homotopic category K(A)
is defined as follows:

        ObK(A)           Ob Kom(A),
       Mor K(A)          Mor Kom(A)        modulo homotopic equivalence

(see Lemma III.l.2). By K+(A), K-(A), Kb(A) we denote full subcategories
of K(A) formed by complexes satisfying the corresponding boundness condi-
tions.

    Lemma IIL1.3 shows that K(A) is an additive category on which the func-
tors Hi are well defined. Hence the definition III.1.5 of a quasi-isomorphism
can be literally applied to K(A).

2. Proposition. The localization of K(A) by quasi-isomorphisms is
canonically isomorphic to the derived category D(A). The same is true for
K*(A) and D*(A) where * = +, -, or b.
Proof. Denote temporarily by D(A) the localization of K(A) by quasi-
isomorphisms. The composition Kom(A) --> K(A) --> D(A) maps quasi-
isomorphisms into isomorphisms; therefore, it factors through a functor
G : D(A) --> D(A). By construction, G is a bijection on objects. The in-
terpretation of morphisms in a localized category as classes of paths (see
IIL2.2) implies that G is surjective on morphisms, because each morphism in
K(A) can be lifted to a morphism in Kom(A).                               D

   It remains to show that G is injective on morphisms. From the description
of morphisms in III.2.2 one can easily see that the injectivity follows from
Lemma III.3.3 below:

3. Lemma. Let f, g : K e --> L e be homotopic to each other. Then Q(j) =
Q(g) in D(A).

Proof. Let f = 9 + dh + hd. Define the morphism c(h) : C(j) --> C(g) by

                    C(h) (ki+l,li) = (ki+1,li + h (k i+1)).

a morphism of complexes.
   Similarly, define the morphism of complexes cyl(h) : Cyl(j) --> Cyl(g) by

                cyl(h) (k i ,ki+1,li) = (k i ,ki+1,li +h (ki+1)) .

from Sect. III.3 for f and 9 respectively:

1. Morphisms in D(A) . Let A be an abelian category, I be a morphism
in Kom(A). By the definition of morphisms in D(A), I = 0 in D(A) iff there
exists a quasi-isomorphism s : Le --+ Me such that sl is homotopic to zero
(equivalently, there exists a quasi-isomorphism t : N e --+ K e such that It is
homotopic to zero).
   If I = 0 in D(A) then, clearly, Hn(f) = 0 for all n. The morphism of
complexes for the length 2 (for A = Ab)

(both band c map the generator into the generator, a and d multiply the
generator by 2) shows the converse is false. So we see that in the chain of
implications

    {I = 0 in Kom(A)}          =}   {f = 0 in K(A)}
                               =}   {I = 0 in D(A)} =} {Hn(f) = 0 for all n}
all implications are strict.

6. Homological Dimension
Theorem III.5.5 shows that, to some extent, the complexity of the derived
category D(A) can be measured by the following parameter.
    Homological dimension dh(A) is the maximum p such that there exist two
objects X, Y E Ob A with Ext~(X, Y) i= 0 (or 00 if there is no such p).
   Obviously, dh(A) ~ O. Categories with dh(A) = 0 are quite simple.
7. Proposition. The following statements are equivalent:
   a} dh(A) = 0,
   b} Ext~(X, Y) = 0 for all X, Y E Ob A.
   c} The category A is semi-simple (see III.2.3).
Proof a) =? b) is obvious.
   b) =? c) is proved as follows. Let
                         K- : 0 ----+ Y ----+ Z ----+ X ----+ 0
be an exact triple. By Theorem I1L5.5a), b) and by I1L5.4c) we get the exact
sequence of abelian groups
         o ----+ Hom(X, Y)         ----+    Hom(X, Z)
                                   ----+    Hom(X, X) ----+ Ext! (X, Y) = O.
Hence there exists h : X -+ Z whose image in Hom(X, X) is idx. It is easy
to see that K- is isomorphic to the following split exact triple:
                      o ----+ Y (i~0) Y EI1 1m h (0~1) X -+ 0
where k : X -+ 1m h is the canonical isomorphism.

8. Categories of Dimension 1

a) The category Ab is one-dimensional.
   b) The category K[x]-mod (where K is a field) is one-dimensional.
   It is easy to prove that these categories have the dimension ~ 1; indeed,
there are indecomposable exact triples
                            O---+Z~Z/mZ---+O,            m>1

      To prove that the dimension is exactly 1, we develop some technique.

14. Modules Over a Principal Ideal Ring

Let A be a commutative ring with unity without zero divisor; we assume that
any ideal in A is a principal on (for example, A may be Z or K[x]). Let C be
the category of A-modules of finite type. The following three classes of object
in C coincide:
    a) free modules; b) projective modules; c) torsion-free modules.
                                                              s:
    Hence any object has a projective resolution of length 2, so that dh C     s:

exact triple
                         0- A ~ A -         A/a - 0
determines a nonzero element of Ext 1 (A/a, A), so that dh C = 1.
   We have proved somewhat weaker result than the one stated in IIL5.8.
However, the assumption of the finiteness of type can be dropped.

15. Hilbert Theorem

In III.I.l we have stated the Hilbert syzygie theorem for modules over poly-
nomial rings k[tl, ... , tr]. In the next subsection we reformulate this theorem
in the category theory language. Being applied to modules over k[tl, ... , t r ],
this theorem gives somewhat weaker result that the classical syzygie theorem,
as we get a bound for the length of a projective resolution, and not of a free
one.
    In fact, any projective module of the finite type over a ring of polynomials
with coefficient in a field is free. This highly nontrivial result was conjectured
by Serre and proved independently by Quillen [5] and Suslin [1].
    Let A be an abelian category. Denote by A[T] the following category:

          Ob A[T] = { pairs(X, t) with X E Ob A, t E HomA(X, X)}.

   A morphism (X, t) -> (X', t') in A[T] is a morphism f : X -> X' in A
such that t' 0 f = f 0 t.
16. Theorem. a) A[T] is an abelian category.
    b) Let A has sufficiently many projective objects, and also all infinite
direct sums. Then for any (X, t) E Ob A[T] we have



     c) Under the above assumptions



     This theorem will be proved in III.5.18-III.5.19.

17. The Classical Hilbert Theorem

Let Ar be the category of modules over the ring k[h, ... ,tr]. Define the
functor

by
         Fr(X, t) = X     with the previous action of k[tl, ... , tr-d and
                          with tr acting as T.
One can easily verify that Fr is an equivalence of categories. If k is a field,
we can start the induction on r from r = 0 using dh A = 0 and obtain from
Theorem III.5.16 that dh Ar = r.
    One can of course take for k an arbitrary (not necessarily commutative)
ring; in the definition of k[tl, ... , t r ] one always assumes that tl, ... , tr com-
mute with k and with each other.

18. A[T] is an Abelian Category

We must verify axioms A.I-A.4 from II.5. Axioms A.I and A.2 are clear
because HOmA[T] ((X,t),(X',t')) is a subgroup of HomA(X, X'). Next, let
(X, t), (X', t') be two objects from A[T]. Define their direct sum as (X, t) E9
(X', t') = (X E9 X', t E9 t'). The construction of morphisms (II.19) and the
verification of (11.20) from II.5.4 is standard.
    Let us show now that A[T] contains kernels and cokernels of all mor-
phisms. Below we will denote by the same letter morphism (X, t) ---- (X', t')
in A[T] and the corresponding morphism X ---- X' in A. Let (K, k) be the
kernel of I in A. Then Itk = t'kl = 0 so that there exists a unique morphism
s : K ---- K' with tk = ks (see the diagram below)

                      K      ~      X     L     X,   ~      C



                      K      ~      X     L     X'   ~ C
     Hence (K, s) E Ob A[T] and k : (K, s) ---- (X, t) is a morphism in A[T].
We leave to the reader an easy verification that ((K, s), k) is the kernel of I
in A[T]. Similarly one can construct the cokernel (( C, v), c) of a morphism I
in A[T].
     Let us prove now the existence of the canonical decomposition of a mor-
phism I : (X,t) ---- (X',t) in A[T]. Let (I,u) = Coker kerl, (I',u') =
kerCoker I and i : (X,t) ---- (I,u), j : (I',u') ---- (X',t') be the corre-
sponding morphisms in A[T]. By II.5.11b there exists a unique morphism
l : (I, u) ---- (I', u') with I = j 0 l 0 i. As I = Coker ker I and l' = ker Coker I
in A, l : I ---- I' is an isomorphism in A. Therefore l : (I, u) ---- (I', u') is an
isomorphism in A[T] and A.4 is satisfied for A[T].

20. Derived Category and Injective Resolutions

The last topic of this section is the characterization of the derived category
in terms of injective resolutions.
    Let I be the full subcategory of an abelian category formed by all in-
jective objects. Let K+(I) be the category whose objects are bounded from
the left complexes of injective objects and whose morphisms are morphisms
of complexes modulo homotopic equivalence. Let K+(I) ~ D+(A) be the
natural functor.
21. Theorem. a) The above functor is an equivalence of K+ (I) with a full
subcategory of D+(A).
   b) If A has sufficiently many injective objects, then the functor yields an
equivalence between K+(I) and D+(A).

22. Plan of the Proof

First we show that one can apply Proposition III. 2. 10 to the pair of categories
K+(I) C K+(A) and to the class of all quasi-isomorphisms in K+(A). To
do this we have to verify that
A. Quasi-isomorphisms in K+(I) form a localizing system Sr.
B. The condition b 2 of Proposition III.2.10 is satisfied.
   After that we get from Proposition III.2.10 that K+(I)[Sil] is a full sub-
   category of K+(A)[S-l] = D+(A) (the last equality is proved in Propo-
   sition III.4.2).
C. Any element of Sr is an isomorphism. Hence
                              K+(I)[Sil] = K+(I).
D. If A has sufficiently many injective objects, then any object of D+(A) is
   isomorphic to one from K+ (I).

23. Proof of III.5.22A

It was proved in Theorem III.5.4 that quasi-isomorphisms form a localizing
system in K+(A). The proof there was done by constructing some objects
via commutative diagrams. These constructions were explicit and all new
complexes were constructed as cones of morphisms of previous ones. As direct
sums of injective objects are injective, we obtain complexes in K+ (I) when
starting from complexes in K+ (I). Therefore, axioms of the localizing system
are satisfied for quasi-isomorphisms in K+(I).

24. Proof of III.5.22B, C

We prove the following statement (which is stronger than the condition b 2 of
Proposition III.2.1O)
180    III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


   (*) Let s : I -+ K be a quasi-isomorphism between an object from K+ (I)
and an object from K+(A). Then there exists a morphism of complexes t :
K -+ I such that t 0 s is homotopic to id J .
    Using Lemma III.3.3 we construct the distinguished triangle I ~ K _
C(s) ~ 1(1) in K+(A). Since s is a quasi-isomorphism, its cone C(s) is
acyclic. We prove now that any morphism from an acyclic complex to a
left bounded complex formed by injective elements, is homotopic to the zero

one may assume that we begin with C 1 :

§6. Derived Functors
1. Motivations

We have already mentioned in IIL1.6 that some of the most important ad-
ditive functors P in abelian categories, such as Horn, 181, r, are not exact,
and to restore their exactness we have to redefine them. More explicitly, let
P : A -+ 8 be a left (resp. right) exact functor between abelian categories. In
this section we define and study its extension RP : D+(A) -+ D+(8) (resp.
LP : D- (A) -+ D- (8)) which will be called right (resp. left) derived functor
of P.
    The functors RP (resp. LP) will be exact in the following sense: they map
distinguished triangles into distinguished ones.
    In particular, if we define classical derived functors by


then any exact triple 0 -+ A -+ B -+ C -+ 0 in A yields a long exact
cohomology sequence
      ... ~ Rip(A) ~ Rip(B) ~ Rip(C) ~ R i +1P(A) ~ ...
and similarly for L.
186     III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


    But what should be an appropriate way to extend F to complexes? The
first and most obvious idea is to make F to act on complexes component-
wise. In any case, such an extension transforms homotopic morphisms into
homotopic ones, so that we obtain functors K*(F) : K*(A) -> K*(8),
* = 0,+,-,b.
2. Proposition. Assume F to be exact.
    a) K*(F) transforms quasi-isomorphisms into quasi-isomorphisms so that
it induces a functor D*(F) : D*(A) -> D*(8).
    b) D* (F) is an exact functor, i. e. it transforms distinguished triangles
into distinguished triangles.

Proof. a) Let us prove first that K*(F) maps an acyclic (i.e. quasi-isomorphic
to 0) complex Ke into an acyclic one. Let Bi = ker     dk
                                                      = 1m dtl. The functor
F maps the exact sequence



into the exact sequence

                o ~ F(Bi) ~ F(Ki) 0t) F(BHl) ~ O.
From d i = e H1 0 pi we get F(d i ) = F(e H1 0 F(pi). Moreover, F(pi) is a
monomorphism, while F(e H1 ) is an epimorphism. Hence F(Bi) is isomorphic
to the image of F(di ) and F(Bi+l) is isomorphic to the cokernel of F(d H1 )
so that F(Ke) is an acyclic complex.
    Let us note that if f : Ke -> L· is a morphism of complexes then there
exists a canonical isomorphism of F(C(j)) onto C(F(j)) (see III.3.2). By
III.3.6, f is an isomorphism if and only if C(j) is acyclic. In this case, as we
have just proved, F(C(j)) is also acyclic and F(j) is a quasi-isomorphism.
    b) Looking through the definitions in III.3.2 and III.3.3 we see that any
additive functor F maps the cylinder of f into the cylinder of F(j), and the
main diagram of Lemma III.3.3 into a similar diagram. Hence any additive
functor F maps a triangle of the form

K*(F) maps distinguished (Le. quasi-isomorphic to the above) triangles into
distinguished triangles.                                                  0


3. Adapted Classes of Objects

The basic idea underlying the construction of derived functors RF and LF
in the general complex case is that we apply F term by term, but not to
every complex. Instead we select some special representatives in equivalence
                                                     6. Derived Functors    187


classes of quasi-isomorphic complexes. For example, as we will see later on,
to compute   Rr                    r
                 we have to apply to (bounded from the left) complexes of
injective sheaves, and to compute M 0 • we have to use (bounded from the
right) complexes of flat modules.
    To proceed in the general case we introduce the following definition. A
class of objects R C Ob A is said to be adapted to a (left or right) exact
functor F if it is stable under finite direct sums and satisfies the following
two conditions:
a) For a left exact F: F maps any acyclic complex from Kom+(R) into an
   acyclic complex.
   For a right exact F: F maps any acyclic complex from Kom - (R) into an
   acyclic complex.
b) For a left exact F: any object from A is a subobject of an object from R.
   For a right exact F: any object from A is a quotient of object from R.
    (If this condition is satisfied we will say that R is sufficiently large, or
that there is sufficiently many elements in R.)
    Let us note that if F is exact, then the first part of Proposition III.6.2
implies that any class R satisfying the condition b) (in particular, the class
of all objects of A) is adapted to F.

4. Proposition. Let R be a class of objects adapted to a left exact functor
F : A ~ Band Sn be a class of quasi-isomorphisms in K+(R). Then Sn is
a localizing class of morphisms in K+(R) and the canonical functor



is an equivalence of categories.
    A similar statement (with K+ and D+ replaces by K- and D- respec-
tively) holds for a right exact F.

Proof To prove that Sn is a localizing system we can argue similarly to
III.5.23.
    Next, repeating the proof of III.5.22d (see III.5.25) with I replaced by R
(this is possible because in III.5.25 we have used only the fact that any object
of A can be embedded into an object from I, but not that objects from I
are injective) we see that
    (*) For any complex K e E K+(R) there exists a quasi-isomorphism
t : K e ~ R e with R e E K+(R). Hence to prove that the functor

K+(R).
   For a left exact F the proof is similar (one must use condition bt).       0

5. The Construction of the Derived Functor
Under the assumptions of Proposition III.6.4 we define the derived functor
RF of a left exact functor F on objects ofthe category K+(n)[Sil] term by
term:
                RF(Ke)i = F(Ki) for K e E Ob K+(n).
   As F maps acyclic objects from K+(n) into acyclic ones, arguments
similar to those used in the proof of Proposition III.6.2 show that quasi-
isomorphisms in K+(n) are mapped into quasi-isomorphisms. So we can
consider RF as a functor from K+(n)[Sil] to D+(13).
   To complete the definition of RF we choose an equivalence D+(A) -+
K+(n)[Sil] that is inverse to the natural embedding and define
                           RF(Ke) = RF(4">(Ke)).
   Similarly we can construct the derived functor LF for a right exact functor
F (replacing everywhere K+ and D+ by K- and D-).
   This construction contains some ambiguity: the choice of 4"> and, more
important, the choice of n, are non-unique. It is rather clear in what sense the
construction does not depend on 4">. To state and to prove the independence
on the choice of n we need a formal definition of the derived functor via a
universal property.
6. Definition. The derived functor of an additive left exact functor F : A -+
13 is a pair consisting of an exact functor RF : D+(A) -+ D+(13) and a
morphism of functors CF : QT3 0 K+(F) -+ RF 0 QA:

F+(13) and any morphism of functor C : QT3 0 K+(F) -+ Go QA there exists
a unique morphism of functors.,., : RF -+ G making the diagram

commutative.

    Similarly, the derived functor of a right exact functor F : A - t 8 is a

of functors E:F : LF 0 QA - t Ql3 0 K- (F) satisfying the universal property
similar to (III.39) (with a morphism of functors rJ : G - t LF).
   Let us remark that if F is exact then, by Proposition III.6.2b) both RF
and LF coincide with the result of the term by term application of F to
complexes.

7. Uniqueness of the Derived Functor

Let (RF, E:F) and (RF, €F) be two derived functors for F. By definition, there
exist unique morphisms RF ~ RF ~ RF with the required commuta-
tivity properties. These commutativity properties imply, in particular, that
rJ' 0 rJ and rJ 0 rJ' are automorphisms of functors RF and RF respectively.
Hence, by the uniqueness, they are identity isomorphisms, so that rJ and rJ'
are mutually inverse isomorphisms of functors which, moreover, are uniquely
defined.

construction from III. 6. 5.

    In application it is sometimes useful to know that some classes of objects
are adapted to all functors that are exact from a fixed side.

reader can easily prove a similar result for right exact functors.

12. Theorem. If A contains sufficiently many injective objects, then the
class I of all injective objects is adapted to any left exact functor F.

Proof. We have to verify that F maps acyclic complexes from Kom + (I) into
acyclic ones. Let r be such a complex. The zero morphism 0 : r ~ r
is a quasi-isomorphism. By III.5.24, it is homotopic to idlo. Hence the zero
morphism of F(Ie) is homotopic to idF(I"), so that F(r) is acyclic.        D


13. Classical Derived Functors

a) A functor H from a derived (or homotopic) category to an abelian category
is said to be cohomological if for any distinguished triangle X ~ Y ~ Z ~
X[I] the sequence

      ... ----+   H(Ti X) ----+ H(Tiy) ----+ H(Ti Z) ----+ H(Ti+l X) ----+ ...
is exact. For example, H = HO is a cohomological functor (see II1.3.5 and
1.6.8). Another example: H = Hom(U, e) (see IV.1.3).
    b) Let F be a left exact (resp. right exact) functor between two abelian
categories. Then RiF = HO(Ti(RF)) = Hi(RF) (resp. LiF = Hi(LF)) is
called the classical i-th derived junctor for F. One can easily see that Ri F = 0
for i <0, ROF = F (resp. £iF = 0 for i > 0, LOF = F).
    Let us give some examples.

14. Exti as a Derived Functor

Let A be an abelian category with sufficiently many injective objects. Fix
an object X and consider the functor Hom(X,e) from A to the category
Ab of abelian groups. This functor is left exact and we can consider the
derived functor RHom(X, e). Let us show that there exists an isomorphism
of functors
                       Exti(X,e) ~ RiHom(X,e).
Indeed, to compute Exti(X, Y) we replace Y by its injective resolution Y ~
Iy so that (see ex. II1.5.5b)

                          Exti(X, Y) = HOmD(A) (X, ly[i]).

By the injectivity of I y we have (see ex. III.5.5a)

                      HOmD(A) (X, ly[i]) = HOmK(A) (X, ly[i]).
                                                        6. Derived Functors      195


On the other hand, by a construction of the derived functor,
                       Ri Hom(X, Y) = Hi (Hom(X, Iy)).
Now we must use the following general construction. Define "inner Hom" in
the category Kom(A) by the formula
                      Homn(A e , Be) =   II Hom (Ai, Bi+n)
                                         iEZ
               df = dB 0 f - (-It f 0 dA,       f E Homn(A e , Be).
Then we have the following canonical isomorphisms:
                  Zi Home(A e , Be) = HOmKom(A) (Ae, Be [i]),
    Bi Home (A e , Be) = {morphisms homotopic to the zero morphism},
so that
                   Hi Home(A-, Be) = HOmK(A) (Ae, Be [i]).
   Similarly, one can prove that if A contains sufficiently many projective
objects then Ext i (., Y) ~ RiHom(., Y) as functors of the first argument
(they are contravariant so that we have to consider them as being defined on
AD).
   To formulate the functoriality properties of Ext i and Ri Hom in both
arguments simultaneously one should develop an appropriate formalism of
derived multifunctors.

15. Functors Tori

    Earlier in this section we used the existence of an adapted class to prove

    Let, say, RF exists. An object X is said to be F-acyclic if Ri F(X) = 0
for all i # O.
16. Theorem. a) An adapted to F class of objects exists if and only if the

object is a subobject of an F -acyclic one.
    b) If Z is sufficiently large then it contains any adapted to F class and
any sufficiently large subclass of Z is adapted.
    c) If Z is sufficiently large then it contains all injective objects.

In Ex. 1-5 we give another construction of derived functors. This construc-

however now RF will take values not in the derived category itself but in
some extension of it.
1. Category of Coindices. A category I is said to be a category of
coindices if it is small, non-empty, and satisfies the following conditions:
    a) I is connected, i.e. any two objects can be joint by a sequence of
morphisms (directions are irrelevant).
    b) Any pair of morphisms j' +- i ---> j can be embedded into a commuta-
tive square

k such that w 0 u = w 0 v.

    A category .:J is said to be a category of indices if .:J0 is a category of
coindices.

3. Inductive Limits and Localization. Let S be a localizing class of
morphisms in a category C (see III.2.6). It is said to be saturated if any
morphism which is both a right divisor of some morphism in S and a left
divisor of some morphism in S, itself belongs to S.
    For any object X E ObC the category Ix of morphisms s : X ~ X', s E

into isomorphisms. Hence they determine canonical functors C[S-I] ~ IndC
and C[S-I] ~ ProC.

7. Projective and Injective Resolutions. Let A be an abelian category.
By a left projective resolution of a complex Ae E Ob Kom(A) we mean
a quasi-isomorphism pe ~ Ae with all pi'S projective in A. Similarly we
define a right injective resolution.
    Theorem 111.4.4 (and its analogue for projective resolutions) claims that
any object Ae E Ob Kom+(A) (resp. Ae E Ob Kom- (A)) has a right in-
jective (resp. left projective) resolution which is unique up to a homotopic
equivalence.
    Without the bound ness assumptions the uniqueness may fail: for A =
(Z/4)-mod the complex
                   pe: ... - t Z/4 ~ Z/4 ~ Z/4 - t . . .
is acyclic and consists of free Z/4-modules. Hence it is a left projective resolu-
tion of the zero complex. However, the morphism pe ~ oe is not a homotopic
equivalence: tensoring with Z/2 we obtain the complex
               pe ® Z/2 : ... - t Z/2 ~ Z/2 ~ Z/2 - t . . .
which has nonzero cohomology and, therefore, is not homotopic to oe.
8. A complex pe is said to be K -projective if for any acyclic complex A e
the complex of abelian groups Home (pe , A e) is acyclic. Similarly one can
define K-injective complexes r (using Home(Ae, r)). Prove the following
properties of K-projective complexes.
    a) Let Ae be a O-complex (i.e. Ai = 0 for i :f. 0). Then Ae is K-projective
if and only if AO is projective in A.
    b) If any two vertices of a distinguished triangle in D(A) are K-projective,
so is the third.
    c) For pe E Ob Kom(A) the following conditions are equivalent:
(i) p. is K-projective.
(ii) For any Ae E Ob Kom(A) the natural homomorphism
                   HOmK(A)(pe,A e ) - t HomD(A)(pe,A e )
      is an isomorphism.
(iii) Any quasi-isomorphism s : Ae ~ pe admits a right inverse t : pe ~ Ae
      in K(A).
    d) By a K-projective (left) resolution of a complex Ae we mean a quasi-
isomorphism pe ~ Ae with a K-projective pe. Prove that a K-projective
resolution, whenever it exists, is unique up to a homotopic equivalence. If
Ae E Ob Kom-(A) and A has sufficiently many projective objects then a
K-projective resolution is a left projective resolution of Ae.
    Similar results hold for K-injective (left) resolutions.
9. In view of the property 8.d we can use K-projective and K-injective
resolutions to compute values of derived functors on unbounded complexes.
In particular, to compute RHom(Ae, Be) we can use either a K-projective
resolution of Ae or a K-injective resolution of Be.
200       III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


    As to the existence of K-resolutions, the following results are proved in
Spaltenstein [1].
    a) Let R be an associative ring with unity and A = R-mod. Then any
complex Ae E Ob Kom(A) admits a K-projective and a K-injective resolu-
tion.
    b) Let V be a sheaf of rings on a topological space X and A be the
category of sheaves of V-modules. Then any complex Ae E Ob Kom(A)
admits a K-projective resolution.
10. Similarly one can define and prove the existence of K-flat resolutions
(used to compute derived functors of the tensor product), K-soft resolutions
(used to compute Rh, see IlL8), and so on.


§7. Derived Functor of the Composition.
Spectral Sequence
1. Theorem. Let A, B, C be three abelian categories, F : A -+ B, G: B -+ C
be two additive left exact functors. Let R,A C Ob A (resp. R8 C Ob B) be a
class of objects adapted to F (resp. to G). Assume that F(R,A) C R8. Then
the derived functors RF, RG, R( G 0 F) : D+ (.) -+ D+ (.) exist and the
natural morphism of functors R( G 0 F) -+ RG 0 RF is an isomorphism.
Proof. The definition of an adapted class in IlL6.3 and the conditions of the
theorem show that R,A is adapted not only to F, but to GoF as well. Hence,
RF, RG and R(GoF) exist and to compute them we can use the construction
from IIL6.6.
   Next, RF and RG are exact functors. Hence R(GoF) is also exact and the
morphism E : R( G 0 F) -+ RG 0 RF is defined by the universality property.
   For Ke E Ob Kom+(R,A) the morphism E(Ke) : R(G 0 F)(Ke) -+ RG 0
RF(Ke) is an isomorphism. Since any object of D+(A) is isomorphic to such
an object K e , E is an isomorphism of functors.                            0

      A similar result holds for right exact functors.

2. Example. Let A, B, C be the categories of sheaves of abelian groups on
topological spaces U, V, W respectively and let F = fe, G = ge, where
f : X -+ Y, g : Y -+ Z are continuous maps. In the classical situation
W is a point, so that G and G 0 F are functors of sections on V and U
respectively. Instead of R( Go F), and RG 0 RF, the classical theory considers
Ri(G 0 F) (:F) and RPG(RqF(:F)). These groups are connected by the Leray
spectral sequence, which is a rather complicated algebraic structure (about
the Leray spectral sequence see Sect. IlL8, in particular Theorem IlL8.3d).
    In our more general setting we can also construct a spectral sequence that
would encode, with some loss of information, the functor isomorphism E.
                7. Derived Functor of the Composition. Spectral Sequence     201


    To be more precise, let us consider the action of E on O-complexes, that
is, on objects X E Ob A. Then E yields an isomorphism in B:


The idea that leads to spectral sequences is that we would like to approximate
the complex RF(X) by complexes with smaller number of non-trivial coho-
mology objects. In the simplest case, when RF(X) has only one nontrivial
cohomology Rk F(X), we have RF(X) ~ Rk F(X)[k] in the derived category
(see Proposition III.5.2) and since RG is exact, we have an isomorphism


In the general case the properties of En(x) are described in the following
manner:
    a) We define some filtration on Rn(G 0 F)(X), such that
    b) the consecutive quotients of this filtration are obtained from
RPG(RqF(X)), p + q = n, by a series of steps, each consisting in taking
a sub quotient.
   It is this algebraic structure that is called a spectral sequence. We give its
formal definition consisting of two main parts: a) abstract spectral sequence;
b) construction of the spectral sequence for the composition of two functors.

3. Spectral Sequence

Let A be an abelian category. A spectral sequence in A is a family of objects
in A of the form E = (E?,q, En), p, q, r E Z, r :::: 1, and a family ofmorphisms
between these objects with some properties that we will describe shortly. But
first we will say a few words about how one may try to look at all this data.
      The reader is advised to imagine a stack of square-lined paper with each
square numbered by a pair of integers (p, q) E Z2. An object E?,q is assumed
to sit in the (p, q)-th square at the r-th sheet. Objects En sit at the last,
"transfinite" sheet, and occupy the whole diagonal p + q = n.
      Now we describe morphisms and the conditions they should satisfy.
      a) On the r-th sheet we have morphisms d~,q : E?,q --+ E~+r,q-r+l. For
r = 1 they act from a square to its right neighbour, for r = 2 they act by
a chess springer move (one square down and two squares to the right). For
r :::: 3 we get a generalized springer.
      Condition: d; = 0; more explicitly, d~+r,q-r+l 0 d~,q = 0 for all p, q, r.
      Using (E?,q, d~,q) we can construct cohomology of the r-th sheet:
                      HP,q (Er) = ker d~,q lIm d~+r,q-r+l.
   The following data are included into the definition of E:
   b) Isomorphisms a~,q : Hp,q (Er) --+ E~:;!1 .
   Usually we will assume that on the (r + l)-th sheet we have just coho-
mology of the r-th sheet and a~,q are identities.
202     III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


    The main condition an Q:~,q is the existence of the limit objects E~q. The
simplest way to ensure this, which usually goes well in applications, is the
following:
    c) For any pair (p, q) there exists ro such that d~,q = 0, d~+r,q-r+1 = 0
for r 2: ro. In this case Q:~,q identify all E~,q for r 2: ro and we will denote
this object by E~q.
    At this moment on the transfinite sheet (r = 00) we have objects E~q
and objects En along the diagonal p + q = n. The last collection of data
connects these two types of objects.
    d) A decreasing regular filtration ... :J FP En :J FP+1 En :J ... on each En
and isomorphisms (3p,q : E~q --+ FP Ep+q / FP+1 Ep+q are given. (Recall that
                                                 n

                                                  P                P

converges to (En) or that (En) is the limit of (E~,q).
     Let us stress once more that the components of one spectral sequence E

4. Remarks About Boundness Conditions

a) Working with complexes that are bounded from one side we usually get
spectral sequences with non-zero objects placed in only one quadrant (?) (the
region of the form, say p > Po, q > qo).
    Let the only non-zero E~,q be those in the quadrant I. Then the con-
dition III. 7.3c is automatically satisfied, because for r > ro(p, q) either the
beginning or the end of arrows d~,q, d~+r,q-r+1 lies outside quadrant. More-
over, filtrations on En are automatically finite: FP En = 0 for p < p_ (n),
FP En = En for p > p+(n). The same holds, of course, for spectral sequences
in quadrant III.
    b) The larger the number of zero objects E~,q and of zero morphisms d~,q,
the better a spectral sequence may serve as a computational tool. One special
case has its own name: E is said to be degenerate at E r , if d~;q = 0 for r' 2: r
and for all p, q. In this case we have obviously Eg,;q = E~,q.
    c) Traditionally people use spectral sequences when they wish to learn
something about En while knowing Ei,q or E~,q.
    Let us show that invariants similar to the Euler characteristic can be com-
puted explicitly even in the case when we know nothing about differentials
d~,q.

7. Theorem. Let under the conditions of Theorem III. 7.1 above the class
RA coincides with the class IA of all injective objects in A and let the class
Is be sufficiently large. Then for any X E Ob A there exists a spectral
sequence with
                           E~,q = RPG(RqF(X))

converging to Rn(G 0 F)(X). It is functorial in X.

Plan of the Proof

Objects RqF(X) are cohomology objects of the complex RF(X) = F(Ix) ,
where Ix is an injective resolution of X. To compute R(G 0 F)(X) = RG 0
RF(X) we have to apply G term by term to a complex from Kom+(Rs)
which is quasi-isomorphic to F(Ix). However, for F(Is) C Rs we can take
F(Ix) itself so that Rn(G 0 F)(X) are cohomology objects of the complex
Go F(Ix).
    On the other hand, to compute RPG(RqF(X)) we must apply G term by
term to resolutions in Kom+(Rs) of objects RqF(X).
    These two resolutions are related via a double complex that is called
the Cartan-Eilenberg resolution of the complex K e = F(Ix ). This Cartan-
Eilenberg resolution consists of the following data.
a) A double complex (Lij) with differentials d[, d II of bidegree (1,0) and
   (0,1) respectively; it satisfies the conditions Lij = 0 for j < 0 or i S; 0,
   Lij are injective (the main definitions concerning double complexes see in
   III. 7.8 below).
b) A morphism of complexes c : K e -+ Le,o.
    To state the conditions imposed in the above data we note that (Lij)
gives rise to the following complexes:

            o ~       Ki ~ Li,o ~ Li,l ~ ...
            o ~       Bi(Ke) ~ B}(Le,o) ~ Bi(Le,l) ~ .. .
                                                                          (III. 52)
            o ~       Zi(K e ) ~ Z}(Le,o) ~ Z}(Le,l) ~ .. .
            o ~       Hi(Ke) ~ H}(Le,o) ~ H}(Le,l) ~ .. .

We require that

c) All these complexes are acyclic.
208     III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


d) Exact triples
               o -+      B}(Le,j) - + Z}(Le,j) - + H}(Le,j) - + 0
               o -+      Z}(Le,j) - + Lij - + B}+1(Le,j) - + 0

   split.
    Under these conditions objects BI, ZI, HI are injective, so that the com-
plexes (III.52) are injective resolutions of Ke, Be(Ke ), ze(Ke), He(K e )
respectively. In particular, R!'G (RqF(X)) can be thought of as the p-th co-
homology of the complex D(HJ(Le,e)). On the other hand, Ke = F(Ix)
is quasi-isomorphic to the diagonal complex SL of L (see III.7.8) so that
Rn(G 0 F)(X) = Hn (G (F (Ix ))) is isomorphic to Hn(SL). Moreover, L has
a filtration Le,?k and the term E2 of the spectral sequence corresponding to

In the conditions of the previous corollary objects RiG(K·) (or Hi(G(SL)))

cause G transforms direct sums into direct sums). By Proposition III.7.10 the

15. Completion of the Proof of Theorem 111.7.7

Let Ke = F(Ix) = RF(X), where Ix is an injective resolution of X.

    The functoriality of the spectral sequence in X follows from the functori-
ality of Ix in X (Theorem III. 1.3 and Remark IIL1.4c) and from functoriality
of Cartan-Eilenberg resolution (Proposition IIL7.11).

3. Exact Pairs and Spectral Sequences. Another (historically earlier)
method to construct a spectral sequence is related to so-called exact pairs. We
will work in a category of modules over a fixed ring R; however, all definitions
and results can be easily generalized to an arbitrary abelian category A.
    a) An exact pair is a collection (D, E, i,j, k) consisting of two modules
and three morphisms:
                                        i
                             D                'D

§8. Sheaf Cohomology
1. Proposition. Let X be a topological space, R be a sheaf of unitary rings
on X. Then any sheaf of R-modules can be embedded into an injective sheaf
of R-modules.
Proof. Let F be a sheaf of R-modules (say, left). For any point x E X we
can construct a monomorphism Fx '--+ I(x) of Rx-modules, where I(x) is
injective over Rx. Let us define now a sheaf of R-modules I by

                         I(U) =   IT I(x),    U open in X
                                  xEU

(with obvious restrictions). We have a canonical embedding F ---- I. The
injectivity of I would follow from the existence, for any sheaf 9 of R-modules,
of the canonical homomorphism

                         Homn{Q,I) =     IT Homnx{Qx'!(x)).
                                        xEX

Denoting by Ix the stalk of I at a point x, we have the canonical homo-
morphism Vx : Ix ---- I(x) of Rx-modules, and the family {v x } determines a
homomorphism of the left-hand side of the required identity into the right-
hand side. We leave to the reader the proof that this homomorphism is an
isomorphism.                                                               0

4. Tensor Products and Flat Sheaves

Let R = R x be a sheaf of unitary rings on a topological space X. For any

                   • r'i9N: mod-R ---- SAb,

can prove that this functor is right exact.

reader can easily verify that N is flat if and only if its stalk N x at any point
x E X is a flat module over the ring Rx. In particular, for any open U C X
the sheaf Ru defined by

(see also 11.6.7) is flat.

7. Higher Direct Images with Compact Support

Up to the end of this section we will consider only sheaves of abelian groups
on locally compact topological spaces satisfying the first count ability axiom.
Later we will impose some additional conditions about the finiteness of the
dimension of involved spaces. They will not, however, be too restrictive; in
particular, they hold for all topological manifolds. In this situation to any map
f : X --> Y we will associate functors Rf! and J' on appropriate categories.

                      Rf, : D+(SAbx) ---+ D+(SAby).

14. Dimension

In general, functors Rf, transform bounded complexes of sheaves on X into
unbounded complexes of sheaves on Y. However, for a wide class of spaces
the boundedness property is preserved.
   By the dimension dime X of a locally compact space X we shall mean the
smallest n such that for any sheaf FE SAbx we have H~(X, F) = 0 for all
i > n. We skip here proofs of the following properties of dime X (see Iversen
[1 J):
228      III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


a) Let
                  o ----+ :F ----+ CO ----+ . . . ----+ en- 1 ----+ en ----+ 0
   be an exact sequence of abelian groups on a locally compact topological
   space X with dime X ~ n and with eO, ... , en- 1 soft. Then en is also
   soft.
b) dime IRn = n.
c) Let Y be an open or a closed subset of X. Then dime Y ~ dime X.
d) dime X can be computed locally: if each point x E X has a neighbourhood
   U with dime U ~ n, then dime X ~ n.
    Properties b )-d) imply, in particular, that the majority of reasonable
topological spaces X (in particular, topological manifolds and geometric re-
alizations of finite-dimensional simplicial sets, see Sect. 1.2) have finite di-
mension dime X.
e) Let f : X --+ Y be a map of locally compact topological spaces with
   dime X ~ n. Then RP f!:F = 0 for p > n and for any :F E SAb x .
f) In the conditions of e) Rf! can be considered as a functor from Db(SAbx)
   to Db(SAby ) and from D-(SAbx ) to D-(SAby).

15. Inverse Image with Compact Support

By the general ideology (see 11.6.17), for any map f : X --+ Y the inverse
image J' with compact support should be defined as a functor from the
category of sheaves on Y to the category of sheaves on X that is adjoint to
f! : SAb x --+ SAby. However, for a general f the functor f! does not possess
a right adjoint functor, and to define f! we have to pass to derived categories.
Moreover, we have to assume X and Y to be finite-dimensional.

25. Properties of f'
a) The construction of J' and Theorem III.8.16 can be generalized to the case
when the category SAb is replaced by the category of sheaves of R-modules,
where R is a fixed noetherian ring (in particular, a field).
    b) Formula (1II.61) (see III.8.13) implies that (gf)! = g! 0 f! for two
continuous maps f: X ~ Y, g: Y ~ Z.
    c) If f : X ~ Y is an embedding of an open or of a closed subset, then
the right adjoint functor for f! exists already on the level of the category of
sheaves, so that we do not need derived categories. Namely, if f : U ~ Y
is an open embedding, then the right adjoint to f! : SAbu ~ SAbx is the
restriction r : SAbx ~ SAb u · On the other hand, if f : X ~ Y is a closed
embedding, then the right adjoint to f! : SAb x ~ SAby is the functor "Yx
of "sections supported on X" defined as follows: let U eX, V c Y be open
sets with U = V n Y. Then
                  r(U,"YxF) = {s E r(V,F), supp seX}.
We leave to the reader the verification of the fact that r(V, "Yx F) is well
defined, "Yx F is a sheaf, and of the required conjugacy propertis.
    To conclude this section we consider the situation which is, in some sense,
opposite to the one in c) above. Namely, we assume that f : X ~ pt is the
map to a point.

26. The Dualizing Complex
Let Y be a point. Then sheaves of Yare just abelian groups, and we will
denote by Z E D+(SAbyX) = D+(Ab) the O-complex with the zero compo-

1)x  = J' (Z), where f : X ~ pt. The complex      1)x  is called the dualizing com-

duality)
                   RHom(Rrc(X,Fe),Z) ~ RHom(F.,1)>'').                       (111.67)

if X is a stratified space whose strata are topological manifolds, then the co-
homology sheaves of    1)x    are constructible with respect to this stratification.
    The structure of  1)x   is the most simple in the case when X is nonsingular.

28. Remarks

Replacing Z with an arbitrary noetherian ring R we can obtain analogs of
the above corollary. In particular, if R = k is a field and X is a topological
236      III. Derived Categories and Derived Functors


manifold without boundary, then Wx = TX is the sheaf of k-orientations of
X (in particular, the constant sheaf k if X is oriented or if char k = 2). If
X is a manifold with boundary ax, then Wx = i!T, where T is the sheaf of
k-orientations of X - ax and i : X - ax -+ X is the inclusion.
    Taking cohomology of both sides of (111.67) we can express the Poincare
duality in a more standard form (if k is a field) by saying that for any sheaf
of k-modules F on X there exists a canonical isomorphism

                                                                      (III. 70)

Denoting by Ix : H,{)(X,wx) -+ k the fundamental class of X, i.e. the preim-
age of 1 E Hom(wx,wx) under the isomorphism (111.70) for i = n = dimX,
F = wx, we can express (III. 70) as the composition of the canonical pairing
Extn-i(F,wx) x H~(X,F) -+ H'{)(X,wx) with Ix.

§ 1. Thiangulated Categories

1. Axioms

Let V be an abelian category. A structure of triangulated category on V is
defined by specifying data a) and b) which satisfy axioms TRI-TR4 below.
   a) Additive automorphism T : V --) V called the translation functor.
    As in III.3.4, we will write X[nJ for Tn(x) and I[nJ for Tn(f). Now we can
literally repeat parts a) and b) of definition III.3.4 introducing in V triangles

and morphisms of triangles

The last part of Definition III.3.4 should be given axiomatically. Namely,
among all triangles in V we should distinguish:
   b) The class of distinguished triangles.
    As we will see, the following axioms give rather satisfactory axiomatic
description of the properties one needs in working with the triangles defined
in III.3.4c).

   TRl. a) X ~ X ----- 0 ----- X[IJ is a distinguished triangle.
   b) Any triangle isomorphic to a distinguished one is itself distinguished.
   c) A ny morphism X ~ Y can be completed to a distinguished triangle
X~Y~Z~X[IJ.

   TR2. A triangle X ~ Y ~ Z ~ X[IJ is distinguished if and only if
the triangle Y ~ Z ~ X[IJ ~J Y[IJ is distinguished.

2. Remarks About the Formal Structure of the Axioms

a) Axiom TR2 implies that any distinguished triangle can be canonically
included into a helix such that any three consecutive morphisms form a dis-
tinguished triangle.
                                                         1. Triangulated Categories   241




                            ._/~.
                                           •



                                -----T-- •

                                           •
                            •   -,-~---=U.L:1::.L-_-T   __ •




    Any morphism of triangles generates two helices chained together by hor-
izontal arrows. Axioms TR2 and TR3 imply that given two neighbouring
horizontal arrows forming a commutative square, one can construct a mor-
phism of helices.
    b) One can consider a diagram of type "upper cap" as a morphism of
distinguished triangles with the middle morphism idy. Including this mor-
phism into the "double helix" as above, and looking at its consecutive full
(360°) turns, one can see that this double helix contains diagrams of the type
"lower cap". An equivalent (modulo axioms TR1-TR3) formulation of the
axiom TR4 is as follows: any lower cap can be completed to an octahedron.
    c) We will show in IV.1.4 below that any completions of a morphism
X ~ Y to a distinguished triangle in TR1 are isomorphic, i.e. if we take
f = idx, 9 = idy , then any morphism h whose existence is guaranteed by
TR3 is an isomorphism.
    Therefore, any upper cap can be recovered uniquely up to an isomorphism
from one of its commutative triangles, say X ---4 Y ---4 Z, by complementing
X ---4 Y and Y ---4 Z to distinguished triangles.

6. Cohomological Functors

A functor H : V ----4 .A from a triangulated category V into an abelian category
.A is called a cohomological functor if it is additive and the sequence

                               H(X)   !!J:i. H(Y) ~ H(Z)
in .A is exact for any distinguished triangle
                                 X~Y~Z~X[IJ

in V (compare III.6.14a).
244      IV. Triangulated Categories


      Axiom TR2 implies that if H is a cohomological functor, then the sequence

          H(X[i)) H(u[i]} I H(Y[i)) H(w[i]} I H(Z[i)) H(w[i]) I H(X[i + 1))

is exact for any distinguished triangle in V.
    One of the main examples of a cohomological functor is the O-cohomology
functor C e -+ HO(C e ) considered as a functor from K(A) or from D(A) to
A (the proof that K(A) and D(A) are triangulated will be given later, see
Theorem IV.1.9 and Corollary IV.2.7j the fact that HO is a cohomological
functor follows from the definition of distinguished triangles in K(A) and in
D(A) and from the long exact cohomology sequence.
    Another example of a cohomological functor is the functor
X 1--+ Homv(U, X) for any U E Ob V (see Proposition IV.1.3).

7. A Cone

By TRI c) and corollary IV.1.4b), any morphism u : X -+ Y in a triangulated
category determines an object C(u) (uniquely up to an isomorphism) as the
third object in a distinguished triangle X ~ Y --+ Z = Cluj --+ X[I]. This
object is called a cone of the morphism u (the indefinite article emphasizes
the non-uniqueness of the choice). A cone is given together with mappings
Y -+ Cluj -+ X[I].
    The explanation of this name is as follows. We will show below (The-
orem IV.1.9) that for any abelian category A the corresponding category
K(A) with distinguished triangles defined in 111.3.4 becomes a triangulated
category. In III.3.4 distinguished triangles were represented by diagrams

                                                                              (IV.I)

On the other hand, the diagram K e ~ L e ~ CU) ~ Ke[I] is isomorphic
to (IV.I) in K(A). The corresponding isomorphism is represented by the
following diagram (with the notation of Lemma III.3.3):

                        ~       Le     ~ CU)         ~     Ke[I]


                   II            in            II             II
                        L     Cylf     ~ CU)         ~     Ke[I]

The reader can easily verify that this diagram is commutative in K(A) (al-
though it is not commutative in Kom(A). The proofthat n is an isomorphism
in K(A) is given in lemma III.3.3.
   Now let us return to a general triangulated category 1). If we want to
make C functorial we could try to proceed as follows:
                                              1. Triangulated Categories   245


a) define C : Ob(Mor V) -+ Ob (V) using the axiom of choice (here Mor V
   is the category of morphisms in V, so that elements of Ob(Mor V) are
   morphisms in V);
b) define C : Mor(Mor V) -+ Mor V using TR3 and the axiom of choice.
    After that we would see no reason why we should have the equality
C(u 0 v) = C(u) 0 C(v). This "nonfunctoriality of a cone" is the first symp-
tom that something is going wrong in the axioms of a triangulated category.
Unfortunately, at the moment, we don't have a more satisfactory version.
    One can ask the following question. In various situations we get various
categories B together with some construction called the "abstract cone" C :
Mor B -+ B. Does there exist some set of useful general axioms for such a
construction? These axioms, if they were to exist, should determine at least
two structures:
a) functorial properties of C with respect to morphisms in Mor B;
b) the behavious of C under the composition of morphisms (with respect to
   morphisms in the category Mor (B)).

Exercises
1. Triangulated Categories and Abelian Categories. Let us recall
that an abelian category A is said to be semisimple if any exact triple in A
splits (see IlL2.3, IlL5.8).
    We sketch here the proof of the following result. Let a triangulated cate-
gory C be abelian. Then C is semisimple and any distinguished triangle in C
is isomorphic to a triangle of the form

                  X L     y ~ ker J[l] EEl Coker f ~ X[l]
(with natural morphisms 9 and h). Conversely, if A is a semisimple abelian
category and T : A -4 A is an arbitrary automorphism, then T, together
with the above triangles, defines the structure of a triangulated category on
A.
    The converse is proved directly. To prove the direct statement it suffices
to check the following. Let C be an arbitrary triangulated category and f :
X -4 Y be a monomorphism in C. Then F is an isomorphism of X with a
direct summand of y.
    Indeed, let Z[-l] ~ X L y L Z be a distinguished triangle con-
structed from f. Then f 0 a = 0, so that a = 0 (J is a monomorphism).
Hence, a[l] 0 id z = 0 and there exists 'Y : Z -4 Y with !3 0 'Y = id z . To prove
that f EEl 'Y : X EEl Z -4 Y is an isomorphism it suffices to prove that a cone
of f EEl 'Y is isomorphic to O. This follows, for example, from the octahedron
whose upper cap contains the commutative triangle

                            X                       • y

                                ~/    XEElZ
                                  2. Derived Categoris are Triangulated    251


2. Examples of Triangulated Categories. Let B be an additive (but not
necessarily abelian) category. Prove that, repeating definitions from 111.4.1,
we can introduce the category K(B) of homotopic classes of complexes over
B. Show that the category K(B) is triangulated (distinguished triangles and
translation functor are defined as in the case of an abelian category).
    In particular, let M be a family of objects of an abelian category A, MEIl
be a full subcategory of A consisting of finite direct sums of objects from
M. Then MEIl is an additive category and the corresponding triangulated
category K(MEIl) is denoted Tr M.
    Other examples of triangulated categories see in Beilinson - Bernstein -
Deligne [1], Sect. 1.1.

§2. Derived Categoris are Triangulated
1. Localizing Classes of Morphisms in Triangulated Categories

The derived category V*(A) is obtained from K*(A) by localization with
respect to some localizing clas~ of morphisms (see 111.2.6). We show that
under certain conditions the localized category can be made triangulated in
a natural way. Namely, the localizing class of morphisms should satisfy the
following conditions of compatibility with triangulation:
a) s E S if and only if T(s) E S.
b) If in the diagram TR3 (see IV.I.1) we have f,g E S, then there exists a
   complementing morphism hE S.
2. Theorem. Let V be a triangulated category and S a localizing class of
morphisms that is compatible with the triangulation. Define the translation
functor Ts in Vs in the natural way (Ts = Z on Ob Vs = Ob V). A
triangle in Vs is be called distinguished if it is isomorphic to the image of a
distinguished triangle in V under the localization V --> Vs.
    Then Vs, with this translation functor and this class of distinguished tri-
angles, is a triangulated category.
Proof. Recall first that a morphism f : X --> Y in Vs is represented by a
roof in V, i.e. by a diagram in V of the form
                                      Z




                          X
                              /~                  Y

with s E S. Two roofs are equivalent, i.e. represent the same morphism,
if they can be included in one "common roof" (see 111.2.8). Define Ts on

9. Cones and the Octahedron Axiom for Morphisms Represented
by Monomorphims of Complexes

Using Proposition IV.2.8 we can give the following interpretation of a cone
and of the octahedron axiom in D*(A).
                                   2. Derived Categoris are Triangulated    257


    If a morphism u : X ---> Y in D*(A) is represented by monomorphism
of complexes then the cone C(u) is the quotient Y/u(X) and Y ---> C(u) is
the factorization. Let us consider now the octahedron diagram (see IV.I.l),
assuming the morphisms X ---> Y and Y ---> Z (and so X ---> Z also) are
represented by monomorphisms of complexes. We claim that the octahedron
axiom asserts essentially the existence of the natural isomorphism

                            Z/Y ~ (Z/X)/(Y/X).
In fact, we have X' = Z/Y, Z' = Y/X. Next, the lower cap shows that Y' =
Z / X and that Z' ---> Y' is represented by the natural injection. Finally, the
third vertex of the left distinguished triangle in the lower cap is represented
by the quotient Y'/Z' = (Z/X)/(Y/X), and the same element in the upper
cap is represented by the quotient Z/Y.


10. Extensions

Proposition IV.2.8 justifies the following terminology. Let V be a triangulated
category. An object Y in V is said to be an extension of Z by X if there exists
a distinguished triangle X ---> Y ---> Z ---> X[I] in V. A full subcategory V'
in V is said to be stable under extensions if, for any distinguished triangle
X ---> Y ---> Z ---> X[I], the condition X, Z E Ob V' implies Y E Ob V'.

4. Thick Subcategories. There exists an alternative way to look at the
localization in triangulated categories, when we start not from morphisms
that become isomorphisms, as in Theorem IV.2.2, but from objects that
become isomorphic to the zero object. A complete triangulated subcategory
C of a triangulated category V is said to be thick (in French, epaisse) if it
satisfies the following condition:
    (T) Assume that a morphism f : X ---- Y in V can be factored through
an object from C (i.e. f can be represented as a composition X --+ V --+ Y
with V E Ob C) and enters a distinguished triangle X ~ Y --+ Z --+ X[I]
with Z E Ob C. Then X, Y E Ob C.
    A standard example of a thick subcategory is the category of all acyclic
objects in K(A). Indeed, the first condition in (T) means that He(J) is the
zero morphism, and the second condition means that He (J) is an isomor-
phism, which implies that both X and Yare acyclic.
    The relation between thick subcategories in d and localizing classes of
morphisms is as follows. A localizing class S in Mor(V) is said to be satu-
rated if s E S {:} {there exist morphisms f, f' in V such that f 0 s E
Sand so f' E S}.

4. The Category Mb(A)/:F

11. Axiom TRlc
We must prove that any morphism J.L in Mb(A)/F can be included into a
distinguished triangle. Working instead with the eseential image tJJ(Rigb) ,
we can find the required triangle using a cone of u, and then construct the
quasi-isomorphic object in Rigb as in IV.3.6.
    Another proof will be useful in IV.3.12. First, any morphism V -+ W
in Mb(A)/F is the composition of a monomorphism in Mb(A) and an iso-
morphism mod F. Indeed, we can embed V into a free module V ® A ®
An+I(E*)( -n - 1), add this free module to Wand then project onto W.
               3. An Example: The Triangulated Category of A-Modules       273


   So we may assume that u is an embedding of a submodule. Then the
corresponding morphism of rigid complexes is also an embedding and we can
take the third object in the distinguished triangle to be the corresponding
quotient complex, which is the image of the quotient module in Mb(A).

12. Axiom TR4

It is sufficient to show that for any upper cap consisting of rigid complexes
one can construct a lower cap also consisting of rigid complexes. By IV.3.11,
we can assume that X - Y and Y - Z in the upper cap are embeddings.
But then IV.2.9 shows that the object Y' in the lower cap is Z/X.

3. Exact Categories. a) Let A be an abelian category, B its full addi-
tive subcategory. Assume B to be closed under extensions. This means, by
definition, that in each exact triple 0 -+ X' -+ X -+ X" -+ 0 in A with
X', X" E ObB the object X is isomorphic to an object from B. The pair
(B, E), where [ is the class of triples in B that are exact in A, is called an
exact category. In particular, each abelian category A is exact ([ is the class
of all exact triples in A).
    One can give the definition of an exact category (B, [) that does not
involve a larger abelian category A (see, e.g., Quillen [4]). There exists a
canonical way to represent B as a full subcategory of an abelian category,
namely, of the category of additive functors F : B O -+ Ab such that the triple
o -+ X" -+ X -+ X' -+ 0 of abelian group is exact for any (X' -+ X -+
X") E [.

(for example,taking for [ the class of all split triples X -+ X x Y -+ Y).

4. Frobenius Categories. a) An exact category (13, £) is called a Frobenius
category if Lg = Pg and for any X E Ob £ there exist triples Y -+ I -+ X and
X -+ I' -+ Y' in £ with I, I' E L g • In other words, projective and injective
objects coincide and there are sufficiently many of them in 13.

a Frobenius category (Proposition IV.3.3).

gebra k[G] of a finite group G is a Frobenius category. More generally, the
category of finite-dimensional modules over any Frobenius k-algebra (for the
definition see Curtis, Reiner [1]) is a Frobenius category.

§4. Cores
1. What is the Problem?

An important discovery in the homological algebra in the last few years was
the fact that the derived categories of two absolutely different abelian cate-
gories can be equivalent as triangulated categories. In this section we describe
an axiomatic approach to a technique that allows us to see various abelian
subcategories inside a given triangulated category. This technique is called
the formalism of t-structures.
    The axioms of a t-structure formalize the following situation. Let A be
an abelian category and V = D*(A) be its derived category. Denote by
v?n (resp. v-s,n) the full subcategory of V formed by complexes K e with
Hi(Ke) = 0 for i < n (resp. for i > n).
    By Proposition III.5.2, the full subcategory V?O n V-s,o coincides with
A; more explicitely, the functor A ----> { the category of HO-complexes } =
V?O n V-s,o is the equivalence of categories.
   It turns out that to prove that the intersection V?O n V-s,o is abelian we
need only the following formal properties.
2. Definition. A t-structure on a triangulated category V is a pair of strictly
full subcategories (V-s,o, V?O) satisfying the conditions a)-c) below. Denote
v-s,n = V:5 0 [-n], v?n = V?O[-n].
a) V-s,o C V:5 1 and V?O C V?l.
b) Hom(X, Y) = 0 for X E Ob V-s,o, Y E Ob V?l.
c) For any X E Ob V there exists a distinguished triangle A -> X -> B ->
   A[l] with A E Ob V-s,o, BE Ob V?l.
      The core of the t-structure is the full subcategory A = V?O n V:5 0 .
3. Proposition. If V = D*(A) is the derived category of an abelian category
A, then the pair (V-s,o, V?O) described in IV.4.1 is a t-structure with the core
A.

12. t-Exact Functors

Let V, jj be two triangulated categories with a t-structure on each and F :
V ---t jj be an exact functor (so that F commutes with translations and maps
distinguished triangles into distinguished triangles). The functor F is said to
be left t-exact if F(V?O) c jj?o, right t-exact if F(V~O) c jj~O, and t-exact
if it is both left t- and right t-exact.
     This definition models, of course, the situation when V = D(A), jj =
D(.4) for two abelian categories A, .4, F is the derived functor (left or right)
of a functor cp : A ---t A (which is respectively left exact, right exact, or
exact).
     Let us remark that cp can be reconstructed from F by the formula

                 cp(X) = HO(F(X)),       X E Ob A c Ob V,
where HO is the cohomology functor in jj.

13. Remarks

The existence of a t-structure in a triangulated category V does not auto-
matically imply that V is the derived category D(A) of the core A of this
t-structure. Moreover, in the general case there is no obvious relation of V
with the category of complexes over A. This is caused by the non-uniqueness
of a cone C(J) of a morphism f. Namely, to construct a functor Kom(A) ---t V
we have to be able to associate an object of V to a complex in A of the form,
say, ... 0 ---> ALB ---> 0... . A natural candidate to the role of such
286     IV. Triangulated Categories


an object is the third vertex CU) of the triangle A ~ B ~ C ~ A[l]
(recall that A is a full subcategory of V). However, such an association is not
functorial because C is determined only up to a non-canonical isomorphism
(see the discussion in IV. 1. 7). This gives one more evidence that the situation
with derived categories is not quite satisfactory (see, however, Ex. IV.2 1-3).
