§1. Closed Model Categories

1. Definition. Let C be an arbitrary category, Land R be two classes of
morphisms in C. The class L is said to be right complementary to R (and R
is said to be left complementary to L) if the following condition is satisfied:
for any commutative square




                                                                          (V.l)

with l E L, r E R there exists a diagonal morphism x making both triangle
commutative.
   In the paper by Quillen [1], where this notion was first introduced, mor-
phisms from L (resp. from R) are said to have the left (resp. the right) lifting
property with respect to morphisms from R (resp. from L).

2. Properties of Complementary Classes

a) The class of all isomorphisms in C is both right and left complementary
to any other class.
    b) For any class L there exists a maximum class right complementary to
L; we denote it by peL). Similarly, for any class R there exists a maximum
class A(R) left complementary to R.
    Indeed, say, p( L) is the union of all classes right complementary to L.
    c) Operations A and p satisfy the following properties:

                             pAp = p,    APA = A                          (V.2)
Indeed, both p and A reverse inclusions: if, say, L' c L, then peL) ::J p(£').
Next, Ap(L) ::J Land pA(R) ::J R by definition. Applying p to the first
inclusion we get pAp(L) c peL). Substituting R = peL) into the second
292    V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra


inclusion we get the converse inclusion. The second equality in (V.2) is proved
in the same way.
    d) The classes p(L) and )"(R) are closed under the composition of mor-
phisms.
    Indeed, let, say, r',r" E p(L). To prove that r'r" E p(L) we construct
consecutively morphisms in the following diagram:

existence of ® shows that r'r" E p(L).
    e) In passing from the category C to the dual category Co complemen-
tary classes remain complementary classes with the words "right" and "left"
interchanged.
    f) If in the diagram (V.I) either l is an epimorphism or r is a monomor-
phism, then x, whenever it exists, is unique.
    g) The classes p(L) and )..(R) are closed under retraction. More precisely,
a morphism f : X -+ Y is said to be a retraction of a morphism g : X' -+ Y'
if f and g can be included into the following commutative diagram:

4. Closed Model Categories

A category C is said to be a closed model category if three classes of mor-
phisms in C are specified: a class F of fibrations, a class G of cofibrations,
and a class W of weak equivalences. These data must satisfy the following
axioms.
CMO. C is closed under finite inductive and projective limits.
CMl. Any two of the classes F, G, W determine the third class as follows:
     a) W = p(F) . )"(G) where for any two classes of morphisms Sand T
        we denote S . T = {st I S E S, t E T}.
     b) G=)..(FnW).
     c) F=p(GnW).
CM2. Mor C = F· (G n W) = (F n W)· G.
294      V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra


CM3. W contains all isomorphisms. If any two of the three morphisms J, g,
     Jg belong to W, then the third one also belongs to W.
   If C is a closed model category then Co has the dual structure of a closed
model category with FO as cofibrations, GO as fibrations and WO as weak
equivalences.

5. Remarks

Axioms CMO-CM3 were suggested by D. Quillen [1]. If C satisfies these ax-
ioms, then localization by W (see III.2.2) enables us to introduce the corre-
sponding homotopic category HoC and the universal functor C ---+ HoC which
makes all weak equivalences isomorphisms.
    Let Top be the category of topological spaces and continuous mappings.
Define F as the class of all Serre fibrations and W as the class of all weak
homotopic equivalences. Define G by the construction from the axiom CMl. b.
Then Top becomes a closed model category and HoTop is the natural category
for hotomopy theory (see Exercises to Sect. V.2).
    Let us assume now that a topological space is the geometric realization
IXI of a simplicial set X. How one can find out homotopic properties of IXI
by looking at X? This problem was solved by Kan. Following Quillen, Kan's
result can be formulated as a theorem saying that Ll ° Set can be endowed
with the structure of a closed model category in such a way that HoLlo Set
and HoTop become (modulo some minor details) equivalent. The description
of this structure on LlO Set is the main theme of the first two sections of this
chapter.
    The situation when quite dissimilar model categories C give equivalent
(or close to being equivalent) homotopic categories HoC is not at all unusual,
and the choice of a convenient model category for treating a given homotopic
problem may lead to a substantial simplification. The category of differential
graded algebras (see IV.3) is an example of a closed model category with
remarkably simple algebraic properties.

§2. Homotopic Characterization of Weak Equivalences

1. Definition. A simplicial set E is called a Kan set if PE E F, where
PE : E -+ ,,1[0] is the projection to the point.

    Thus, any morphism of a horn V (n, k) to a Kan set can be extended to a
morphism of the simplex L1[n] filling V(n, k). This property is an imitation
of "homotopy extension" property. In the next subsection we shall give the
precise definitions, and now let us try to seize the generality of the notion.
    a) In the situation of V.l.ll, let us consider the decomposition X -+
ExOO(px) -+ ,,1[0] of the morphism Px : X -+ ,,1[0]. Evidently, ExOO(px) is a
Kan set. Hence any simplicial set can be embedded into a Kan set in such a
way that the embedding is a weak equivalence (Le. belongs to W).

similar to the role played by function spaces in the homotopic topology.

2. Homotopies of Simplicial Sets

a) Let X be a simplicial set. Vertices x, y E Xo are said to be strongly
connected if d~ z = x, dt z = y for some z E Xl (again we denote dl =
X (at)). Vertices x, y E Xo are said to be connected if there exists a chain
x = Xo, Xl, X2, ... , Xp = Y such that either (Xi, XH1) or (XHb Xi) are strongly
connected for all 0 ::; i ::; p - 1. Let us remark that if E is a Kan set,
then any two connected vertices are strongly connected. To prove this it
suffices to verify that the relation of being strongly connected is symmetric
and transitive. Both properties are proved using liftings of appropriate horns
V(2, k).
   For any X denote by 7ro(X) the set of the equivalence classes of Xo with
respect to the connectedness relation. Evidently, this is the same as 7ro(IXI).
   b) For two simplicial sets X, Y denote
                             [X, Y] = 7ro(Hom(X, Y))
where the inner Hom in £1OSet is defined in II.4.24.
    c) Let f, 9 : X ~ Y be two morphisms in £10 Set. Being elements from
Hom(X, Y)o they define classes [f], [g] E [X, Y]. Morphisms f, 9 are called
homotopic if [f) = [g). The mapping p : X ~ Y is called a homotopic
equivalence if there exists q : Y ~ X such that pq is homotopic to idy and
qp is homotopic to idx.
    One can give a direct definition of homotopy between two morphisms,
similarly to a). Namely, f, 9 : X ~ Yare said to be strongly homotopic if
there exists a morphism k : X x £1[1] ~ Y (homotopy between f and g) such
that k O = f, P = g, where
           k i = k 0 (id x x C (aD) : X = X x £1[O]---t X x £1[l)---t Y.
The equivalence relation generated by the strong homotopy is the homotopy.
If E is a Kan set, then homotopic morphisms f : X ~ E are strongly
homotopic.
    The main goal of the present section is to prove the following theorem.
3. Theorem. A morphism f : X ~ Y in £10 Set is a weak equivalence if and
only if for any K an set E the induced map
                             [f, E) : [Y, E] ---t [X, E)
is one-to-one.
   Axiom CM3 for £1OSet obviously follows from this theorem.
302    V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra

17. Proposition. G n '\(FK) = G n '\(F).

Plan of the Proof

Clearly, '\(F) c .\(FK)' so that we have to prove that Gn.\(FK) c .\(F). Fix

dashed arrows in various squares of the form

because f E '\(FK) and the arrow @ exists by universality.
    Unfortunately, we can not guarantee that any fibration is induced by a
fibration from FK. We will introduce a special class of fibrations - the so called
minimal fibrations - and prove that any fibration is in a sense equivalent to
a minimal one. On the other hand, for any Kan set Z we will construct a
universal fibration p : E -- B with the fiber Z, prove that B (and, therefore,
also E) is a Kan set, and prove that any minimal fibration with the fiber Z
is induced by p.

22. Definition. A fibration 9 : X ---+ Y is said to be minimal if any two
g-equivalent simplices coincide.
   Roughly speaking, the minimality of 9 means that there are no redun-
dant relative homotopies in X: any two homotopic morphisms are equal. In
particular, the reader can easily verify that if 9 : X ---+ Y is minimal, then
any connected component of any fiber of 9 contains exactly one O-simplex.
   The proof of following lemma is easy.

23. Lemma. Suppose that 9 : X ---+ Y is a minimal fibration and r.p : Y' ---+ Y
is an arbitrary morphism. Then the induced fibration g' : X x Y' = X' ---+ Y'
                                                                x
is also minimal.

Proof. a) Evidently, g-connectivity is an equivalence relation. Mark one rep-

V.2.22, a degenerate simplex in a given class is unique). Then all degenerate
simplices will be marked. Denote by X' the simplicial set which consists of

    b) First, if x E Xn is a marked simplex and d~x E X' for all i, 0 ~ i ~ n,

38. Remark. The mapping <p induced the given fibration 9 from the univer-
salone p, is essentially unique. Namely, there exists the following classification
theorem. Let Z be a simplicial set with an effective action of a simplicial group
G. Let p : E ~ B be the universal fibration constructed from G as in V.2.34.
For any morphism <p : Y ~ B let P<p : X x B E ~ Y be the induced fibration.
Then <p ~ P<p is an one-to-one correspondence between homotopy classes of
morphisms <p : Y ~ B and isomorphism classes of locally trivial G-fibrations
9 : X ~ Y with the fiber Z. This theorem, which belongs, essentially, to
Eilenberg and Maclane, is proved, for example, in May [1].

Exercises
1. Simplicial Group of Automorphisms of a Simplicial Set. a) Prove
that the product Y *Z of two simplicial sets (see 11.4.23) satisfies the following
property:
           HomLlOSet(X, Y * Z) = HomLlO Set (X, Y) x HomLlOSet(X, Z)
for any X E Ob ,1DSet.
    b) Let X, Y, Z, U be four simplicial sets, f = (In) E HomLlOSet(X * Y, Z),
9 = (gn) E HomLlOSet(X * Z, U). Prove that the family of maps hn : Xn x
Yn ~ Zn, n = 0,1,2, ... , where hn(x, y) = gn (x, fn(x, y)), x E X n , defines a
morphism h = f * 9 E HomLlOSet(X * Y, U) so that we get a map
      HomLlOSet(X * Y, Z) x HomLlOSet(X * Z, U) --+ HomLlOSet(X * Y, U).
    c) Using a) and b) together with the first part of Theorem 11.4.24 construct
a simplicial map
                  Hom(Y, Z) * Hom(Z, U) --+ Hom(Y, U).
Prove that for this morphism defined on Hom(Y, Y) the structure of a sim-
plicial semigroup E(Y). Prove that invertible elements ofthis semigroup (Le.
invertible elements of all semigroups En(Y)) form the simplicial group A(Z)
from n. V.2.34.
                     2. Homotopic Characterization of Weak Equivalences      331


2. Complexes as a Closed Model Category. Let A be an abelian cat-
egory with sufficiently many injective objects, C = Kom - (A) the category
of left bounded complexes over A. By definition, the class F of fibrations in
C consists of all epimorphisms in C, the class G of cofibrations consists of
monomorphisms in C whose cokernels are complexes of projective objects in
A, and the class W of weak equivalencies consists of quasi-isomorphisms in
C. Prove that the axioms CMO-CM3 are satisfied.
3. Hausdorff Topological Spaces as a Closed Model Category. The
structure of a closed model category is determined by the following data.
    A fibration is a continuous map f : X ---+ Y satisfying the Serre condition:
f E p{faces of a cube}, where a face of a cube is a map [0, It- l ---+ [0, It of
the form (Xl, ... , Xr-l) t---> (Xl, ... , Xr-l, 0).
    A weak equivalence is a map f : X ---+ Y incuding isomorphisms of ho-
motopic groups 7rq(X, x) ---+ 7rq(Y, f(x)) for all q ~ 0, X E X (Le. a weak
homotopic equivalence in the topological sense).
    Finally, cofibrations are defined by the formula G = )"(F n W).
    The geometric realization functor maps a weak equivalence f of simplicial
sets into a weak equivalence If I of topological spaces. Conversely, if If I is a
weak equivalence, f also is.
4. Homological Classification of Weak Equivalencies of Simplicial
Sets. Prove that a morphism f : X ---+ Y of simplicial sets is a weak equiv-
alence if and only if it induces isomorphisms

                7rO(X) ---=::... 7ro(Y),   7r1(X,X) ---=::... 7r1(Y,f(x)),
                     Hq(y, A) ---=::... Hq(X, rCA)),         q ~ 0,
where A is any homological coefficient system of abelian groups.
    The sketch of the proof a) Let f be a weak equivalence. Then If I also is a
weak equivalence. It suffices to consider the case when both IXI and WI are
connected. If IXI and WI are also simply connected, then any homological
coefficient system is trivial and f induces an isomorphism on cohomology. Fi-
nally, the general case reduces to the above using the Leray spectral sequences
of the universal coverings X ---+ X and Y ---+ y.
    b) Let f satisfies the above conditions. To prove that f and If I are
weak equivalences it suffices, by the Whitehead theorem, to prove that f
induces isomorphisms 7rq(IXI,x) ---+ 7rq(IYI,f(x)) for all q ~ 2. If IXI and WI
are simply connected, this directly follows from isomorphisms Hq(y, A) ---=::...
Hq(X, rCA)) and in the general case one must use the Leray spectral se-
quences as above.
5. Simplicial Groups as a Closed Model Category. A morphism of
simplicial groups is said to be a fibration if it is a fibration as a morphism of
simplicial sets.
   To define a weak equivalence we need the notion of Moore homotopic
groups. Let G = {G n } be a simplicial group. Denote
332     V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra


                                  No(G) = G,
                     Nq(G) = ni>okerG(8!) for q ~ 1;
           8q : Nq(G) ----+ Nq- 1 (G)   the map induced by G(8~).
One can verify that Im8q+1 is a normal subgroup of ker8q • Denote

                           "lrq(G) = ker8q jIm8q +1.
Then "lro(G) = "lro(IGI) and "lrq(G) is an abelian group for q ~ 1.
   A morphism of simplicial groups is said to be a weak equivalence if it
induces an isomorphism of groups "Irq for all q.
   Finally, cofibrations are again defined by the formula G = )"(F n W).
6. Fibrant and Cofibrant Objects. An object X of a closed model cat-
egory C is said to be a fib rant if X -+ w (where w is a final object of C) is a
fibration. Similarly, an object X is said to be cofibrant if a -+ X (where a is
an initial object of C) is a cofibration.
    In ..1°Set any object if cofibrant and Kan sets are fibrant. In Kom-(A) any
object is fibrant and complexes formed by projective objects are cofibrant.
In Top any object is fibrant and OW-complexes are cofibrant.
7. Homotopic Categories. a) Let C be a closed model category. For
X, Y E Ob C denote by X II Y their coproduct in C. For two morphisms
f : X -+ Z, 9 : Y -+ Z denote by f + 9 : X II Y -+ Z the morphism
constructed by the universality property of X II Y. Similarly one can define
(h, k) : Z -+ X x Y for a pair of morphisms h ; Z -> X, k ; Z -+ Y.
    b) Cylinder of an object. Let X E Ob C. By a cylinder of an object we
mean a family (Cyl X,<5o, <51 ,a) consisting of an object Cyl X E Ob C and
three morphisms <50 , <51 : X -+ Cyl X, a : Cyl X -+ X such that a<5o = a<51 =
idx, a is a weak equivalence, <50 + <51 ; X II X -+ Cyl X is a cofibration
(whence <50 , <51 are also cofibrations).
    c) Path space of an object X is defined dually as a collection (Xl, do, db"T)
where Xl E Ob C, do,dl ; Xl -+ X, "T : X -+ Xl with the properties
dO"T = d 1"T = idx, "T is a weak equivalence, (d o,d1 ) : Xl -+ X x X is a
fibration (whence do, d1 are also fibrations).
    Both a cylinder and a path space need not exist, and if they do exist, they
may be nonunique. In particular, X 1--+ CylX is not necessarily a functor from
C into itself.
    In ..1°Set functorial cylinders and path spaces always exist: Cyl X =
X * ..1[1], Xl is constructed from Hom(..1[I], X) as in V.2.11. In Top they
also exist but are not necessarily given by the standard construction. For
example, the product of a space X by the unit interval is a cylinder in the
sense of b) if and only if X is a cofibrant space (i.e. a OW-complex). In
Kom - (A) a cylinder of a complex X is the cylinder of the identity morphism
idx in the sense III.3.2. Verify all these assertions.
                             3. DC-Algebras as a Closed Model Category         333


    d) Homotopies. Let j, 9 : X ---> Y be two morphisms in C. A left homotopy
between j and 9 is a morphism h : Cyl X ---> Y (where (Cyl X, 80, 81. 0") is a
cylinder of X) such that M o = j, Ml = g. A right homotopy between j and
9 is a morphism k : X ---> Y I (where (Y I, do, d 1 , T) is a path space of Y) such
that dok = j, d1k = g. The corresponding morphisms j and 9 are said to be
homotopic from the left (resp. from the right).
    e) Left and right homotopies. Let f, 9 : X ---> Y be two morphisms in C
with X cofibrant and Y fibrant. Using axioms of a closed model category
Quillen [1] proved that if f,g are homotopic from the right (resp. from the
left), then a right homotopy exists for any cylinder of X (resp. for any path
space of Y) and that the notions of a right and of a left homotopy between
f and 9 are equivalent.
    f) The category ho C. Objects ofho C are objects of C that are both fibrant
and cofibrant. Morphism in ho C are classes of homotopic (does not matter,
from the left or from the right) morphisms in C. Verify that the composition
in ho C is well defined.
    g) The category Ho C and the equivalence. The category Ho C is defined
as the localization of C by the class W of weak equivalences, Ho C = C[W- 1 ].
One of the main theorems in Quillen [1] says that the localization functor
Q : C ---> Ho C induces an equivalence of categories ho C ---> Ho C. Specialize
this theorem to C = Kom-(A) and to C = Top.


§3. DG-Algebras as a Closed Model Category

1. DG-Algebras

By a DC-algebm (short for Differential Craded Algebra) over a field k of
characteristic 0 we mean the following data:
   a) A complex of linear spaces over k: A = (Ai, d), Ai = 0 for i < 0,
d: Ai ---> Ai+l.
   b) A multiplication Ai®Aj ---> Ai+j, a ® b f--+ ab, which makes A an
                              k
associative algebra with unit 1 E AO; this algebra also will be denoted by A.
Ths multiplication must be graded- (or super-) commutative:
                                          a E Ai,   bE Aj

and d must be a derivation of degree 1:

                    d(ab) = da· b + (-l)i a . db,

    A morphism of DC-algebras is a morphism of complexes which is also a
morphism of algebras with unit.
    Let V9A denote the category of DC-algebras (the field k is assumed to
be fixed).
334    V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra


2. Examples

a) The de Rham Algebm. Let X be a Coo-manifold, ,n-(X) the algebra of
smooth differential forms on X (over lR or C). It is a DG-algebra and ,n-
determines, evidently, the functor from the category of Coo-manifolds to the
category vg A.
    b) The Polynomial de Rham Algebm. For X = lR n let us consider the
subalgebra in ,n- (lRn) consisting of forms with polynomial coefficients. The
restriction of d on this subalgebra defines on it the structure of a DG-algebra.
We use this algebra to define the de Rham functor on the category of simpli-
cial sets. To do this we have to modify slightly the definitions.
    Denote
      V(n) = EElV(n)q     the DG-algebra of polynomial differential
                          forms in variables xo(n), ... ,xn(n) subject to
                          the relation xo(n) + ... + xn(n) = 1.
For any nondecreasing map f : [m]- [n] define

                        V(f) (xi(n» =      L xj(m),
                                         j:f(j)=O
and extend V(f) to maps V(f)q : V(n)q - V(n)q
                           do V(f)q-l = V(f)q 0 d.

We get, for any q ~ 0, a simplicial set (even a simplicial linear space)
vq = (V(n)q). All these simplicial sets together give the structure of a
simplicial DG-algebra (Le. of a simplicial object in the category V9A) on
V = (V(n), n ~ 0). For any simplicial set X define
                          ,nq(X) = Hom,d0Set(X, vq).
In other words, one element of ,nq(X) is a family of polynomial q-forms, one
on each simplex of X, which are compatible with face and degeneration op-
erators. One can easily see that ,n-(X) = EEl ,nq(X) has a natural structure
                                          q2:O
of a DG-algebra over k. In particular, ,n-(Ll(n» = V(n).
    The map X 1--+ ,n-(X) determines, evidently, a contravariant functor
.10 Set - V9A.
    c) Let A be an arbitrary DG-algebra. Its cohomology H-(A) inherits the
multiplication from the multiplication in A: (class of a) x (class of b) = (class
of ab). Define d = 0 on H-(A). Then H-(A) becomes a DG-algebra and H-
yields a covariant functor vg A - vg A.
3. Definition. a) A morphism f : A - B of DG-algebms is said to be a
weak equivalence if H-(f) : H-(A) - H-(B) is an isomorphism (i.e. f is a
quasi-isomorphism) .
   b) A morphism of DG-algebms is said to be a fibration if it is a surjection.
                            3. DC-Algebras as a Closed Model Category               335


    c) A morphism of DC-algebras is said to be a cofibration if it belongs
to >..(F n W), where F is the class of fibrations and W is the class of weak
equivalences (see V.l.l).
4. Theorem. The category V9A with the above classes of weak equivalences,
fibrations, and cofibrations is a closed model category.

    This theorem will be proved below in nn. V.3.5-V.3.11. It shows that the
Sullivan - de Rham functor n e : .1° Set ---- V9A is a contravariant functor
between two closed model categories. However the functor does not map one
structure into the dual to another. In particular, the image of a fibration in
.1OSet might fail to be a cofibration in VAg.
    Nevertheless, the functor n e induces an equivalence of appropriate local-
ized categories.

7. Lemma. a) For any DC-algebra A and for any V, V' the morphism
i : A --+ A <>9 S(V) <>9 T(V'), a H a <>9 1 <>9 1, is a cofibration.
             k         k

§4. Minimal Algebras
1. Notation

Let (A = EI1 Ai, d) be a DC-algebra over a field k of characteristic O. Denote
              i2:0

        i>l                                                                 i=O
and dAn. Evidently, dA(n) C A(n) and A = U~=oA(n). Let also A(-l) = k.

A(n, q)
                A(n -1) = A(n,O) C A(n, 1) C ... C A(n)

                                                        4. Minimal Algebras       343


2. Definition. An algebra A is said to be minimal if AO = k, A is free
(as commutative graded Z-algebra) and the differential d in A is such that
A(n) = Uq~oA(n,q) for all n ~ O.
    This definition is useful for the following three reasons:
a) Minimal algebras admit a rather explicit inductive description (see VA.6-
   V.4.1O) and are well adapted to computation of homotopic groups (see
   V.S.12, V.S.13, V.S.14).
b) Any simply connected DC-algebra is weakly equivalent to a minimal alge-
   bra (V.4.ll) and this correspondence has rather nice functorial properties
   (V.S.S).
c) Cofibrant DC-algebras can be completely described in terms of minimal
   ones (V.SA).
   The first simple result about minimal algebras is given by the following
lemma.
3. Lemma. If A is a minimal algebra, then its differential d is decomposable;
by definition, this means that dA + C A + . A + .
Proof Denote by a( n, q) the union of the following two statements:

                              A(n, q)i C [a(n, q)+]2 for all   i > n,
                          {
             a(n, q) =        dA(n, q) C [A(n, q)+]2.

     a) a(O,O) is true because A(O,O) = k.
    b) a(n,q -1) implies a(n,q). Indeed, any element of A(n,q) is a sum of
elements of the following three types: elements from A( n, q - 1), elements
x E An such that dx E A(n, q - 1), and products of elements from An that
belong to [A(n, q)+j2. For elements ofthe first type both includions in a(n, q)
follow from a(n, q - 1). For elements of the third type they are evident. For
elements of the second type only the second inclusion should be verified, and
it follows from the first inclusion in a( n, q - 1).
    c) If a(n, q) is true for all q, then a(n + 1, q) is true. This follows from
the minimality of A.
    By the minimality, any element of A belongs to one of the subalgebras
A(n, q) and the second inclusion in a(n, q) shows that its boundary is decom-
posable.                                                                       0

for A.                                                                         0

    Let us remark that the above construction is not functorial due to arbi-
trary choices of spaces C, K, K used for kernel and cokernel killing. But,
similarly to the case of a derived category, the minimal model becomes func-
torial after localizing by homotopic equivalences. In the category VQA the
notion of homotopic equivalence should be slightly modified because the mul-
tiplication should be taken into account.
    In the next section we shall formulate the main facts about the homotopic
theory of DC-algebras and about its relation to the homotopic theory of
simplicial sets.

4. Minimal Models of de Rham Algebras. Examples. The following
method enables us in some cases to compute a minimal model M of the de
Rham algebra rr(X) of a simplicial set X using only the cohomology algebra
A = He(x, k) (we work in the category V9A, a field assumed to be of zero
characteristic) .
    Let us assume that A can be represented in the form A = S (V) / I, where
V is a graded vector space and I is an ideal in S(V) generated by the sequence
ft, ... , In E S(V) of elements in S(V) such that:
a) all Ii are homogeneous and decomposable in S(V);
b) Ii is not a zero divisor in S(v)/(ft,···, Ii-I)'
    Let M = S(V EB kg 1 EB ... EB kgn ), where deggi = deg/i - 1; define the
differential in M by the formula dv = 0 for v E V, dg i = Ii (essentially, M is
the Koszul resolution of the S(V)-module A, see Ex. 1.7.5).
    One can verify that M is a minimal algebra and the natural map € :
M ~ ne(x) is a weak equivalence, so that M is a minimal model of ne(x)
in vgA.
    For example, let X be the two-dimensional sphere 8 2 (more precisely, a
simplicial set whose geometric realization is homeomorphic to the sphere).
Then M is freely generated by x E M2, Y E M3 with dx = 0, dy = x 2. For
X = CP2 the algebra M is freely generated by x E M2, Y E M 5 with dx = 0,
dy = x 3 .


§5. Equivalence of Homotopic Categories
Ths section contains no proofs. We present here the main results about the
equivalence of homotopic categories following the paper by Bousfield - Gu-
genheim [1].
1. Definition. a) An elementary homotopy between two morphisms
fo, ft : B ~ A of DG-algebras is a morphism F : B ~ A ® T(V) satis-
                                                                 k
fying the following property. Let morphisms Oi : A ® T(V) ~ A, i = 0, 1, be
                                                     k
defined by the formulas
                  oo(a ® p(v» = p(O)a,    oo(a ® p(v)dv) = 0,
                  ol(a ® p(v» = p(l)a,    ol(a ® p(v)dv) = O.
Then we require that 00 0 F = fo, 01 0 F = ft.
                                  5. Equivalence of Homotopic Categories    353


   b) Two morphisms of DG-algebras are said to be homotopic if they are
connected by a chain of elementary homotopies.
2. Lemma. Homotopy is an equivalence relation between morphisms of DG-
algebras, which is compatible with the composition of morphisms. Homotopic
morphisms of DG-algebras induce homotopic morphisms of underlying com-
plexes, and, therefore, equal maps in cohomology.
    For two DG-algebras A, B denote by [B, A] the set of equivalence classes
of morphisms B ~ A. For generic A, B the elementary homotopy is a non-
transitive relation. But for cofibrant algebras the following is true.
3. Proposition. a) If B is a cofibrant algebra (i.e. B ~ k is a cofibraiton),
then for any DG-algebra A the elementary homotopy is a transitive relation
on [B,A].
    b) If B is a cofibrant algebra, then for any weak equivalence f : A ~ A'
the induced mapping f* : [B, A] ~ [B, A'] is one-to-one.
      Connected cofibrant algebras admit the following description.
4. Proposition. The class of connected cofibrant algebras coincides with the
class of algebras of the form M ® T(V), where M is a minimal algebra and
T(V) is the tensor algebra of a graded linear space V.
5. Theorem (Uniqueness Theorem). Let f: M ~ A, g: N ~ A be two
weak equivalences, where M and N are minimal, HO(A) = k. Then there
exists an isomorphism h : M ~ N such that morphisms f and g . hare
(elementary) homotopic.

6. Homotopy Groups of DG-Algebras

To define homotopy groups we must work in the category VQAo whose ob-
jects are augmented DG-algebras (Le. morphisms c: : A ~ k) and morphisms
are the morphisms in VQA commuting with augmentations. The classes W,
F, G in VQ Ao are defines as intersections of corresponding classes in VQ A
with morphisms in VQA o. Looking through proofs in Sect. V.3 one can see
that VQAo is a closed model category.
    For A E Ob VQAo denote A+ = kerc:.
7. Definition. Homotopy groups of A are defines as 7l"n(A)
H n (A+ / A+ . A+) where A+ / A+ . A+ is considered as the complex with the
grading induced by Ai and with the differential induced by d: A ~ A.
     Let us remark that if AO = k and d is decomposable (for example,
if A is minimal), then 7l"n(A) = At. mod (A+ . A+). According to V.3.6-
V.3.1O, in this case there exists a canonical structure of Lie superalgebra on

the Whitehead multiplication. To give the description of 7l'n(A) that looks

(with homotopy classes of morphisms in VQAo at the left).
9. Proposition. a) Homotopic morphisms of augmented DG-algebras A ---*
B induce equal mappings 7l'e(A) ---* 7l'e(B).
   b) A weak equivalence of cofibrant algebras A ---* B induces an isomor-
phism 7l'e(A) ~ 7l'e(B).
   This proposition, together with the theorem about the existence of a
minimal model, implies that it suffices to compute homotopic groups for
minimal algebras only.
   Let us formulate the main results about the relation between homotopic
theories of simplicial sets and of DG-algebras.

10. Two Homotopic Categories

Denote by hS the following category:
                            Ob hS = Kan sets in    .10 Set,
                     HomhS(X, Y) = [X, Y]         (see n. V.2.2).
Similarly define the category hA as follows:

                       Ob hA = cofibrant algebras in VQA.
                     HomhA(A,B) = [A,B]           (see n. V.5.2)
(the composition of homotopy classes of morphisms is defined, by the com-
position of the corresponding representatives).
   There are the following functors between these two categoriea:
    a) The functor S : hA ---* hSo. For A E Ob VQA let

                            S(A)n = Hom1)QA(A, 'V(n)) ,
where 'V(n) is the algebra of polynomial differential forms of the n-simplex
(see V.3.2). On S(A) = (S(A)n) there exists a natural structure of a simplicial
                                  5. Equivalence of Homotopic Categories      355


set which is functorial in A under morphisms in VQA. For a cofibrant algebra
A the set SeA) is a Kan set. If morphisms f, 9 of two cofibrant algebras are
homotopic, then they are homotopic in L10 Set. Hence S determines a functor
hA -+ hSo, which will be denoted by the same letter.
    b) The functor M : hSO -+ hA. This functor associates to a Kan set the
class of its de Rham algebra. Unfortunately, the de Rham algebra of a Kan
set is not, in general, a cofibrant algebra. Hence to define M one must use
the choice axiom. Let us choose, for each Kan set X, a weak equivalence
X -+ M(X), where M(X) is a cofibrant algebra (which is minimal for a
connected X). After that one can prove that M can be extended to homotopy
classes of morphisms and is unique up to a homotopy. This guarantees the
existence of the required functor.

11. Nilpotent Rational Simplicial Sets

a) Let G be a group. Let us recall that its lower central series G = Zl (G) :l
Z2 (G) :l ... is defined inductively as follows:
     Zi+l(G) is generated by commutators xyx-1y-l, x E Zi(G), y E G. The
group G is called nilpotent if Zi(G) = {e} for i ~ io.
    b) Let N be a G-module. Define Zl(N) = N and let Zi+l(G) be generated
by gn-n for 9 E G, n E Zi(N). A module N is called nilpotent if Zi(N) = {O}
for i ~ i o.
    c) A group G is called a uniquely divisible group if for any 9 E G, k ~ 1,
the equation xk = 9 in G has exactly one solution.
    d) A connected simplicial set or topological space X is called nilpotent if
its fundamental group 7rl (X) is nilpotent (for one, thus for all, base points),
and all 7rl(X)-modules 7rn (X) are also nilpotent.
    e) A nilpotent simplicial set or topological space X is called rational if all
groups 7rn (X), n ~ 1, are uniquely divisible groups.
    f) A nilpotent simplicial set or topological space X is said to have a
finite Q-type if all homology spaces Hn(X, Q), n ~ 1, are finite dimensional.
The equivalent requirement is that H1(X,Q) and all7rn(X) 0Q, n ~ 2, are
finite-dimensional.
    g) Let A be a cofibrant DG-algebra over Q with HO(A) = Q and let
M -+ A be a weak equivalence with a minimal algebra M. The algebra A is
said to have a finite Q-type if Mi are finite dimensional for i ~ O.
    Denote by hfS (resp. by hfA) the full subcategories of hS (resp. of hS)
formed by rational nilpotent Kan sets of finite Q-type (resp. by cofibrant
DG-algebras of finite Q-type).
    The categories hfSo (resp. hfAo) are defined similarly using sets with a
base point (resp. augmented algebras).

12. Theorem. The restrictions of functors Sand M to hfS and hfA re-
spectively induce adjoint equivalences of these categories. The same is true
for hfSo and hfAo.
356    V. Introduction to Homotopic Algebra


13. Homotopy Groups

Theorem V.5.12 gives a rather simple procedure to compute homotopy groups
of simplicial sets (up to torsion). Let X be a simplicial set whose geometric
realization IXI is a connected rational nilpotent space of finite Q-type. Then
the following results are true.
    a) A simplicial set X is weakly equivalent to a Kan set Sing lXI, which is
connected, nilpotent and has finite Q-type.
    b) Let 7rj(M(X)) be the homotopy groups of minimal model M(X) of
the de Rham algebra of Sing IXI over Q.
14. Theorem. For j 22 there exist functorial isomorphisms 7rj(M(X)) =
Hom (7rj(X),Q). If7r1(X) is abelian, then the same is true for j = 1.
    (For 71'1 (X) = {e} we have to work with spaces having a base point and
with augmented algebras).
    The proof of this result and of various generalizations is based on the fol-
lowing localization technique which, enables one to give a geometric meaning
to the replacement of 7rj(X) by 7rj(X) ® Q (or by Hom(7rj(X), Q)).
15. Theorem. Let X be a canonical nilpotent simplicial set of finite Q-type.
Then the natural morphism X -+ S 0 M(X) is universal in the class of
morphisms X -+ Y in hfSo such that Y is a rational simplicial set of finite
Q-type.

      and the Environment




We    at Springer-Verlag firmly believe that an
international science publisher has a special
obligation to the environment, and our corpo-
rate policies consistently reflect this conviction.


                   We      also expect our busi-
ness partners - paper mills, printers, packag-
ing manufacturers, etc. - to commit themselves
to using environmentally friendly materials and
production processes.


         The paper in this book is made from
low- or no-chlorine pulp and is acid free, in
conformance with international standards for
paper permanency.
